Universality of global asymptotics of Jack-deformed random Young diagrams at varying temperatures Thanks: This research was funded in whole or in part by Narodowe Centrum Nauki , grant 2021/42/E/ST1/00162. For the purpose of Open Access, the author has applied a CC-BY public copyright licence to any Author Accepted Manuscript (AAM) version arising from this submission.
Cesar Cuenca, Maciej Dołęga, Alexander Moll

Abstract
. This paper establishes universal formulas describing the global asymptotics of two distinct discrete versions of β \beta -ensembles in the high, low and fixed temperature regimes. Our results affirmatively answer a question posed by the second author and Śniady. We first introduce a special class of Jack measures on Young diagrams of arbitrary size, called the “Jack–Thoma measures”, and prove the LLN and CLT in the three aforementioned limit regimes. In each case, we provide explicit formulas for polynomial observables of the limit shape and Gaussian fluctuations around the limit shape. These formulas have surprising positivity properties and are expressed as sums of weighted lattice paths. Second, we show that the previous formulas are universal: they also describe the limit shape and Gaussian fluctuations for the model of random Young diagrams of a fixed size derived from Jack characters with the approximate factorization property. Finally, in stark contrast with continuous β \beta -ensembles, we show that the limit shapes at high and low temperatures of our random Young diagrams are one-sided infinite staircase shapes. For the Jack–Plancherel measure, we describe this shape explicitly by relating its local minima with the zeroes of Bessel functions.



1.
Introduction and main results

1.1.
Jack-deformed random Young diagrams as discrete
\beta
-ensembles

The
Gaussian Unitary Ensemble
(GUE) is one of the most well-studied models of
random matrices. The joint density of eigenvalues
x_{1}>\dots>x_{N}
of the GUE is given by a formula of the form

\frac{1}{Z_{\beta,N,V}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{\beta}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\exp(-V(x_{i})),

where
\beta=2,\,V(x)=\frac{x^{2}}{2}
, and
Z_{\beta,N,V}
is the normalization constant. When we choose an arbitrary
\beta>0
and
V(x)=\frac{x^{2}}{2}
, we obtain the joint density of eigenvalues of the model
of random matrices known as the

Gaussian
\beta
-Ensemble
(G
\beta
E). For generic
V(x)
and arbitrary
\beta>0
, this model is known in probability as a
\beta
-ensemble
and in statistical mechanics as a 1d
log-gas system
of
N
particles in a potential
V
at inverse temperature
\beta>0

[
For10
]
.

While the asymptotic behavior as
N\to\infty
of
\beta
-ensembles is well
understood for a fixed
\beta>0
(see for instance
[
BAG97
,
Joh98
,
DE02
,
VV09
,
RRV11
]
), the asymptotic analysis of
\beta
-ensembles in the
high-temperature regime
in which
N\rightarrow\infty
and
\beta\rightarrow 0
simultaneously with
N\beta\sim c
for some constant
c
has recently gained a lot of attention in the literature
[
ABG12
,
BGP15
,
DS15
,
NT18
,
AB19
,
Pak20
,
HL21
,
For22
,
BGCG22
]
.

It is well known by now that the remarkable
asymptotic properties of GUE as
N\to\infty
are reflected in
the asymptotics of its discrete counterpart,
given by the
Plancherel measure
on Young
diagrams
[
VK77
,
LS77
,
BDJ99
,
BOO00
,
Oko00
,
IO02
]
,
see e.g.
[
BDS16
]
and references therein. This
measure has a natural one-parameter deformation, introduced by Kerov
[
Ker00
]
, and called the
Jack–Plancherel
measure
. By definition, the Jack–Plancherel measure is the probability measure on the
set
\mathbb{Y}_{d}
of Young diagrams of a fixed size
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
that depends on the
Jack deformation parameter
\alpha>0
and is given by the formula

(1)

\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{d}(\lambda):=\frac{d!\,\alpha^{d}}{\prod_{(i,j)\in\lambda}\big(\alpha(\lambda_{i}-j)+(\lambda_{j}^{\prime}-i)+1\big)\big(\alpha(\lambda_{i}-j)+(\lambda_{j}^{\prime}-i)+\alpha\big)}.

It was expected (see e.g.
[
Oko03
]
) that the
Jack–Plancherel measure would be the most
natural discrete analogue of the Gaussian
\beta
-Ensemble;
the parameter
\alpha
would need to be related to the inverse temperature

\beta>0
by the relation
\alpha=2/\beta
.
This prediction was confirmed in several ways, for instance, the second author and
Féray proved in
[
DF16
]
the LLN and CLT for random Jack–Plancherel
Young diagrams and conjectured that the edge scaling limit is given by the
\beta
-Tracy–Widom law; this conjecture was later proved by Guionnet and Huang
[
GH19
]
.

In the last two decades, different models for discrete

\beta
-ensembles which are analogs of the continuous

\beta
-ensembles with a generic potential
V
, and simultaneously are generalizations of the
Jack–Plancherel measure, have been proposed.
It turns out that all these models are significantly different, and the methods developed for their analyses are different as well.

The first models are the
Jack measures
; they are natural

\alpha
-deformations of Schur measures
[
Oko01
]
and, at
the same time, distinguished limits of the Macdonald
measures
[
BC14
]
.
The study of Jack measures was first suggested by Borodin and Olshanski in
[
BO05
]

and were first studied in general by the third author in
[
Mol23
]
.
Jack measures are probability measures on the infinite set of all Young diagrams
\mathbb{Y}
defined by two homomorphisms
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\colon\SSym\to\mathbb{C}
on the real algebra of symmetric functions
\SSym
.
Denote the power-sum symmetric functions by
p_{1},p_{2},\dots
, and the Jack symmetric functions by

J^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}
. If

(2)

\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{\rho_{1}(p_{k})\overline{\rho_{2}(p_{k})}}{k}<\infty,\qquad\rho_{1}\left(J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}\right)\overline{\rho_{2}\left(J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}\right)}\geq 0,\quad\forall\,\lambda\in\mathbb{Y},

then the Jack measure
M_{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}^{(\alpha)}
is defined by the formula

(3)

M_{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}^{(\alpha)}(\lambda):=\frac{\rho_{1}\left(J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}\right)\overline{\rho_{2}\left(J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}\right)}}{j^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}}\exp\left(-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\rho_{1}(p_{k})\overline{\rho_{2}(p_{k})}}{k\alpha}}\right),\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}.

A second model, introduced by the second author and Śniady
[
DS19
]
,
defines probability measures on the finite set of Young diagrams

\mathbb{Y}_{d}
of a fixed size
d
. These measures

\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}
are defined by the decomposition of
a general choice of
reducible Jack character

\chi_{d}:\mathbb{Y}_{d}\to\mathbb{C}
into a convex combination of
irreducible Jack-characters

\chi^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}
:

(4)

\chi_{d}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}}\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(\lambda)\ \chi^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}.

The irreducible Jack characters are defined
as the normalized coefficients of the Jack polynomial

J^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}
expressed in the power-sum basis
p_{\mu}
, and they coincide
with the normalized irreducible characters of the symmetric groups in
the special case
\alpha=1
(see
Section

2
for more details).
In particular, the case
\alpha=1
recovers measures considered by
Biane
[
Bia01
]
and Śniady
[
Śni06
]
, in
the context of asymptotic representation theory of the symmetric groups.

Some other variants have been proposed, of which lastly we will mention
the
discrete
\beta
-ensembles
of Borodin–Gorin–Guionnet
[
BGG17
]
,
defined as certain probability measures on the set
\mathbb{Y}^{(N)}
of Young diagrams with at most
N
rows.
These measures have attracted much interest recently, e.g.
[
DK19
,
GH19
]
, because of their appearance, when
\beta=2
, as marginals of 2d statistical mechanical models, such as random tilings, last passage percolation, etc.

These discrete variants of
\beta
-ensembles all have different origins and are
even supported on different sets, namely
\mathbb{Y}
,
\mathbb{Y}_{d}
and
\mathbb{Y}^{(N)}
.
Remarkably, the asymptotic behaviors of large Young diagrams sampled from each of them
admit nice analyses and descriptions.
The third author proved in
[
Mol23
]
a weak version of LLN and
CLT for certain specialization of Jack measures in high, low,
and fixed temperature regimes
1
1

1

He used a different
parametrization for
M_{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}
; in particular his regimes are not the same as the
ones in this paper and his assumptions are not satisfied in
case of Jack–Thoma measures from this paper; see
[
Mol23
, Sec. 7.1.3]
.
under
certain regularity assumptions, provided that the specializations
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
satisfy
the nonnegativity assumption (
2
).
He considers especially the Jack measures
M_{\rho,\rho}
with the same specializations
\rho_{1}=\rho_{2}=:\rho
, so that (
2
) is trivially satisfied.
Next, in the work of the second author and Śniady
[
DS19
]
,
the authors prove a LLN and CLT for large random Young diagrams sampled by the
measures
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}
, when
(\chi_{d})_{d\geq 1}
is a
sequence of Jack characters satisfying the
Approximate Factorization
Property
or
AFP
(see
Section

2
for precise definitions). In the special case
\alpha=1
, it corresponds to the LLN
for random representations of large symmetric groups studied by
Biane
[
Bia01
]
and the CLT for those representations proved by
Śniady
[
Śni06
]
.
Finally, for the discrete
\beta
-ensembles
[
BGG17
]
, the authors prove a LLN and CLT when
\beta>0
is fixed, by using the discrete loop equations.

Our understanding of the
relations between all these models was limited to the simple fact
that their intersection includes (not directly, though) the Jack–Plancherel
measure (
1
). The problem of finding an intrinsic relation between these
models and their methods of analysis has been suggested to us on
several occasions by experts in the field, and the second author
proposed it already in his joint work with Śniady
[
DS19
, Section 1.3]
.
In this paper, we take a step forward in this direction, by establishing a deep connection between the first two models mentioned previously (Jack measures and Jack character measures with the AFP). Specifically, we find an infinite-parameter family of measures, called the
Jack–Thoma measures
, and their depoissonized versions which fit into both settings.
We prove that the global asymptotic behavior of these measures is governed by explicit formulas expressed in terms of simple and elegant combinatorial objects known as
lattice paths
.
Furthermore, we show that this special family of measures is dense enough so that, actually, all Jack character measures with the AFP display the same universal global-scale behavior.
The path to this surprising discovery was intricate, and we describe each step of our journey in detail, presenting a series of novel results along the way.

1.2.
Main result for Jack measures

Our first main results describe the asymptotic behavior of certain
Jack measures via explicit combinatorial formulas. Let
\alpha>0
,
\mathbf{v}=(v_{1},v_{2},\cdots)\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
, and
u>0
. We say that the measure

(5)

M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}(\lambda):=\exp\left(-\frac{u^{2}\cdot v_{1}}{\alpha}\right)\frac{J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}(u\cdot\mathbf{v})\cdot u^{|\lambda|}}{j^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}},\quad\forall\,\lambda\in\mathbb{Y},

is a
Jack–Thoma measure
; it is a special case of
Jack measure
M_{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}
from (
3
) with
the following choice of specializations:
\rho_{1}(p_{k})=u\cdot v_{k}
and
\rho_{2}(p_{k})=u\cdot\delta_{1,k}
, for all
k\geq 1
.
The nonnegativity assumption in (
2
) is no longer automatically satisfied, as
it was for the special case of
M_{\rho,\rho}
studied in
[
Mol23
]
.
For our Jack–Thoma measures, and for a fixed
\alpha>0
, the nonnegativity assumption
reduces to the classification of
\alpha
-Jack-positive specializations of
\SSym
.
This classification was completed by Kerov, Okounkov and Olshanski in
[
KOO98
]
,
where they extended the celebrated Thoma’s classification of extremal characters
of the infinite symmetric group (see
[
Tho64
,
KOO98
,
Mat19
]
).
Based on their result,
M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}
defines a probability measure on the set
\mathbb{Y}
of partitions of
arbitrary size whenever
u,\mathbf{v}
are points parametrized by the points from the
Thoma cone

\Omega=\{(a,b,c)\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}\times\mathbb{R}^{\infty}\times\mathbb{R}\mid a=(a_{1},a_{2},\cdots),\quad b=(b_{1},b_{2},\cdots),\\
a_{1}\geq a_{2}\geq\cdots\geq 0,\quad b_{1}\geq b_{2}\geq\cdots\geq 0,\quad\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{(a_{i}+b_{i})}\leq c\}

by setting
v_{1}=u^{-1}\cdot c
and
v_{k}=u^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}a_{i}^{k}+u^{-1}(-\alpha)^{1-k}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}b_{i}^{k}
,
for
k\geq 2
, and
u>0
is arbitrary.
This shows that the class of Jack–Thoma measures is a rich infinite-parameter family.
Moreover, there exists a unique measure belonging to both classes

M_{\rho,\rho}
and
M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}
, and it is the Jack–Plancherel measure.
Hence, next to the previously studied
M_{\rho,\rho}
, the Jack–Thoma measures
form the most natural class of Jack measures that exist.

We are interested in the behavior of
M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}
-distributed Young diagrams
for a generic choice of the infinite family of parameters
\mathbf{v}=(v_{1},v_{2},\cdots)\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
as the ratio
\frac{\alpha}{u^{2}}
tends
to zero. This asymptotic regime describes the typical behavior of a large

M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}
-distributed random Young diagram.
We study the asymptotic behavior of
M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}
in three different regimes: the

fixed temperature regime
when
\frac{\alpha-1}{u}\to 0
(for instance when
\alpha>0
is fixed and
u\to\infty
), the

high temperature regime
when
\frac{\alpha-1}{u}\to g>0
,
and the
low temperature regime
when
\frac{\alpha-1}{u}\to g<0
.
Therefore, in this subsection assume that
\alpha=\alpha(d)
,
u=u(d)
are
sequences of positive real numbers such that

(6)

\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\alpha}{u^{2}}=0,\qquad\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\alpha-1}{u}=g\in\mathbb{R},\qquad M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}(\lambda)\geq 0,\quad\forall\,\lambda\in\mathbb{Y},\ \forall\,d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

Note that in the case when
g\neq 0
(the high and low temperature regimes), it is quite nontrivial to find sequences
\alpha,u
that satisfy the conditions (
6
), with the nonnegativity of
M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}
being especially troublesome.
When
g=0
and
\alpha>0
is fixed, we noted that
[
KOO98
]
furnishes examples where
v_{k}
depends on
\alpha
; however, this becomes an issue if
\alpha
varies, because the parameters
v_{k}
must remain constant.
Nevertheless, we manage to find a large family of pairs
(g,\mathbf{v})\in(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})\times\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
for which these conditions are satisfied: see
Definition

4.5
and
Proposition

4.6
.
Our construction is based on a new classification theorem for
totally Jack-positive specializations
, see
Theorem

4.4
, i.e. specializations which take nonnegative values on all Jack symmetric functions
J^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}
, for all
\alpha>0
.

In our first main limit theorem, we prove that the profile
\omega_{\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}}
of a rescaled random Young diagram
\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}
, obtained from a Young diagram
\lambda
by replacing each box of size
1\times 1
by a box of size
\frac{\alpha}{u\sqrt{v_{1}}}\times\frac{1}{u\sqrt{v_{1}}}
, concentrates around a deterministic
shape, and this holds true in all three limit regimes.
Let us remark that each box in the Young diagram was rescaled to have size
\frac{\alpha}{u\sqrt{v_{1}}}\times\frac{1}{u\sqrt{v_{1}}}
, because of the following two principles that we followed:

\bullet
Each box should be
\alpha
-anisotropic, meaning that the ratio between the width and height is
\alpha
.

\bullet
The expected area of a
M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}
–distributed random Young diagram, after the rescaling, should be equal to
1
.
2
2

2

See
Section

3.7
and specifically
Example

3
.

As a side comment, we mention that we deduce our first main theorem from a general statement about the convergence of random functions (see
Theorem

3.15
) that might be helpful in a wide variety of similar contexts, and is of independent interest.
Here is the abbreviated version of our first main result (and see
Theorem

3.7
for the detailed version).

Theorem 1.1
.

Fix
g\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbf{v}=(v_{1},v_{2},\cdots)\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
. Suppose that
\alpha,u\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
satisfy (
6
).
Then there exists some deterministic function
\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
with the property that

(7)

\lim_{d\rightarrow\infty}\omega_{\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}}=\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}.

The limit in (
7
) means convergence with respect to the supremum norm, in probability.
Moreover, the limit shape
\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}
is described by explicit formulas for various
observables that uniquely determine it (see (
8
) below for the moments of the
associated transition measure, and (
84
) for the moments of the measure with the density
\frac{\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}(x)-|x|}{2}
).
These formulas are expressed in terms of a weighted enumeration of Łukasiewicz paths.

Informally, a
Łukasiewicz path
is a directed lattice path
with steps
(1,k),\,k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-1}
, starting at
(0,0)
, finishing at
(\ell,0)
, and staying in the first quadrant.
These objects are in bijection with non-crossing
set partitions, a combinatorial model intrinsically related to free
probability (see
Remark

11
and the discussion
in
Section

1.3
).
Consider the following weighted generating
series for the set
\mathbf{L}_{0}(\ell)
of Łukasiewicz paths of length
\ell
:

(8)

M_{\ell}:=v_{1}^{-\ell/2}\cdot\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathbf{L}_{0}(\ell)}{\prod_{i\geq 0}(ig)^{\text{number of horizontal steps at height $i$}}\cdot\prod_{j\geq 1}v_{j}^{\text{number of steps $(1,j)$}}}.

We prove that the
\ell
-th moment of the transition measure

\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
associated with the limit shape

\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}
via the
Markov–Krein correspondence is given by this series:

\int_{\mathbb{R}}{x^{\ell}\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}(dx)}=M_{\ell},\quad\text{for all $\ell\geq 1$}.

Note that in the special case when
g=0
(e.g. when
\alpha>0
is fixed),
\mathbf{v}=(1,0,0\dots)

and
\mathbf{v}=(1,c,c^{2},\dots)
, our formula (
8
)
recovers the combinatorial formula for the moments of the
semicircle
distribution
and the
free Poisson distribution
, respectively.
These two special cases correspond to the LLN for the
Jack–Plancherel measure (
[
LS77
,
VK77
]
for

\alpha=1
,
[
DF16
]
for general
\alpha>0
) and for the
Schur-Weyl measure (
[
Bia01
]
for

\alpha=1
); the
high and low temperature regimes give one-parameter
deformations of these laws.
In particular, note that when specialized to
\alpha=1,\,g=0
, we provide a new proof of Biane’s result, see
Remark

11
.
Moreover, Jack–Thoma measures are distinguished mixtures of the measures on partitions of a fixed size already
studied by Kerov, Okounkov and Olshanski
[
KOO98
]
in the
context of harmonic functions on the Young graph with Jack
multiplicities and a fixed
\alpha
. In light of our depoissonization analysis (see
Section

1.3
), our
Theorem

1.1
describes the limit
shape for all these measures. We want to point out here that this limit
shape is described not only for an arbitrary and fixed parameter

\alpha
(which turns out to be the same as in the
\alpha=1
case
studied by Okounkov in
[
Oko00
]
), but it also covers the high and low temperature limits of the Kerov–Okounkov–Olshanski measures.

We conclude our analysis of Jack–Thoma measures by proving
the central limit theorem. We determine the
(non-centered) Gaussian process describing fluctuations around the limit shape
by giving explicit formulas for its mean shift and covariance matrix.
For technical reasons, we require that the assumptions on the
growth of
\alpha
and
u
in (
6
) are slightly
enhanced and replaced by the following conditions (see
Remark

8
):

(9)

\frac{\alpha}{u^{2}}=\frac{1}{d}+o\left(\frac{1}{d}\right),\quad\frac{\alpha-1}{u}=g+\frac{g^{\prime}}{\sqrt{d}}+o\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right),\quad\text{ as }d\to\infty.

This is the abbreviated version of our CLT (see
Theorem

3.8
for the precise statement):

Theorem 1.2
.

Fix
g,g^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbf{v}=(v_{1},v_{2},\cdots)\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
.
Suppose that the sequences of positive reals
\alpha,u
satisfy (
9
).
Then there exists a Gaussian process
\Delta_{g,g^{\prime};\mathbf{v}}
such that the random functions

\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}:=\frac{u}{\sqrt{\alpha}}\bigg(\frac{\omega_{\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}}-\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}}{2}\bigg)

converge to
\Delta_{g,g^{\prime};\mathbf{v}}
in distribution for multidimensional
polynomial test functions. This process is uniquely determined
by equation (
65
) that describes its mean vector,
and equation (
66
) that describes its covariance matrix.

To prove these results, we develop a combinatorial approach to
Nazarov–Sklyanin operators, building on the work initiated
in
[
Mol23
]
. For random partitions distributed according to the Jack–Thoma measures,
and their transition measures, we can describe various important observables
by elegant combinatorial models of Łukasiewicz ribbon paths that we introduce here
(e.g. see
Theorem

3.10
).
These combinatorial formulas allowed us to take limits in our regimes of interest and were the basis of our LLN and CLT. Finally, let us reiterate that we are able to construct, in
Section

4
, a very rich family
of specializations with certain positivity properties that lead to valid sequences of measures for which
Theorem

1.1
and
Theorem

1.2
are applicable in the high and low temperature regimes. These sequences are parametrized by
g\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
and
c\geq a_{1}\geq a_{2}\geq\cdots\geq 0
with
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{a_{i}}\leq c
(see
Theorem

4.4

and
Proposition

4.6
). These ideas turn out to be a key link between Jack–Thoma measures and Jack character measures with the AFP, thus allowing us to establish that the asymptotic behavior of both models is governed by universal formulas expressed in terms of Łukasiewicz ribbon paths.

1.3.
Main result for measures with the approximate factorization property

Figure 1
.
The profiles of anisotropically-scaled Young diagrams of size
d=400
and
d=6400
, sampled
by the Jack–Schur–Weyl measure from
Section

2.6.2
with
parameters
\alpha=4
,
N=\sqrt{\alpha d}
, where each box is not of
size
1\times 1
, but of size
\frac{2}{\sqrt{d}}\times\frac{1}{2\sqrt{d}}
.
The blue curve is the limit shape in the fixed temperature regime (fixed
\alpha>0
),
discovered by Biane
[
Bia01
]
.

Our second main result relates the previously studied Jack measures
with the model introduced by the second author and
Śniady in
[
DS19
]
. We recall that given a general reducible Jack character

\chi_{d}\colon\mathbb{Y}_{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, we can associate a probability measure on the set of Young
diagrams
\mathbb{Y}_{d}
of size
d
by the formula
(
4
). The Jack characters

\chi_{d}
which are interesting from the probabilistic point of view are those which have the
approximate factorization
property
(
AFP
). Roughly speaking, in the simplest case this property says
that the character
\chi_{d}(\pi_{1}\cdot\pi_{2})
, evaluated on the disjoint product of
partitions
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
, behaves asymptotically the same as the product of characters

\chi_{d}(\pi_{1})\cdot\chi_{d}(\pi_{2})
, and
similar formulas hold asymptotically for all higher order cumulants;
see
Section

2
for the precise definitions. The second author and Śniady
proved in
[
DS19
]
the LLN and CLT for the Jack character measures
with the AFP in all three previously discussed regimes
(see
Theorem

2.4
and
Theorem

2.5
).

The primary tool used in
[
DS19
]
is the rich algebraic
structure of Jack characters. This approach, also known as the

dual approach
, stems from the successful viewpoint of Kerov and
Olshanski
[
KO94
]
, who observed that the set of
characters of the symmetric groups, treated as functions on the set
of Young diagrams representing the associated irreducible
representation, is strictly related to the algebra of shifted
symmetric functions. The generalization of this approach to the
setting of Jack characters was proposed by Lassalle
in
[
Las08
,
Las09
]
, who formulated challenging
conjectures on various positivity phenomena of Jack
characters. Despite significant
efforts
[
DFS14
,
DF16
,
Ś19
,
BD23
]
,
these conjectures remain wide open prior to our work.
3
3

3

The
second author in a joint work with Ben Dali
[
BDD23
]

have found a proof of the Lassalle’s positivity
conjecture from
[
Las08
]
that relies on the
results obtained in this paper.
Consequently, finding formulas
for the moments of the limiting transition measure

\mu_{\Lambda_{\infty}}
from
Theorem

2.4
and for the
covariance of the Gaussian process
\Delta_{\infty}

from
Theorem

2.5
turned out to be far beyond our reach. Our second main result solves this problem and we show that that the formulas for the moments and for the
covariance described in the previous section are in fact universal for both models.

Theorem 1.3

(Abbreviated version of
Theorems

5.1
and
5.2
)
.

Assume that
\chi_{d}\colon\mathbb{Y}_{d}\to\mathbb{R}
are reducible Jack characters
such that the sequence
(\chi_{d})_{d\geq 1}
fulfills the
AFP (enhanced AFP, respectively) given by
Definition

2.2
. Let

\mu_{\Lambda_{\infty}}
and
\Delta_{\infty}
be the limiting
transition measure and the limiting Gaussian process described
by
Theorem

2.4
and
Theorem

2.5
, respectively. Then
the moments
M_{\ell}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}{x^{\ell}\mu_{\Lambda_{\infty}}(dx)}
are given by the
polynomial formula (
8
), and the mean shift
\mathbb{E}[\langle\Delta_{\infty},\,x^{\ell-2}\rangle]
and the covariance
\Cov\left(\langle\Delta_{\infty},x^{k-2}\rangle,\langle\Delta_{\infty},x^{l-2}\rangle\right)
are given by the explicit
polynomial formulas (
101
) and (
102
), respectively,
that are obtained from the universal formulas (
65
)
and (
66
) by incorporating the terms

v_{k}^{\prime},v_{(k|l)}\,
from the second order asymptotics of the character
\chi_{d}
(given by (
24
)).

Our method for proving
Theorem

1.3
synthesizes tools described in the previous section with algebraic tools developed in
[
DF16
,
DS19
]
. We show that the Jack–Thoma measures described earlier are, in fact, a Poissonized version of a specific class of examples of measures with the AFP (see
Footnote

6
). This allows us to deduce
Theorem

1.3
for this specific class. However, to understand the asymptotic behavior of arbitrary measures with the AFP, we need new ideas.
We use the theory developed in
[
DF16
,
DS19
]
to prove that the class of examples from
Footnote

6
is large enough to ensure that the polynomial formulas described in
Theorem

1.3
are universal for the entire class of measures with the AFP.

1.4.
Connections to Free Probability Theory and Algebraic Combinatorics

Before delving into direct applications of our formulas, it is worth
mentioning some interesting ramifications of our work in related subjects.

Biane’s fundamental work
[
Bia98
]
established a connection between asymptotic representation theory of symmetric groups and free probability; we recover this fact because (
8
) implies that the
v_{k}
’s are the free cumulants of the limiting transition measure
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
when
g=0
, see
Remark

11
.
For a general
g
, the formula (
8
) defines a novel notion of

g
-deformed free cumulants
that we hope to investigate in future works.
Other such deformations have appeared previously in
the literature, e.g. see
[
BGCG22
,
MP22
,
Nic95
]
. Ours is more similar to the one in

[
BGCG22
,
MP22
]
because both
have, conjecturally, the property that the natural notion of
g
-deformed free
convolution is positivity preserving:
4
4

4

The
q
-deformed free
convolution of Nica
[
Nic95
]
is not positivity preserving,
see
[
Ora05
]
.
if
\mu,\nu
are probability measures, it
is conjectured that there exists a probability measure

\mu\boxplus_{g}\nu
whose
g
-cumulants are the sums of the

g
-cumulants of
\mu
and
\nu
. This is related to the conjecture of
Stanley
[
Sta89
]
on the positivity of
the structure constants for Jack polynomials. Furthermore, a different
positivity of the
g
-deformed free cumulant
v_{k}
was proved by
Śniady
[
Ś19
]
: there exists a polynomial
K_{k}(-g,R_{2},R_{3},\dots)
in
g
and the
free cumulants
R_{2},R_{3},\dots
with positive integer coefficients,
such that
v_{k}=K_{k}(-g,R_{2},R_{3},\dots)
. This polynomial is the top-degree part of
the so-called
Kerov polynomials
, whose positivity was conjectured by
Lassalle
[
Las09
]
and is still wide open (see
also
[
Fér09
,
DFŚ10
,
DF16
]
). Śniady found a
combinatorial interpretation of polynomials
K_{k}
in terms of graphs
embedded into non-oriented surfaces such that the exponent of
g
is
computed by a recursive decomposition of these graphs. In practice,
controlling this exponent is almost impossible, except for some special
cases, see
[
DFS14
,
Doł17
,
CD22
,
BD22
]
. Comparing to Śniady’s formula, our combinatorial
interpretation involves drastically simpler
combinatorial objects and, most importantly, the exponent of
g
is an
explicit statistic of the underlying
path. Therefore we provide new combinatorial tools to attack
Lassalle’s positivity conjectures
[
Las08
,
Las09
]

and they already turned out to be very successful — the second
author in a joint work with Ben Dali
[
BDD23
]
used the results presented here
to prove integrality of the coefficients in Kerov’s polynomials and to
fully prove Lassalle’s conjecture from
[
Las08
]
. We believe
that the tools developed in this paper will lead to ultimate
understanding of the Kerov’s polynomials.

1.5.
Further applications

Figure 2
.
The limit shape
\Lambda_{g;(1,0,0,\cdots)}
with
g\!=\!-\frac{1}{4}
(low temperature regime),

\mathbf{v}\!=\!(1,0,0,\cdots)
, drawn in the French notation and obtained from
Theorem

1.4
, is shaded in light blue.
Juxtaposed is an anisotropically-scaled Young diagram
\lambda^{(d)}
of size
d=1600
sampled by the Jack–Plancherel measure
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{d}
with
\alpha=\frac{4^{2}}{d}
, so each box (with gray-colored sides) is not of size
1\!\times\!1
, but of size
\frac{4}{d}\!\times\!\frac{1}{4}
.
The three smallest zeroes of the Bessel function
J_{-4z}(8)
are equal to

l^{(1/4)}_{1}=-1.086...
,
l^{(1/4)}_{2}=-0.424...
, and
l^{(1/4)}_{3}=0.102...
, therefore

Theorem

1.4
implies

\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\lambda^{(d)}_{1}}{-g\cdot d}=1.336...
,

\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\lambda^{(d)}_{2}}{-g\cdot d}=0.924...
,
and
\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\lambda^{(d)}_{3}}{-g\cdot d}=0.647...
in probability.

Theorem

1.1
implies that the limit shape
\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}
in the fixed temperature regime (i.e., when
g=0
) does not depend on the choice of
\alpha>0
, as long as it is fixed, and is the same as in the classical setting when
\alpha=1
. In this case, the limit shape is
balanced
, meaning that
\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}(x)=|x|
, for
|x|
large enough. However, in the high and low temperature regimes, i.e., when
g>0
and
g<0
, respectively, the situation is different.

Drawing an analogy with recent studies on the high and low temperature
regimes of log-gases (see
Section

1.1
), we apply our
combinatorial formulas to study the limit shape

\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}
(or equivalently, the associated transition
measure
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
) in the high and low temperature regimes. We show
that, unlike in the continuous counterpart of log-gases, there is a
phase transition in our models from the fixed
temperature regime to the high and low temperature
regimes. Specifically, we prove that for
g\neq 0
, the measure

\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
is discrete with an infinite support bounded
only on one side and with a unique accumulation point at
+\infty
or

-\infty
, depending on the sign of
g
(see

Propositions

6.6
and
6.7
, and compare
Fig.

2
to
Fig.

1
).

Finally, we relate
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
to a specific unbounded Toeplitz-like
matrix, and we use it to describe the edge asymptotics of the random
partitions. By analogy with the celebrated
works of Baik, Borodin, Deift, Johanson, Okounkov, Olshanski
[
BDJ99
,
BOO00
,
Oko00
]
on the
relation between the asymptotics of the largest rows of
Plancherel-sampled Young diagrams and the asymptotics of the largest
eigenvalues of GUE,
we relate the asymptotics of the largest rows of
Jack–Plancherel-sampled Young diagrams with the asymptotics of the largest eigenvalues
of finite deterministic Jacobi matrices. It allows us to prove the
following theorem that gives an explicit description of the limit
shape of Jack–Plancherel-sampled Young diagrams in the regimes of high and low temperature,
and expresses the asymptotics of the largest rows in terms of zeroes of the
Bessel function of the first kind, as a function of the label parameter (here we only state the abbreviated version for the case of low temperature, i.e. for
g<0
; see
Theorem

6.12
for the more general statement and
Fig.

2
to see the limit shape drawn with the help of this theorem).

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
(\lambda^{(d)}\in\mathbb{Y}_{d})_{d\geq 1}
be a sequence of random Young
diagrams distributed by the Jack–Plancherel measures.
Next, assume that
g<0
and let

\big\{l_{1}^{(-g)}<l_{2}^{(-g)}<\cdots\big\}=\{z\in\mathbb{R}\colon J_{z\cdot g^{-1}}(-2g^{-1})=0\}

be the set of zeroes of the Bessel function of the first kind
J_{z\cdot g^{-1}}(-2g^{-1})
.
In the regime
d,\alpha^{-1}\to\infty,\,\sqrt{\alpha d}\to-g^{-1}
, then

\frac{\lambda^{(d)}_{i}}{-g\cdot d}\longrightarrow-l_{i}^{(-g)}-ig,\quad\text{in probability},

for all
i=1,2,\cdots
, and moreover the limit shape is

\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}(x)=\begin{cases}x,&\text{ if }x>-l_{1}^{(-g)}-g,\\
-2\big(l_{i}^{(-g)}+ig\big)-x,&\text{ if }x\in\big[-l_{i}^{(-g)},\ -l_{i}^{(-g)}-g\big],\text{ for some }i\geq 1,\\
x-2ig,&\text{ if }x\in\big[-l_{i+1}^{(-g)}-g,\ -l_{i}^{(-g)}\big],\text{ for some }i\geq 1.\end{cases}

1.6.
Organization of the paper

In
Section

2
, we review the definition of Jack characters with the AFP, their associated probability measures, and we
restate the LLN and CLT obtained in
[
DS19
]
.
We also give a large family of examples relating this model to the Jack–Thoma measures.
The latter model is introduced in
Section

3
, where we also prove our first main theorems:
the LLN and CLT for the Jack–Thoma measures.

Section

4
is devoted to study Jack-positive specializations in different
regimes and relating our new family of measures with the Thoma cone.
In
Section

5
, we prove our second main theorem: first we show that
the conditional Jack–Thoma measure fits into the model of Jack-characters
with the AFP, and second we study the algebraic structure of Jack characters
in order to deduce formulas for the moments of the limiting measure and for the mean shift and covariance of the limiting Gaussian process.
The last
Section

6
is devoted to the study of the
limit shape, as well as its transition measure, arising from the high and low temperature limits of random partitions.
In particular, for the example of the Jack–Plancherel measure, we find the limits in probability of the largest rows/columns of the random partitions in terms of the zeroes of the Bessel function.

2.
First model of random Young diagrams: Jack characters and the asymptotic factorization property

In this section and the next, we review two different constructions of
probability measures on the set of Young diagrams that depend on the
Jack parameter
,
and that were introduced in
[
DS19
,
Mol23
]
.
It turns out that these two a priori different models are intimately related
(see e.g.
Proposition

5.3
), and this relation will be exploited
later in
Section

5
.
In this section, we review the construction from
[
DS19
]

that yields probability measures on the finite set
\mathbb{Y}_{d}
of Young diagrams of a fixed size
d
,
and is inspired by the relation between symmetric functions and the
representation theory of symmetric groups; see
[
Bia01
,
Śni06
]
.
We also review the LLN and CLT as the size
d
of the partitions tends to infinity.

2.1.
Asymptotics of the Jack parameter

In this paper, we employ the Jack parameter, classically denoted by
\alpha>0
.
Later in this section, when we state our limit theorems, we will have instead a sequence
(\alpha(d))_{d\geq 1}
of Jack parameters, i.e. of positive real numbers. To simplify our notation, a general term of this sequence will be denoted by
\alpha=\alpha(d)
. We will assume that

(10)

\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha}}}{\sqrt{d}}=g+\frac{g^{\prime}}{\sqrt{d}}+o\left(d^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right),\ \text{ as }d\to\infty,

for some constants
g,g^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
.

The regime when
g>0
and
\alpha,\,d
are of the same order, i.e.
\alpha=\Theta(d)
(resp.
g<0
and
\alpha=\Theta(d^{-1})
) is called the
high temperature regime
(resp.
low temperature regime
). Likewise, the regime when
g=0
,
\,\alpha=\Theta(1)
and
g^{\prime}=\sqrt{\alpha}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha}}
is called the
fixed temperature regime
.
5
5

5

The parameter
\alpha
should be regarded as
2/\beta
, where
\beta>0
is the
inverse temperature
parameter from log-gas systems or beta ensembles, e.g. see
[
For22
]
.

2.2.
Conditional cumulants

Let
\mathbb{Y}_{d}
denote the set of partitions (equivalently, Young diagrams)
of
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and let
\mathbb{Y}:=\bigsqcup_{d\geq 0}{\mathbb{Y}_{d}}

be the set of all partitions. For any
\mu,\mu^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Y}
, we define their
product

\mu\cdot\mu^{\prime}
as their union,
for example
(4,2,1)\cdot(5,3,1)=(5,4,3,2,1,1)
. In this way,
the set of all partitions
\mathbb{Y}

becomes a unital semigroup with the unit being the empty partition
\emptyset
. The
corresponding semigroup algebra of formal linear combinations
of partitions is denoted by
\mathbb{R}[\mathbb{Y}]
.
Any function
\chi\colon\mathbb{Y}\to\mathbb{R}
can be canonically extended to a linear map
\chi\colon\mathbb{R}[\mathbb{Y}]\to\mathbb{R}
, such that
\chi(1)=1
, which will be denoted by the same symbol.

For any partitions
\mu^{1},\dots,\mu^{n}\in\mathbb{Y}
, we define their
conditional cumulant

(or simply
cumulant
, for simplicity) with respect to the
function
\chi\colon\mathbb{Y}\to\mathbb{R}
as a coefficient in the expansion of the following
formal power series in
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}
:

(11)

\kappa^{\chi}_{n}(\mu^{1},\dots,\mu^{n}):=\left.\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial t_{1}\cdots\partial t_{n}}\log\chi\left(e^{t_{1}\mu^{1}+\cdots+t_{n}\mu^{n}}\right)\right|_{t_{1}=\cdots=t_{n}=0}.

An important case for us will be when each partition
consist of just one part
\mu^{i}=(k_{i})
; then we will use a simplified notation:

\kappa^{\chi}_{n}(k_{1},\dots,k_{n}):=\kappa^{\chi}_{n}\big((k_{1}),\dots,(k_{n})\big).

For example,

\displaystyle\kappa^{\chi}_{1}(k)

\displaystyle=\chi(k),

\displaystyle\kappa^{\chi}_{2}(k,l)

\displaystyle=\chi(k,l)-\chi(k)\chi(l).

2.3.
Irreducible Jack characters

Let
\alpha>0
be a positive real.
Let
J^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}
, denote the integral versions of Jack
symmetric functions; see
[
Mac95
, Ch. VI.10]
.
For any
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}
, expand

J^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}
into power-sum symmetric functions:

(12)

J^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}=\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}}\theta^{(\alpha)}_{\mu}(\lambda)\ p_{\mu}.

For a partition
\mu
with
m_{i}(\mu)
parts equal to
i
, define the
standard numerical factor

(13)

z_{\mu}:=\prod_{i\geq 1}m_{i}(\mu)!\ i^{m_{i}(\mu)}.

We renormalize the coefficients
\theta^{(\alpha)}_{\mu}(\lambda)
by
setting

(14)

\chi^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}(\mu):=\alpha^{-\frac{\|\mu\|}{2}}\ \frac{z_{\mu}}{d!}\ \theta^{(\alpha)}_{\mu}(\lambda),

where

\|\mu\|:=|\mu|-\ell(\mu),\quad|\mu|:=\sum_{i\geq 0}\mu_{i},\quad\ell(\mu):=\sum_{i\geq 1}m_{i}(\mu),

and call them
irreducible Jack characters
. This terminology
is justified by the fact that in the special case
\alpha=1
, the
irreducible Jack character
\chi^{(\alpha=1)}_{\lambda}(\mu)
coincides with
the normalized character of the irreducible representation of the
symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{d}
that is associated to
\lambda
.

We regard each irreducible Jack character
\chi^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}
,
as a function
\mathbb{Y}_{d}\to\mathbb{R}
. It is known that
\theta^{(\alpha)}_{(1^{d})}(\lambda)=1

(see
[
Mac95
, (10.29)]
), therefore

(15)

\chi^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}(1^{d})=1,\text{ for any }\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}.

Moreover we have the following duality between irreducible Jack
characters:

(16)

\chi_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}(\mu)=(-1)^{\|\mu\|}\chi_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{(1/\alpha)}(\mu),

where
\lambda^{\prime}
is the conjugate of the partition
\lambda
.
For this, recall the automorphism
\omega_{1/\alpha}
of the algebra
of symmetric functions
\Symm
defined by
\omega_{1/\alpha}(p_{r})=(-1)^{r-1}\alpha^{-1}p_{r}
, for all
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
,
see
[
Mac95
, Ch. VI, (10.6)]
. It satisfies, see

[
Mac95
, Ch. VI, (10.24)]
,

(17)

\omega_{1/\alpha}(J^{(1/\alpha)}_{\lambda})=\alpha^{-|\lambda|}J^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda^{\prime}}\quad\forall\,\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}.

This directly implies that

\theta^{(\alpha)}_{\mu}(\lambda)=(-\alpha)^{\|\mu\|}\cdot\theta^{(1/\alpha)}_{\mu}(\lambda^{\prime}),

and plugging it into definition of the irreducible Jack character
(
14
) yields the result.

Remark 1
.

In the special case
\alpha=1
, this identity expressed in the
language of representation theory states that the tensor product
of the irreducible
representation of
\mathfrak{S}_{d}
associated with partition
\lambda
with the sign representation corresponds to the
irreducible representation associated with the conjugate partition
\lambda^{\prime}
.

2.4.
Characters with the approximate factorization property and
associated probability measures

A general function
\chi_{d}\colon\mathbb{Y}_{d}\to\mathbb{R}
will be called a

reducible Jack character
(or simply
Jack character
, for short)
if it is a convex combination of the irreducible Jack characters.
In particular, this definition and (
15
) imply that
\chi_{d}(1^{d})=1
.

Definition 2.1

(First model of random Young diagrams)
.

Given a Jack character
\chi_{d}:\mathbb{Y}_{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, its associated
probability measure
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}
on the set
of Young diagrams
\mathbb{Y}_{d}
is defined by the expansion:

(18)

\chi_{d}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}}\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(\lambda)\ \chi^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}.

Remark 2
.

It was proved in
[
DF16
]
that

\{\chi^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}(\cdot)\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}}
is a linear basis
of the space of functions
\mathbb{Y}_{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, so
the decomposition (
18
) uniquely determines the values
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(\lambda)
.

Remark 3
.

If
\chi_{d}:\mathbb{Y}_{d}\to\mathbb{C}
is any function with
\chi_{d}(1^{d})=1
, then (
18
) defines a complex measure
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}
on
\mathbb{Y}_{d}
with total mass
1
.
If
\chi_{d}
takes real values, then
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}
is a signed measure.

Example 1
.

The representation theory of the infinite symmetric group
S(\infty)
furnishes a large class of probability measures as in
Definition

2.1
, in the case
\alpha=1
.
In fact, let
\chi\colon S(\infty)\to\mathbb{C}
be a character of
S(\infty)
, i.e. a central, positive definite function that takes value
1
at the identity.
Denote the restriction of
\chi
to
S(d)\subset S(\infty)
by
\chi\big|_{S(d)}\colon S(d)\to\mathbb{C}
, and let
\chi_{d}\colon\mathbb{Y}_{d}\to\mathbb{C}
be defined by

\chi_{d}(\mu):=\chi\big|_{S(d)}\big(\textrm{any permutation of cycle-type }\mu\big),\quad\textrm{ for all }\mu\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}.

Then
\chi_{d}
takes real values and, moreover,
\chi_{d}
is a Jack character for
\alpha=1
.
In particular,
\chi_{d}
leads to a valid probability measure
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha=1)}_{\chi_{d}}
by the prescription of
Definition

2.1
;
for a proof, see
[
BO17
, Prop. 1.6 and Prop. 3.5]
.
As
d\to\infty
, these measures converge, in certain sense, to the
spectral measure
of
\chi
on the Thoma simplex; see
[
BO17
, Sec. 7]
for the precise statements and for further ideas in this direction.

We need to extend the definition of cumulants (recall
Eq.

11
) to make sense of
\kappa^{\chi_{d}}_{n}(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})
for Jack characters
\chi_{d}\colon\mathbb{Y}_{d}\to\mathbb{R}
.
First, extend the domain of
\chi_{d}
to the set

\mathbb{Y}_{\leq d}:=\bigsqcup_{0\leq k\leq d}\mathbb{Y}_{k}

of partitions of smaller numbers by adding an appropriate number of parts, each equal to
1
:

(19)

\chi_{d}(\pi):=\chi_{d}(\pi,1^{d-|\pi|})\qquad\text{for }|\pi|\leq d.

In this way, the cumulant
\kappa^{\chi_{d}}_{n}(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})

is well-defined if
d\geq k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n}
is large enough.
Note that
\chi_{d}(\emptyset)=1
.

Definition 2.2
.

Assume that for each integer
d\geq 1
, we are given a function
\chi_{d}\colon\mathbb{Y}_{d}\to\mathbb{R}
.

We say that the sequence
(\chi_{d})_{d\geq 1}
has the
approximate factorization property
(the
AFP
for short)
[
Śni06
,
DS19
]
if the following two conditions are satisfied:

(i) For each integer
n\geq 1
and all integers
k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\geq 2
, the limit

(20)

\lim_{d\to\infty}\kappa^{\chi_{d}}_{n}(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})\ d^{\frac{k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n}+n-2}{2}}

exists and is finite.

(ii) The following quantity is finite

(21)

\sup_{k\geq 2}\frac{\sqrt[k]{|v_{k}|}}{k}<\infty,

where
v_{k}
are the limits appearing in
(
20
) for
n=1
:

(22)

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\kappa^{\chi_{d}}_{1}(k)\ d^{\frac{k-1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{d\to\infty}\chi_{d}(k)\ d^{\frac{k-1}{2}}=v_{k}.

Further, we say that
(\chi_{d})_{d\geq 1}
has the
enhanced AFP
if, in addition to (i) and (ii),
the condition (
22
) is strengthened as follows: for each
k\geq 2
,
there exist some real constants
v_{k}^{\prime}
such that

(23)

\kappa^{\chi_{d}}_{1}(k)\ d^{\frac{k-1}{2}}=\chi_{d}(k)\ d^{\frac{k-1}{2}}=v_{k}+v_{k}^{\prime}\,d^{-\frac{1}{2}}+o\left(d^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right),\qquad\text{for }d\to\infty.

In the following, for each
k,l\geq 2
, we define the real constants

v_{(k|l)}=v_{(l|k)}
as the limits appearing in (
20
) for
n=2
:

(24)

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\kappa^{\chi_{d}}_{2}(k,l)\ d^{\frac{k+l}{2}}

\displaystyle=v_{(k|l)}.

Remark 4
.

Our definitions of the AFP and enhanced AFP differ from those in
[
DS19
]
.
We also point out that condition (
21
) might be relaxed in such a way that
Theorems

2.4
and
2.5
remain satisfied; see
[
DS19
, (1.13)]
for an example of such a weaker condition in some cases.

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
\mathbf{v}=(v_{1},v_{2},\cdots)\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
and
\pm\mathbf{v}:=(v_{1},-v_{2},v_{3},-v_{4},\cdots)
. For
g,g^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
define

\mathcal{F}^{\eAFP}_{g,g^{\prime},\mathbf{v}}\subset\mathcal{F}^{\AFP}_{g,g^{\prime},\mathbf{v}}

as the sets of the sequences of Jack characters with the enhanced AFP, and the AFP,
respectively, with the parameters
g,g^{\prime},\mathbf{v}
described
in (
10
) and

Definition

2.2
. Then, we have the
bijective map

\mathcal{F}^{\AFP}_{g,g^{\prime},\mathbf{v}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}^{\AFP}_{-g,-g^{\prime},\pm\mathbf{v}},\quad\left(\chi^{(\alpha)}_{d}\right)_{d\geq 1}\mapsto\left(\tilde{\chi}^{(\tilde{\alpha})}_{d}\right)_{d\geq 1},

which restricts to the bijective map
\mathcal{F}^{\eAFP}_{g,g^{\prime},\mathbf{v}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}^{\eAFP}_{-g,-g^{\prime},\pm\mathbf{v}}
and is given by

(25)

\tilde{\alpha}:=\alpha^{-1},\quad\tilde{\chi}^{(\tilde{\alpha})}_{d}(\mu):=(-1)^{\|\mu\|}\chi^{(\alpha)}_{d}(\mu).

In particular,

(26)

\mathbb{P}^{(\tilde{\alpha})}_{\tilde{\chi}_{d}}(\lambda)=\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(\lambda^{\prime}).

Proof.

Most claims follow directly from our definition for
\tilde{\chi}^{(\tilde{\alpha})}_{d}
.
In fact, the only non-trivial statement is that

\tilde{\chi}^{(\tilde{\alpha})}_{d}
are Jack characters, but this is assured by (
26
).
Thus, it remains only to prove (
26
):

\tilde{\chi}^{(\tilde{\alpha})}_{d}(\mu)=(-1)^{\|\mu\|}\chi^{(\alpha)}_{d}(\mu)=(-1)^{\|\mu\|}\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}}\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(\lambda)\ \chi^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}(\mu)=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}}\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(\lambda^{\prime})\ \chi^{(\tilde{\alpha})}_{\lambda}(\mu),

where the last equality follows from (
16
).
∎

2.5.
The LLN and CLT for characters with the approximate factorization property

The main results of
[
DS19
]
are the Law of Large Numbers
(LLN) and Central Limit Theorem (CLT) for large random Young
diagrams. In order to state them, we need to work with rescaled Young diagrams. For two
positive numbers
w,h\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and a given Young diagram
\lambda
,
we denote by
T_{w,h}\lambda
the
anisotropic

diagram obtained from
\lambda
by replacing each box of size

1\times 1
by a box of size
w\times h
, see
Fig.

3
.
We will be mostly interested in the following scaling:

\Lambda^{(\alpha)}:=T_{\sqrt{\frac{\alpha}{|\lambda|}},\sqrt{\frac{1}{\alpha|\lambda|}}}\lambda,

so that the rescaled partition
\Lambda^{(\alpha)}
has
\alpha
-anisotropic boxes (the ratio between the width and height of a box should be
\alpha
) and the area of
\Lambda^{(\alpha)}
is equal to
1
.
In fact, since we will use this notation only when it is clear that the size of the random partition
\lambda
is a fixed number
d
, we shall prefer the following more detailed notation:

(27)

\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}:=T_{\sqrt{\frac{\alpha}{d}},\sqrt{\frac{1}{\alpha d}}}\lambda,\quad\textrm{if }|\lambda|=d.

In order to state asymptotic results, it is convenient to draw Young diagrams using the
Russian convention
, which is given by changing the usual Cartesian coordinates
(x,y)
into a new coordinate system
(u,v)
given by

u=x-y,\qquad v=x+y.

The boundary of an anisotropic Young diagram
T_{w,h}\lambda

drawn in the Russian convention
is the graph of a function
\omega_{T_{w,h}\lambda}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,
which will be called the
profile
of
T_{w,h}\lambda
, see
Fig.

3
.

Figure 3
.

Young diagram
\lambda=(4,3,1,1)
(left) and
the anisotropic Young diagram

T_{2,\frac{1}{2}}\lambda
(center)
are in the French convention.
The solid line in the rightmost picture is the
profile

\omega_{T_{2,\frac{1}{2}}\lambda}
of

T_{2,\frac{1}{2}}\lambda
, obtained by
switching from the French to the Russian convention.
The local minima
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}
and maxima
y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}
of the profile
are indicated in red and blue, respectively.

Theorem 2.4

(Law of Large Numbers
[
DS19
]
)
.

Assume that
\chi_{d}\colon\mathbb{Y}_{d}\to\mathbb{R}
are Jack characters
such that
(\chi_{d})_{d\geq 1}
fulfills the AFP.
Let
\lambda_{d}
be a random Young diagram with
d
boxes
distributed according to
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}
, and let

\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}
be the associated rescaled diagram.

Then there exists some deterministic function
\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

with the property that

(28)

\lim_{d\to\infty}\omega_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}=\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}},

where the convergence holds true with respect to the supremum norm, in probability.

Theorem 2.5

(Central Limit Theorem
[
DS19
]
)
.

Assume that
\chi_{d}\colon\mathbb{Y}_{d}\to\mathbb{R}
are Jack characters such that
(\chi_{d})_{d\geq 1}
fulfills the enhanced AFP. Consider the sequence of random functions

{\Delta}^{(\alpha)}_{d}:=\sqrt{d}\cdot\frac{\omega_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}-\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}}}{2}

and the observables

\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{d},\,x^{\ell}\rangle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}x^{\ell}\,\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{d}(x)dx,\quad\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}.

Then the joint distribution of the random variables
\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{d},\,x^{\ell}\rangle
,
for
\ell=1,2,\cdots
, converges weakly to a (non-centered) Gaussian distribution.

In other terms, the sequence of random functions
{\Delta}^{(\alpha)}_{d}
converges to some (non-centered) Gaussian random vector
\Delta_{\infty}
valued in the space
(\mathbb{R}[x])^{\prime}
of distributions, the dual
space to polynomials, and the convergence holds in distribution as
d\to\infty
.

Informally, the LLN and CLT above describe the first and second order asymptotics of the profile
\omega_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}
of a large random anisotropic partition:

\omega_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}=\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}}+\frac{2}{\sqrt{d}}\cdot\Delta_{\infty}+o\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)\!,\text{ as $d\to\infty$}.

2.6.
Examples of characters with the AFP and the corresponding probability measures

2.6.1.
Jack–Plancherel measure

Let

(29)

\chi_{d}(\mu):=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if $\mu=(1^{d})$},\\
0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

be the normalized character of the regular representation of the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{d}
.
For
\alpha=1
, the associated probability measure
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha=1)}_{\chi_{d}}

is the celebrated
Plancherel measure
studied in
[
VK77
,
LS77
,
BDJ99
,
BOO00
,
Oko00
,
IO02
]
.
For any
\alpha>0
, one can show (see
[
DS19
]
) that
\chi_{d}
is a Jack character and the corresponding probability measure is the
Jack–Plancherel measure
with formula

\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(\lambda)=\frac{\alpha^{d}\,d!}{j_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}},

where

(30)

j^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}:=\prod_{(i,j)\in\lambda}\big(\alpha(\lambda_{i}-j)+(\lambda_{j}^{\prime}-i)+1\big)\big(\alpha(\lambda_{i}-j)+(\lambda_{j}^{\prime}-i)+\alpha\big),

and
\lambda^{\prime}=(\lambda^{\prime}_{1}\geq\lambda^{\prime}_{2}\geq\cdots)
is the
conjugate
of the partition of
\lambda
, whose Young diagram (in French convention) is obtained from reflecting the Young diagram of
\lambda
across the line
y=x
. The quantity
j^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}
originates from symmetric function theory, see e.g.
Eq.

38
below.

Note that the cumulants associated to the Jack characters
\chi_{d}
are given by

\kappa^{\chi_{d}}_{n}(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})=\begin{cases}1,&\text{when }n=1\text{ and }k_{1}=1,\\
0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

so the sequence
(\chi_{d})_{d\geq 1}
has the enhanced AFP: indeed, all the constants in (
24
) and (
23
) vanish, i.e.
v_{k}=v_{k}^{\prime}=v_{(k|l)}=0
, for all
k,l\geq 2
.
Hence, the theorems from
Section

2.5
, as well as our main theorems from
Section

5
,
can be applied to this example, as long as the asymptotic condition (
10
)
on
\alpha
is satisfied. The regimes of high temperature (
g>0,\,\alpha=\Theta(d)
), low temperature
(
g<0,\,\alpha=\Theta(d^{-1})
), and fixed temperature (
g=0,\,\alpha=\Theta(1)
) are all covered.

2.6.2.
Jack–Schur–Weyl measure

Let
N\in\mathbb{R}
be a real parameter and set

(31)

\chi_{d}(\mu):=N^{-\|\mu\|},

For
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, this is the normalized
\mathfrak{S}_{d}
-character
considered in Schur–Weyl’s construction, where
\mathfrak{S}_{d}
acts naturally on

(\mathbb{C}^{N})^{\otimes d}=\mathbb{C}^{N}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbb{C}^{N}
. Using
[
Mac95
, Ch. VI, (10.25)]

for the value of the principal specialization of a Jack symmetric function, one finds:

(32)

\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(\lambda)=\frac{d!\,\sqrt{\alpha}^{\,d}}{N^{d}\cdot j^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}}\cdot\prod_{(i,j)\in\lambda}\big(N\sqrt{\alpha}+(j-1)\alpha-(i-1)\big).

For
\alpha=1
, the measure
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha=1)}_{\chi_{d}}
is the

Schur–Weyl measure
studied by Biane
[
Bia01
]
, who
proved the LLN for the corresponding large random
Young diagrams and found an explicit formula for the limit shape.
In addition, for any
\alpha>0
, the measure (
32
)
becomes the Jack–Plancherel measure in the limit
N\to\infty
.
Finally, the Jack–Schur–Weyl measure can be obtained as
a limit of the
z
-measures
M^{(d)}_{z,z^{\prime},\theta}

studied by Borodin and Olshanski, namely

\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(\lambda)=\lim_{z^{\prime}\to\infty}M^{(d)}_{N\sqrt{\theta},z^{\prime},\theta}(\lambda),

holds with
\theta=\alpha^{-1}
(see
[
BO05
, Equation (1.1)]
).

We remark that formula (
32
) defines a probability measure
whenever
N\sqrt{\alpha}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\,
or
\,-N/\sqrt{\alpha}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
;
in these cases, each
\chi_{d}
is a Jack character by definition.
If
N\sqrt{\alpha}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
(resp.
-N/\sqrt{\alpha}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
),
then
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}
is supported on Young diagrams
\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}

with
\ell(\lambda)\leq N\sqrt{\alpha}
(resp.
\ell(\lambda^{\prime})\leq-N/\sqrt{\alpha}
).

The functions
\chi_{d}
considered in this example are
multiplicative, i.e. for
\mu^{1},\dots,\mu^{n}\in\mathbb{Y}_{\leq d}

such that
\mu^{1}\cdots\mu^{n}\in\mathbb{Y}_{\leq d}
, one has

\chi_{d}(\mu^{1}\cdots\mu^{n})=\chi_{d}(\mu^{1})\cdots\chi_{d}(\mu^{n})
.
Then all the cumulants
\kappa^{\chi_{d}}_{n}(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})
of order
n\geq 2
vanish,
and in particular (
24
) is satisfied with all
v_{(k|l)}=0
;
then
(\chi_{d})_{d\geq 1}
has the enhanced AFP if (
23
) holds.
As a result, all theorems from
Section

2.5
and
Section

5
can be applied if each
\chi_{d}
is a Jack character, and if (
10
) and
(
23
) are satisfied. We discuss all three possible regimes:

\bullet

High temperature:
Let
g,c>0
, and
w,g^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
be arbitrary parameters.
Find sequences
\alpha=\alpha(d)>0,\ N=N(d)\in\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha}}\cdot\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
such that

N\sqrt{\alpha}=\frac{\sqrt{\alpha d}}{c}+w\sqrt{\alpha}+o(\sqrt{d}),\qquad\sqrt{\alpha}=g\sqrt{d}+g^{\prime}+o(1),\ \text{ as }d\to\infty.

This is possible by first choosing
\alpha
satisfying the second condition,
and then choosing a positive integer
N\sqrt{\alpha}
satisfying the first condition; the latter
amounts to choosing an integer in an interval of length
o(\sqrt{d})
, which is always feasible for large
d
.
With these conditions on
\alpha,N
, the asymptotic relation (
10
)
holds, and moreover

\chi_{d}(k)\ d^{\frac{k-1}{2}}=c^{k-1}-(k-1)wc^{k}\cdot d^{-\frac{1}{2}}+o(d^{-\frac{1}{2}}),

so (
23
) holds too, with
v_{k}=c^{k-1},\,v_{k}^{\prime}=-(k-1)wc^{k}
.

\bullet

Low temperature:
Let
g,c<0
, and
w,g^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
be arbitrary parameters. Find sequences
\alpha=\alpha(d)>0,\ N=N(d)\in-\sqrt{\alpha}\cdot\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
such that

-\frac{N}{\sqrt{\alpha}}=-\frac{\sqrt{d}}{c\sqrt{\alpha}}-\frac{w}{\sqrt{\alpha}}+o(\sqrt{d}),\qquad\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha}}=-g\sqrt{d}-g^{\prime}+o(1),\ \text{ as }d\to\infty.

With these asymptotics on
\alpha,N
, both (
10
) and
(
23
) hold with
v_{k}=c^{k-1},\,v_{k}^{\prime}=-(k-1)wc^{k}
.

\bullet

Fixed temperature:
In this regime, we would like
\alpha(d)=\alpha>0
to be fixed.
Set
g=0,\,g^{\prime}=\sqrt{\alpha}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha}}
,
so that (
10
) holds. Let
c>0,\,w\in\mathbb{R}
be arbitrary parameters.

The enhanced AFP property requires finding
N=N(d)\in\mathbb{R}
such that either
N\sqrt{\alpha}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1},\ N\sqrt{\alpha}=\frac{\sqrt{\alpha d}}{c}+w\sqrt{\alpha}+o(1)
, or
-\frac{N}{\sqrt{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1},\ -\frac{N}{\sqrt{\alpha}}=-\frac{\sqrt{d}}{c\sqrt{\alpha}}-\frac{w}{\sqrt{\alpha}}+o(1)
.
However, it is impossible to choose an integer in an interval of
length
o(1)
. Nevertheless, we can pick
N,\alpha
s.t.
N\sqrt{\alpha}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1},\ N\sqrt{\alpha}=\frac{\sqrt{\alpha d}}{c}+O(1)
, or
-\frac{N}{\sqrt{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1},\ -\frac{N}{\sqrt{\alpha}}=-\frac{\sqrt{d}}{c\sqrt{\alpha}}+O(1)
, which guarantees that the AFP
condition is satisfied with
v_{k}=c^{k-1}
. The LLN from
Theorem

5.1
only requires
the AFP, so we can apply it to our example. The
c
–dependent limit
shape was found by Biane
[
Bia01
]
in the special case
\alpha=1
; it coincides with the celebrated Vershik–Kerov/Logan–Shepp
limit shape in the limit
c\to 0
.

Moreover, the proof of
Theorem

5.2
shows that the weaker
conditions imposed by the AFP guarantee that the
cumulants of order
\geq 3
vanish for the random variables
\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{d},x^{m}\rangle
,
and the limits
\lim_{d\to\infty}{\Cov(\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{d},x^{k-2}\rangle,\,\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{d},x^{l-2}\rangle)}

exist and have explicit combinatorial formulas. The
difference between the AFP and the enhanced AFP only affects the
existence of the limit

\lim_{d\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}[\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{d},x^{m}\rangle]}
, which is not
guaranteed by the weaker assumption of the AFP. Note that the special
case
\alpha=1
discussed in
[
Śni06
]
omits this problem by
proving the CLT for the centered observables
(see
[
Śni06
, Corollary 3.3]
). Our case of a general fixed
\alpha>0

covers this theorem, but we also prove a
stronger result: our choice of
N,\alpha
, e.g. satisfying

N\sqrt{\alpha}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1},\ N\sqrt{\alpha}=\sqrt{\alpha d}/c+O(1)
,
implies that we can restrict to a subsequence
(d_{k})_{k}
such that

N(d_{k})\sqrt{\alpha}=\sqrt{\alpha d_{k}}/c+w\sqrt{\alpha}+o(1)
,
for some fixed
w\in\mathbb{R}
. Then the convergence of the random
function
\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{d}
to the Gaussian process
\Delta_{\infty}

stated in
Theorem

5.2
still holds true but with the sequence

(\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{d})_{d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}}
replaced by
(\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{d_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}}
.

2.6.3.
Conditional Jack–Thoma measure

6
6

6

In connection with this example, see also
Section

3.2.4
and
Definition

3.5
below.

Let
(v_{k})_{k\geq 2}
be any sequence of reals satisfying (
21
),
and for any integer
k\geq 2
, choose a sequence
(v^{(d)}_{k})_{d\geq 1}
such that

(33)

v^{(d)}_{k}\cdot d^{\frac{k-1}{2}}=v_{k}+o(d^{-\frac{1}{2}}),\text{ as }d\to\infty.

Then consider the following function on
\mathbb{Y}_{d}
:

\chi_{d}(\mu):=\prod_{{k}\geq 2}\left(v^{(d)}_{{k}}\right)^{m_{k}(\mu)}.

These functions are multiplicative; together with (
33
), we conclude
that
(\chi_{d})_{d\geq 1}
satisfies the enhanced AFP with
v_{k}^{\prime}=v_{(k|l)}=0
.
We prove in
Proposition

5.3
that the associated measure can be expressed as

\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(\lambda)=\frac{\alpha^{d}\,d!}{u^{d}}\cdot\frac{J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}(u\cdot\mathbf{v}_{u,\alpha})}{j^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}},

where
u>0
is arbitrary, and
J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}(u\cdot\mathbf{v}_{u,\alpha})
is defined as
the image of
J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}
under the specialization (unital algebra homomorphism)

\rho\colon\SSym\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
from the
\mathbb{R}
-algebra of symmetric
functions
\SSym
that maps the power sums
p_{k}\,
to

\,u\cdot(u/\sqrt{\alpha})^{k-1}\cdot v_{k}^{(d)}
.

In order to apply our main results, including those from
Section

5
,
we must impose that
\alpha
satisfies (
10
)
and that each
\chi_{d}
is a Jack character.
One contribution of this paper is the classification of the
totally Jack positive specializations

in
Theorem

4.4
that allows to find large families of examples of parameters

\alpha,\,v_{k}^{(d)}
that fit into this setting.
By
Proposition

4.6
and
Proposition

5.3
,
we are able to consider all three limit regimes as follows.

\bullet

High temperature:
Consider any parameters
g>0
and
a_{1}\geq a_{2}\geq\cdots\geq 0

such that
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{a_{i}}\leq 1
.
Set
\alpha=g^{2}d
, so that (
10
) holds with
g^{\prime}=0
. Also set

(34)

v_{k}^{(d)}=\left(\frac{g\sqrt{d}}{\lceil gd\rceil}\right)^{k-1}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{a_{i}^{k}},\ \text{ for }k\geq 2.

Then
(\chi_{d})_{d\geq 1}
are Jack characters with the enhanced AFP
and
v_{k}=\!\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{a_{i}^{k}}
.

\bullet

Low temperature:
Consider any parameters
g<0
and
a_{1}\geq a_{2}\geq\cdots\geq 0

such that
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{a_{i}}\leq 1
. Set
\alpha=\frac{1}{g^{2}d}
, so that (
10
) holds with
g^{\prime}=0
. Also set

(35)

v_{k}^{(d)}=\left(\frac{g\sqrt{d}}{\lceil-gd\rceil}\right)^{k-1}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{a_{i}^{k}},\ \text{ for }k\geq 2.

Then, due to
Proposition

2.3
,
(\chi_{d})_{d\geq 1}
are Jack characters with the enhanced AFP
and
v_{k}=(-1)^{k-1}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{a_{i}^{k}}
.

\bullet

Fixed temperature:
For this regime,
\alpha>0
is fixed, so

g=0,\,g^{\prime}=\sqrt{\alpha}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha}}
.
We prove in
Proposition

5.3
that

\chi_{d}
is a Jack character whenever

(\sqrt{\alpha},\sqrt{\alpha}\,v_{2}^{(d)},\sqrt{\alpha}\,v_{3}^{(d)},\dots)

determines an
\alpha
-Jack-positive specialization, as in
Definition

4.1
.
Then
Theorem

4.2
, due to
Kerov–Okounkov–Olshanski
[
KOO98
]
,
characterizes the valid sequences
(v_{k}^{(d)})_{k}
.
Take for instance any reals
a_{1}\geq a_{2}\geq\dots\geq 0
and
b_{1}\geq b_{2}\geq\dots\geq 0
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}(a_{i}+b_{i})\leq 1
,
and a sequence of integers
n=n(d)
such that
n\leq\sqrt{\alpha d}

and
n=\sqrt{\alpha d}+O(1)
; then set:

v_{k}^{(d)}:=\frac{n}{\sqrt{\alpha}}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{a_{i}}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k}+\frac{n}{\sqrt{\alpha}}\cdot(-\alpha)^{1-k}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{b_{i}}{\sqrt{d}}\right)^{k},\ \text{ for }k\geq 2.

Then it turns out that all results from
Section

5
are true, except for the existence of the limits

\lim_{d\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}[\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{d},x^{m}\rangle]}
; for that, one must
restrict to subsequences
(d_{k})_{k}
with

n(d_{k})=\sqrt{\alpha d_{k}}+o(1)
,
cf.
Section

2.6.2
and
Remark

6
.
The parameters
v_{k}
are
v_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{a_{i}^{k}}+(-\alpha)^{1-k}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{b_{i}^{k}}
.

Remark 5
.

We point out that the scaling of the random partitions in our LLN is different from that in
[
KOO98
,
Ver74
]
. Indeed, these other articles discuss a LLN in which the parameters
a_{1},a_{2},\cdots
(resp.
b_{1},b_{2},\cdots
) appear as the rates of linear growth of rows (reps. columns) of the random partitions. In contrast, our random partitions
\lambda^{(d)}\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}
are
balanced
in the sense that both the number of rows and columns are asymptotically proportional to
\sqrt{d}
.

Remark 6
.

In general, whenever we replace
the assumption of the enhanced AFP by the (weaker) AFP in

Theorem

5.2
, we have its weaker version which says that the
family of centered random variables
x_{\ell}:=X_{\ell}-\mathbb{E}X_{\ell}
, where

X_{\ell}:=\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{d},x^{\ell}\rangle
, converges jointly to a
Gaussian distribution in the weak topology of probability
measures. The special case
\alpha=1
recovers the result of
Śniady
[
Śni06
]
. Similarly, by replacing
(
23
) in the definition of the
enhanced AFP by a weaker condition
\chi_{d}(k)\ d^{\frac{k-1}{2}}=v_{k}+O(d^{-\frac{1}{2}})

that we encountered in all our examples, then
Theorem

5.2
holds
true after restricting to a subsequence.

3.
Second model of random Young diagrams: Jack measures

In this section, we review the construction from
[
Mol23
]
that yields
probability measures on the infinite set
\mathbb{Y}:=\bigsqcup_{d\geq 0}\mathbb{Y}_{d}
of all
Young diagrams and it relies on the Cauchy identity for Jack symmetric functions;
the result is a natural
1
-parameter generalization of Okounkov’s Schur measures
[
Oko01
]
.
We also isolate a special family of Jack measures, to be called

Jack–Thoma measures
, that are closely related to the
model of random Young diagrams discusssed in
Section

2
,
and to the work of Kerov–Okounkov–Olshanski
[
KOO98
]
.
We prove the LLN and CLT for this new family of measures.

3.1.
Definitions and preliminaries

Recall the Cauchy identity for Jack symmetric functions
[
Mac95
, Ch. VI.10]
:

(36)

1+\sum_{d=1}^{\infty}{t^{d}\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}}{\frac{J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}(\mathbf{p})J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}(\mathbf{q})}{\|J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}\|^{2}}}}=1+\sum_{d=1}^{\infty}{t^{d}\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}}{\frac{p_{\mu}q_{\mu}}{\alpha^{\ell(\mu)}z_{\mu}}}}=\exp\bigg(\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{t^{k}p_{k}q_{k}}{{k}\alpha}\bigg),

where
\mathbf{p}=(p_{1},p_{2},\dots)
and
\mathbf{q}=(q_{1},q_{2},\dots)
are two
independent families of variables which play the role of power-sums.
We employed the usual notations:
p_{\mu}:=\prod_{i\geq 1}{p_{\mu_{i}}}
,

J_{\lambda}(\mathbf{p}):=\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}}\theta^{(\alpha)}_{\mu}(\lambda)p_{\mu}
,
and
q_{\mu},J_{\lambda}(\mathbf{q})
are defined similarly.The norm
\|\cdot\|
in the LHS of (
36
) is with respect to the
\alpha
-deformed Hall scalar product, defined on the
\mathbb{R}
-algebra of symmetric functions
\SSym
by declaring
the basis
\{p_{\mu}\}_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}}
to be orthogonal, and

(37)

\|p_{\mu}\|^{2}=\alpha^{\ell(\mu)}z_{\mu},\quad\forall\,\mu\in\mathbb{Y}.

It is known that the Jack symmetric functions
J^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}
are orthogonal and have norms

(38)

\|J^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}\|^{2}=j^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda},

where
j^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}
is given in (
30
).
Note that
j^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}[\alpha]
, in particular
j^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}>0
, whenever
\alpha>0
.

Definition 3.1

(Second model of random Young diagrams: Jack measures)
.

Given a fixed
\alpha>0
, let
\rho_{1},~\rho_{2}:\Symm\to\mathbb{C}
be any two
unital
\mathbb{R}
-algebra homomorphisms such that

(39)

\displaystyle\rho_{1}\left(J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}\right)\overline{\rho_{2}\left(J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}\right)}

\displaystyle\geq 0,\text{ for all }\lambda\in\mathbb{Y},

(40)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\rho_{1}(p_{k})\overline{\rho_{2}(p_{k})}}{k}}

\displaystyle<\infty.

Then define the
Jack measure

M^{(\alpha)}_{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}
as the
probability measure on
\mathbb{Y}
given by

(41)

M_{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}^{(\alpha)}(\lambda):=\frac{\rho_{1}\left(J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}\right)\overline{\rho_{2}\left(J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}\right)}}{j^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}}\exp\left(-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\frac{\rho_{1}(p_{k})\overline{\rho_{2}(p_{k})}}{k\alpha}}\right),\quad\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}.

The normalization
\,\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}}{M^{(\alpha)}_{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}(\lambda)}=1

follows from (
36
), while the
nonnegativity
M^{(\alpha)}_{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}(\lambda)\geq 0
is a direct
consequence of the assumptions (
39
), (
40
).

3.2.
Examples of Jack measures

3.2.1.
Principal series Jack measures

The measure
M_{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}^{(\alpha)}
was studied by the
third author in the special case when
\rho:=\rho_{1}=\rho_{2}
,
see
[
Mol23
]
. Note that (
39
) is automatically satisfied
when the specializations
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
are equal.
The main results of
[
Mol23
]
are the LLN and CLT for
M_{\rho,\rho}^{(\alpha)}

under a natural assumption that guarantees (
40
), namely

|\rho(p_{k})|\leq A\cdot r^{k}
, for all
{k}\geq{1}
, and some constants
A>0,\,r<1
.

3.2.2.
Poissonized Jack–Plancherel measures

Let us look at the special case of
Section

3.2.1
when
\rho(p_{k})=u\cdot\delta_{1,k}
, for some
u\in\mathbb{C}
, and
\delta_{1,k}
is the Kronecker delta.
This is the so-called
Plancherel specialization
.
From the fact that the coefficient of
p_{1}^{|\lambda|}
in the expansion of
J^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}
is
1
(see
[
Mac95
, Ch. VI, (10.29)]
), one finds the explicit formula

M_{\rho,\rho}^{(\alpha)}(\lambda)=\exp\left(-\frac{|u|^{2}}{\alpha}\right)\frac{|u|^{2|\lambda|}}{j^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}}.

Observe that
M_{\rho,\rho}^{(\alpha)}
is
the Poissonization of the Jack–Plancherel measures from

Section

2.6.1
with the Poisson parameter equal to

\frac{|u|^{2}}{\alpha}
. In other words,

M^{(\alpha)}_{\rho,\rho}(\lambda)=\exp\left(-\frac{|u|^{2}}{\alpha}\right)\frac{|u|^{2d}}{\alpha^{d}d!}\cdot\mathbb{P}_{\chi_{d}}^{(\alpha)}(\lambda),\quad\forall\,\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}_{d},\ d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},

where
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}
is the probability measure associated
with the character
\chi_{d}
from
Section

2.6.1
.

3.2.3.
Poissonized Jack–Schur–Weyl measures

Consider a generalization of the previous example, when

\rho_{1}
is the
principal specialization

\rho_{1}(p_{k})=u\cdot c^{k-1}
, while
\rho_{2}
is still the Plancherel specialization
\rho_{2}(p_{k})=u\cdot\delta_{1,k}
.
This example is not an instance of
Section

3.2.1
, but
it clearly satisfies (
40
).
One can also check that (
39
) is satisfied whenever
u\in\mathbb{R},c\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
, and

u/c\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
or
-u/(c\alpha)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.
Indeed, by
[
Mac95
, Ch. VI, (10.25)]
, one finds

M_{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}^{(\alpha)}(\lambda)=\exp\left(-\frac{u^{2}}{\alpha}\right)\cdot\frac{(uc)^{|\lambda|}}{j_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}}\cdot\prod_{(i,j)\in\lambda}{\left(\frac{u}{c}+\alpha(j-1)-(i-1)\right)}.

If
u/c\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
(resp.
-u/(c\alpha)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
), then

M_{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}^{(\alpha)}
is supported on partitions
\lambda

with
\ell(\lambda)\leq u/c
(resp.
\ell(\lambda^{\prime})\leq-u/(c\alpha)
).
Moreover,
M_{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}^{(\alpha)}
is the
Poissonization of the Jack–Schur–Weyl measures
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}
from

Section

2.6.2
with
N=\frac{u}{c\sqrt{\alpha}}
and
Poisson parameter
\frac{u^{2}}{\alpha}
.

3.2.4.
Jack–Thoma measures

7
7

7

See also
Definition

3.5
below.

The example from
Section

3.2.3
is a special case of the following general framework
that will be the main object of interest later in this section. Let
\mathbf{v}=(v_{1},v_{2},\cdots)\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}

and
u>0
. Define
M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}
as the Jack measure

M^{(\alpha)}_{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}
with specializations

\rho_{1}(p_{k})=u\cdot v_{k}
and
\rho_{2}(p_{k})=u\cdot\delta_{1,k}
.
If we denote
u\cdot\mathbf{v}:=(u\cdot v_{1},u\cdot v_{2},\cdots)
and

J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}(u\cdot\mathbf{v}):=\rho(J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)})
, for the specialization
\rho(p_{k}):=u\cdot v_{k}
, then

M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}(\lambda)=\exp\left(-\frac{u^{2}\cdot v_{1}}{\alpha}\right)\frac{J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}(u\cdot\mathbf{v})\cdot u^{|\lambda|}}{j^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}}.

This is a probability measure if and only if

J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}(u\cdot\mathbf{v})\geq 0
, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}
.
We find in
Section

4
some choices of
u,\mathbf{v}
that imply this positivity assumption,
see e.g.
Proposition

4.6
.
We prove in
Proposition

5.3
that if
v_{1}=1
, the measure
M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}
is the Poissonization of the measures
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}
from
Footnote

6
with Poisson parameter
\frac{u^{2}}{\alpha}
.

3.3.
Transition measures

Given any Young diagram
\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}
, we use Kerov’s idea
[
Ker93a
]
to
associate to
\lambda
a probability measure
\mu_{\lambda}
on
\mathbb{R}
,
called the
transition measure of
\lambda
; it is characterized by the following identity for its Cauchy transform:

(42)

G_{\mu_{\lambda}}(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{d\mu_{\lambda}(x)}{z-x}=\frac{1}{z+\ell(\lambda)}\prod_{i=1}^{\ell(\lambda)}\frac{z+i-\lambda_{i}}{z+i-1-\lambda_{i}}=\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{k}(z-y_{i})}{\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}(z-x_{i})},

where
x_{1},\dots,x_{k+1}
(
y_{1},\dots,y_{k}
, respectively) are the local
minima (maxima, respectively) of the profile
\omega_{\lambda}
, see
Fig.

3
.
More generally, for any interlacing sequence
x_{1}<y_{1}<x_{2}<\cdots<y_{k}<x_{k+1}
of real numbers, there exists a unique probability measure whose Cauchy transform is the rational function given by the RHS of (
42
) and, in particular, it has mean
\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}{x_{i}}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}{y_{i}}
.
This allows us to define the transition measure
\mu_{T_{w,h}\lambda}
of any anisotropic diagram
T_{w,h}\lambda
by the same equation (
42
), where the
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
are the local minima and maxima of
T_{w,h}\lambda
, respectively.
Note that the mean of any transition measure is zero, since
\sum_{i=1}^{k+1}{x_{i}}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}{y_{i}}
.

Going even further, for any infinite interlacing sequence of real numbers
x_{1}<y_{1}<x_{2}<y_{2}<\cdots
, or
x_{1}>y_{1}>x_{2}>y_{2}>\cdots
, where we assume that
\lim_{k\to\infty}|y_{k}|=\infty
, and

(43)

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{x_{i+1}-y_{i}}{y_{i}}<\infty

(we remove from the sum the term corresponding to
y_{i}=0
, if it exists), one can show that (see
[
Ker98
, Section 1.3 and Section 2.1]
):

•

the infinite product

G(z):=\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{z-y_{i}}{z-x_{i}}

converges for
z\neq x_{i}
,
i=1,2\dots
, the zeroes of
G(z)
are located at
y_{1},y_{2},\dots
, and its poles are located at
x_{1},x_{2},\dots
;

•

the partial fraction decomposition

G(z)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{i}}{z-x_{i}}

is such that
\mu_{i}>0
, for all
i\geq 1
, and
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{\mu_{i}}=1
.
As a result,

(44)

\mu:=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mu_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}

defines a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}
whose Cauchy transform is precisely
G_{\mu}(z)=G(z)
.

3.4.
Observables of measures with all finite moments

We will use several sequences of observables on the set of probability measures on
\mathbb{R}
with finite moments of all orders.
Besides the
moments
, we will use the
Boolean cumulants
, the
free cumulants
and the
fundamental functionals of shape
.
If
\mu
is a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}
with finite moments of all orders, then let us denote its
\ell
-th moment by
M^{\mu}_{\ell}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}{x^{\ell}\mu(dx)}
.
The sequence of moments
(M^{\mu}_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}
can also be described by expanding
the Cauchy transform of
\mu
in a neighborhood of infinity:

(45)

G_{\mu}(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{d\mu(x)}{z-x}=\frac{1}{z}+\sum_{\ell\geq 1}M^{\mu}_{\ell}z^{-\ell-1}.

The associated sequences of Boolean cumulants
(B^{\mu}_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}
, free cumulants
(R^{\mu}_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}
, and fundamental functionals of shape
(S^{\mu}_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}
can be defined by the following relations between their generating functions:

(46)

\displaystyle B_{\mu}(z)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\ell\geq 1}B^{\mu}_{\ell}\,z^{-\ell+1}=z-\frac{1}{G_{\mu}(z)},

(47)

\displaystyle K_{\mu}(z)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\ell\geq 1}R^{\mu}_{\ell}\,z^{\ell-1}=G^{-1}_{\mu}(z)-\frac{1}{z},

(48)

\displaystyle S_{\mu}(z)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\ell\geq 1}S^{\mu}_{\ell}\,z^{-\ell}=\log\left(zG_{\mu}(z)\right),

where
G^{-1}_{\mu}(z)
denotes the (formal) compositional inverse
of
G_{\mu}
. Observe that the first elements in these sequences
of observables are the same:
B^{\mu}_{1}=R^{\mu}_{1}=S^{\mu}_{1}=M^{\mu}_{1}
.

Proposition 3.2
.

For any integer
\ell\geq 1
,

(49)

\displaystyle M^{\mu}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\geq 1}\sum_{k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\geq 1\atop k_{1}+\cdots k_{n}=\ell}B^{\mu}_{k_{1}}\cdots B^{\mu}_{k_{n}},

(50)

\displaystyle S^{\mu}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\geq 1\atop k_{1}+\cdots k_{n}=\ell}B^{\mu}_{k_{1}}\cdots B^{\mu}_{k_{n}}.

Proof.

In order to prove (
49
), we use (
46
):

M^{\mu}_{\ell}=[z^{-\ell-1}]G_{\mu}(z)=[z^{-\ell-1}]\frac{1}{z-B_{\mu}(z)}=[z^{-\ell}]\frac{1}{1-z^{-1}B_{\mu}(z)}=[z^{-\ell}]\sum_{n\geq 1}(z^{-1}B_{\mu}(z))^{n},

which equals the right hand side of (
49
). Similarly, to prove (
50
), we use (
46
) and (
48
) to get

S^{\mu}_{\ell}=[z^{-\ell}]\log\left(zG_{\mu}(z)\right)=[z^{-\ell}]\log\bigg(\frac{1}{1-z^{-1}B_{\mu}(z)}\bigg)=[z^{-\ell}]\sum_{n\geq 1}{\frac{1}{n}(z^{-1}B_{\mu}(z))^{n}},

which equals the right hand side of (
50
).
∎

The moments, Boolean cumulants, free cumulants and fundamental functionals of shape associated with a transition measure
\mu_{\lambda}
will be denoted by
M_{\ell}(\lambda)
,
B_{\ell}(\lambda)
,
R_{\ell}(\lambda)
,
S_{\ell}(\lambda)
, respectively, and we think of them as functions on the set of Young diagrams.
We will also use the analogous notations if the Young diagram
\lambda
is replaced by an anisotropic diagram
T_{w,h}\lambda
.
We remark that, for all
X=M,B,R,S
, and all anisotropic diagrams
T_{w,h}\lambda
, we have

(51)

X_{1}(T_{w,h}\lambda)=0,\qquad X_{2}(T_{w,h}\lambda)=wh|\lambda|;

see
[
Ker93b
, Sec. 2.6]
for more details.
Finally, we have the following
scaling property
:

(52)

X_{\ell}(T_{cw,ch}\lambda)=c^{\ell}X_{\ell}(T_{w,h}\lambda),\textrm{ for all }c>0\textrm{ and }\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}.

In this section, we will be interested in the following scaling of random partitions:

(53)

\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)}:=T_{\frac{\alpha}{u},\frac{1}{u}}\lambda,

for some
u>0
; note that this rescaled partition has boxes whose ratio of dimensions is
\alpha
.
The parameter
u
will be chosen later, depending on the random partition ensemble, in such a way that the area of the rescaled partition
\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)}
is equal to
1
.
With this new notation, the scaling property implies in particular that:

(54)
