X_{\ell}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})=u^{-\ell}X_{\ell}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;1)}).

3.5.
The Markov–Krein correspondence

The relation between the profile
\omega_{\Lambda}
and the transition measure

\mu_{\Lambda}
of an anisotropic diagram
\Lambda
is given by (
42
) and this can be restated in the following form, known as the
Markov–Krein correspondence
:

(55)

\frac{1}{z}\exp\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{x-z}\left(\frac{\omega_{\Lambda}(x)-|x|}{2}\right)^{\prime}dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{d\mu_{\Lambda}(x)}{z-x}.

Applying (
48
) to the above equation, we obtain the following formula for the fundamental functional of shape:

(56)

S_{n}(\Lambda)=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}x^{n-1}\left(\frac{\omega_{\Lambda}(x)-|x|}{2}\right)^{\prime}dx=(n-1)\int_{\mathbb{R}}x^{n-2}\left(\frac{\omega_{\Lambda}(x)-|x|}{2}\right)dx.

The Markov--Krein correspondence works in a much wider generality, and we briefly state it here in the most general framework that we are aware of.
8
8

8

Typically the Markov–Krein correspondence is stated for probability measures with compact support, e.g.
[
Ker93a
]
. We follow the more general framework from
[
M1́7
]
, which is based on Kerov’s work
[
Ker98
]

We call
generalized continuous Young diagram
a continuous function

\omega\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
with Lipschitz constant
1
and such that
the following integrals converge:

(57)

\int_{-\infty}^{0}\frac{1+\omega^{\prime}(x)}{1-x}dx<\infty;\ \ \ \ \int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1-\omega^{\prime}(x)}{1+x}dx<\infty.

For instance, if
\omega(x)
is a zig-zag line with slopes
\pm 1
, such that
\omega(x)=-x
whenever
x\ll 0
, and whose local minima and maxima are achieved at points in the interlacing sequence
x_{1}<y_{1}<x_{2}<y_{2}<\cdots
, then the condition (
57
) is equivalent to the condition (
43
) (see
[
Ker98
, Section 1.3]
).

We denote by
\mathbb{Y}^{1}
the set of equivalence classes of generalized
continuous Young diagrams, where
\omega_{1}\sim\omega_{2}\iff\omega_{1}^{\prime}\equiv\omega_{2}^{\prime}
. The space of generalized continuous Young diagrams
\mathbb{Y}^{1}

is a Polish space (separable and metrizable complete space) with the
following topology: we say that
\omega_{n}\to\omega
if the following integrals are uniformly bounded:

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\left(\int_{-\infty}^{0}\frac{1+\omega_{n}^{\prime}(x)}{1-x}dx\right)<\infty;\ \ \ \ \sup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1-\omega_{n}^{\prime}(x)}{1+x}dx\right)<\infty,

and there exist representatives
\omega_{n}(x)
and

\omega(x)
such that for any
N>0
one
has
\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\omega_{n}-\omega\|_{L^{\infty}([-N,N])}=0
.

We define two other Polish spaces (see
[
M1́7
]
for the details):
\mathscr{M}^{1}
the space of
probability measures on the real line
\mathbb{R}
with the topology of weak
convergence and
\mathscr{N}^{1}
the space of holomorphic functions
h

on the Poincáre half-space

\mathbb{H}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\colon\Im(z)>0\}

which take values with negative imaginary parts and such that
\lim_{y\to+\infty}\mathbf{i}yh(\mathbf{i}y)=1
if
\mathbf{i}=\sqrt{-1}
(the topology is given by the proper convergence
i.e.
h_{n}\to h
if
h_{n}
converges uniformly to
h
on all compact
subsets of
\mathbb{H}
and

\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\atop y\geq N}|\mathbf{i}yh_{n}(\mathbf{i}y)-1|=0).

Then, the following Markov–Krein correspondence holds true.

Theorem 3.3
.

[
M1́7
, Theorem 7.25]

There is a sequence of homeomorphisms

\omega\in\mathbb{Y}^{1}\leftrightarrow G_{\omega}\in\mathscr{N}^{1}\leftrightarrow\mu_{\omega}\in\mathscr{M}^{1}

given by:

(58)

G_{\omega}(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{d\mu_{\omega}(x)}{z-x}=\frac{1}{z}\exp\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{x-z}\left(\frac{\omega(x)-|x|}{2}\right)^{\prime}dx.

Next, we will study the typical shape of the rescaled diagram
\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)}
(recall the rescaling (
53
)) when
\lambda
is sampled from the Jack–Thoma measure, and the Poisson parameter tends to infinity.
We prove that in various regimes such a typical shape
\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}
exists, the fluctuations around this shape are Gaussian and we describe the limit shape via the Markov–Krein correspondence (we give an explicit combinatorial formula for the associated Cauchy transform in terms of certain lattice paths).

3.6.
Łukasiewicz paths and Łukasiewicz ribbon paths

An
excursion

\Gamma
of length
\ell
is a sequence of points
\Gamma=(w_{0},\dots,w_{\ell})
on
(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{2}
such that
w_{j}=(j,y_{j})
, with
y_{0}=y_{\ell}=0
,
y_{1},\dots,y_{\ell-1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and it is uniquely
encoded by the sequence of its
steps

e_{j}:=w_{j}-w_{j-1}=(1,y_{j}-y_{j-1})
. Informally speaking, an excursion is a directed lattice path
with steps of the form
(1,k),k\in\mathbb{Z}
, starting at

(0,0)
, finishing at
(\ell,0)
, and that stays in the first
quadrant. A step
e=(1,0)
is called a
horizontal step
, while
steps
e=(1,k),\,k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
(resp.
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{<0}
), are
called
up steps
(resp.
down steps
)
of degree
k
,
see
Fig.

4
for examples.

For a given excursion
\Gamma=(w_{0},\dots,w_{\ell})
, the set of
points
\mathbf{S}(\Gamma):=\{w_{1},w_{2},\dots,w_{\ell}\}
(not counting the origin
w_{0}=(0,0)
)
naturally decomposes into pairwise disjoint subsets
consisting of those points which are preceded by a horizontal, down step or up step:

\mathbf{S}(\Gamma)=\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\Gamma)\cup\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\searrow}(\Gamma)\cup\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\nearrow}(\Gamma).

Denote by
\mathbf{S}_{n}(\Gamma)
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
,
the set of points preceded by a step
(1,n)
, i.e.
\mathbf{S}_{n}(\Gamma)

is the set of points preceded by an up step
of degree
n
, if
n>0
, or by a down step of degree
-n
, if
n<0
.
Also denote by
\mathbf{S}^{i}(\Gamma)
the subset of points with second coordinate equal to
i
, i.e.

\mathbf{S}^{i}(\Gamma):=\{w_{j}=(j,y_{j})\in\mathbf{S}(\Gamma)\,\mid\,y_{j}=i\}.

We say that points
w_{j}\in\mathbf{S}^{i}(\Gamma)
are at
height
i
.
The set
\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma)
will play an especially prominent role
(e.g. see
Theorem

3.8
below);
note that the origin
w_{0}=(0,0)
does not belong to
\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma)
.

Define the sets
\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\Gamma),\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\searrow}(\Gamma),\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\nearrow}(\Gamma),\mathbf{S}^{i}_{n}(\Gamma)
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
, analogously.

Figure 4
.
Four excursions
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\Gamma_{3}
and

\Gamma_{4}
. Note that
\Gamma_{3}
and
\Gamma_{4}
are Łukasiewicz paths,
but
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
are not.

Summing up, we have the following natural decompositions into disjoint subsets:

\mathbf{S}(\Gamma)=\bigcup_{i=0}^{\infty}\,\Bigl\{\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\Gamma)\cup\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\searrow}(\Gamma)\cup\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\nearrow}(\Gamma)\Bigr\}=\bigcup_{i=0}^{\infty}\,\Bigl\{\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\Gamma)\cup\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\bigl(\mathbf{S}^{i}_{-n}(\Gamma)\cup\mathbf{S}^{i}_{n}(\Gamma)\bigr)\Bigr\}.

For an ordered tuple
\vec{\Gamma}=(\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{k})
of
k
excursions

\Gamma_{i}
, we will treat
\vec{\Gamma}
itself as the excursion
obtained by concatenating
\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{k}
.
We define
\mathbf{S}(\vec{\Gamma}),\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\rightarrow}^{i}(\vec{\Gamma}),\mathbf{S}_{n}^{i}(\vec{\Gamma})
, etc. in the same way as before, by treating

\vec{\Gamma}
as the excursion obtained from the concatenation of
\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{k}
.
For example, if
\vec{\Gamma}=(w_{0},w_{1},\dots,w_{\ell})
, then

\mathbf{S}(\vec{\Gamma})=\{w_{1},\dots,w_{\ell}\}
.
We say that
p=(w_{i},w_{j})
is a pairing of degree
n>0
if
w_{i}\in\mathbf{S}_{-n}(\vec{\Gamma}),w_{j}\in\mathbf{S}_{n}(\vec{\Gamma})
, and
w_{i}
appears
before
w_{j}
in
\vec{\Gamma}
, i.e.
i<j
.
See
Fig.

4
and
Fig.

5
for an example.

Figure 5
.
A ribbon path
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2},\Gamma_{3},\Gamma_{4})
on
4
sites of lengths
8,7,4,5
, which is also a Łukasiewicz ribbon path belonging to
\mathbf{L}(8,7,4,5)
.
The three pairs of dots of different colors (red, green and blue)
are examples of pairings, and the colored arcs join
the pairs of preceding steps of the same degree. The degrees of the red, green and blue
pairings are
4
,
2
and
2
, respectively.

By definition, a
ribbon path on
k
sites of lengths
\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{k}

is a pair
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}=(\vec{\Gamma},\,\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}))
consisting of an ordered tuple

\vec{\Gamma}
of
k
excursions
\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{k}
of lengths
\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{k}
,
respectively, and a set
\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})
of pairwise disjoint
pairings
p_{1},\dots,p_{q}
on
\vec{\Gamma}
. This notion was first introduced by the third author in
[
Mol23
]
. We denote by
\mathbf{P}_{n}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})\subset\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})

the subset of pairings of degree
n
.
For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
, define

\mathbf{S}_{n}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}):=\mathbf{S}_{n}(\vec{\Gamma})\setminus\mathbf{P}_{|n|}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})

as the set obtained by removing the points belonging to
\mathbf{P}_{|n|}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})
from

\mathbf{S}_{n}(\vec{\Gamma})
; likewise,
\mathbf{S}_{n}^{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}):=\mathbf{S}_{n}^{i}(\vec{\Gamma})\setminus\mathbf{P}_{|n|}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})
.
Finally, define
\mathbf{S}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}):=\mathbf{S}(\vec{\Gamma})
and
\mathbf{S}_{\to}^{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}):=\mathbf{S}_{\to}^{i}(\vec{\Gamma})
.
Then we have the following decomposition:
9
9

9

In this paragraph,
we abused the notation: the set of pairings
\mathbf{P}_{n}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})
contains pairs of
distinct points
(w_{i},w_{j})
, but we implicitly treated such pairs as the 2-element sets
\{w_{i},w_{j}\}
, for simplicity of notation.

(59)

\mathbf{S}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbf{P}_{n}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})\cup\bigcup_{i=0}^{\infty}\,\Bigl\{\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})\cup\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\bigl(\mathbf{S}^{i}_{-n}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})\cup\mathbf{S}^{i}_{n}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})\bigr)\Bigr\}.

Example 2
.

The decomposition (
59
) for the ribbon path
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}
from
Fig.

5
is given by:

\displaystyle\mathbf{P}_{2}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\bigl((4,2),(9,2)\bigr),\bigl((10,0),(11,2)\bigr)\bigr\},\quad\mathbf{P}_{4}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})=\bigl\{\bigl((6,1),(20,4)\bigr)\bigr\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}^{0}_{-1}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})

\displaystyle=\{(8,0),(15,0),(19,0),(24,0)\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}^{1}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})

\displaystyle=\{(7,1),(17,1),(18,1)\},\quad\mathbf{S}^{1}_{-1}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})=\{(14,1),(23,1)\},\quad\mathbf{S}^{1}_{1}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})=\{(16,1)\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}^{2}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})

\displaystyle=\{(12,2),(13,2)\},\quad\mathbf{S}^{2}_{-1}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})=\{(2,2),(22,2)\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{S}^{3}_{-1}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})

\displaystyle=\{(21,3)\},\quad\mathbf{S}^{3}_{3}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})=\{(1,3)\},\quad\mathbf{S}^{4}_{2}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})=\{(3,4)\},\quad\mathbf{S}^{5}_{3}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})=\{(5,5)\},

and the remaining sets in the decomposition from
Eq.

59
are
empty.

Definition 3.4

(Łukasiewicz paths and Łukasiewicz ribbon paths)
.

(i) An excursion
\Gamma
without horizontal steps at height
0

and whose down steps all have degree
1

is called a
Łukasiewicz path
.
In symbols, if
\mathbf{S}^{0}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\Gamma)=\mathbf{S}_{-n}(\Gamma)=\emptyset
, for all
n\geq 2
, then
\Gamma
is a Łukasiewicz path.
We denote
\mathbf{L}_{0}(\ell)
the set of Łukasiewicz paths of length
\ell
.

(ii) A ribbon path
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}
without horizontal steps at height
0
and
whose non-paired down steps all have degree
1
is called
a
Łukasiewicz ribbon path
.
In other words, if
\mathbf{S}^{0}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})=\mathbf{S}_{-n}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})=\emptyset
, for all
n\geq 2
, then
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}

is a Łukasiewicz ribbon path.
We denote
\mathbf{L}(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{k})
the set of
Łukasiewicz ribbon paths on
k
sites of lengths
\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{k}
.

Łukasiewicz paths are classical objects in combinatorics, see e.g.
[
FS09
]
,
but observe that, contrary to the literature, our definition
does not allow horizontal steps at height
0
.

3.7.
The LLN and CLT for Jack–Thoma measures

From
Section

3.2.4
, recall:

Definition 3.5
.

Let
\alpha>0
,
u>0
,
\mathbf{v}=(v_{1},v_{2},\cdots)\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
be such that
J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}(u\cdot\mathbf{v})\geq 0
, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}
. Then the
Jack–Thoma measure
, denoted by
M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}
, is the probability measure on the set of all Young
diagrams
\mathbb{Y}
defined by

(60)

M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}(\lambda):=\exp\left(-\frac{u^{2}\cdot v_{1}}{\alpha}\right)\frac{J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}(u\cdot\mathbf{v})\cdot u^{|\lambda|}}{j^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}},\quad\forall\,\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}.

Recall that
j^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}
is the quantity from
Eq.

30
.

Remark 7
.

For any fixed
\alpha>0
, the parameters
u,\mathbf{v}
, such that
J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}(u\cdot\mathbf{v})\geq 0
, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}
, are classified by
Theorem

4.2
, due to Kerov–Okounkov–Olshanski.
This theorem generalizes Thoma’s classification of extremal characters
of the infinite symmetric group
[
Tho64
]
, which is equivalent to the
\alpha=1
case;
this connection to Thoma’s theorem motivated the name of the measures
M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}
.

Example 3
.

As
M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}
is a special case Jack measure, we will be able to calculate various statistics of
M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}
-random partitions by known techniques; see
Section

3.8
.
For example, the simplest equation coming from
Theorem

3.10
shows that the expected value of
B_{2}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})
, where
\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)}=T_{\frac{\alpha}{u},\frac{1}{u}}\lambda
and
\lambda
is
M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}
-distributed, is equal to
v_{1}
.
Since
B_{2}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})=\frac{\alpha|\lambda|}{u^{2}}
, due to (
51
), it follows that the expected value of the size of a
M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}
-distributed random partition
\lambda
is equal to:

\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\big[|\lambda|\big]=\frac{u^{2}v_{1}}{\alpha}.

From now on, let us assume in this section that
(\alpha(d))_{d\geq 1}
,
(u(d))_{d\geq 1}
are
sequences of positive real numbers. Let
\alpha
and
u
denote
the general
d
-dependent terms in the corresponding sequences:
\alpha=\alpha(d),\,u=u(d)
. We will impose the following assumption:

Assumption 3.6

(Assumptions on parameters
g,g^{\prime},\mathbf{v}
, and sequences
\alpha(d),\,u(d)
)
.

The sequences
(\alpha(d))_{d\geq 1}
,
(u(d))_{d\geq 1}
of positive reals and the infinite tuple
\mathbf{v}=(v_{1},v_{2},\cdots)\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
, with
v_{1}>0
, are such that

M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}(\lambda)\geq 0
, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{Y},\,d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.
Moreover, assume that there exist
g,g^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
such that one of the following two asymptotic conditions holds:

(i) Weak version:

(61)

\frac{\alpha}{u^{2}}=o(1),\quad\frac{\alpha-1}{u}=g+o(1),\quad\text{as }d\to\infty.

(ii) Refined version:

(62)

\frac{\alpha}{u^{2}}=\frac{1}{d}+o\left(\frac{1}{d}\right),\quad\frac{\alpha-1}{u}=g+\frac{g^{\prime}}{\sqrt{d}}+o\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right),\quad\text{as }d\to\infty.

Remark 8
.

The weak version of
3.6
is the minimal requirement needed for the LLN in
Theorem

3.7
below, while the refined version is needed for the CLT in
Theorem

3.8
. These conditions should be compared to the AFP and enhanced AFP from
Definition

2.2
.

In the next section, we investigate the existence of fixed parameters
g,g^{\prime},\mathbf{v}
and
sequences
\alpha(d),\,u(d)
satisfying the refined version of
3.6
, see e.g.
Proposition

4.6
.

Our main theorems in this section study the limits of the rescaled random Young diagrams
\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}
, given by (
53
) and sampled w.r.t. the Jack–Thoma measures, as
d\to\infty
.
Let us mention that the choice of scaling is so that the area of the rescaled Young diagram
\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}
is equal to
1
, as seen from
Example

3
.
Moreover, note that the area of a rescaled box in
\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}
equals
\,\frac{\alpha}{u^{2}v_{1}}
,
whereas the difference between the box’s width and height is
\,\frac{\alpha-1}{u\sqrt{v_{1}}}
; these points show that
3.6
is geometrically motivated.
The following LLN and CLT are detailed versions of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
from the introduction.

Theorem 3.7

(Law of Large Numbers)
.

Fix
g\in\mathbb{R},\,\mathbf{v}=(v_{1},v_{2},\cdots)\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
, and suppose that the weak version of
3.6
is satisfied.
Then there exists a deterministic function
\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that

(63)

\lim_{d\to\infty}\omega_{\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}}=\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}},

with respect to the supremum norm, in probability.
In other words, for each
\epsilon>0
,

\lim_{d\to\infty}M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\big(\|\omega_{\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}}-\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}\|_{\infty}>\epsilon\big)=0.

The associated transition measure (via the Markov–Krein correspondence)
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}:=\mu_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}
is the unique probability measure on
\mathbb{R}
with moments given by:

(64)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}{x^{\ell}\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}(dx)}=v_{1}^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\cdot\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathbf{L}_{0}(\ell)}\,\prod_{i\geq 1}(i\cdot g)^{|\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\rightarrow}^{i}(\Gamma)|}\,v_{i}^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\Gamma)|},\quad\forall\,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}.

Theorem 3.8

(Central Limit Theorem)
.

Fix
g,g^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R},\,\mathbf{v}=(v_{1},v_{2},\cdots)\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
with
v_{1}>0
, and suppose that the refined version of
3.6
is satisfied.
Consider the sequence of random functions

\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}:=\frac{u}{\sqrt{\alpha}}\bigg(\frac{\omega_{\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}}-\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}}{2}\bigg).

and the polynomial observables
\displaystyle\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}},\,x^{\ell}\rangle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}{x^{\ell}\,\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}(x)dx}
, for
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
Then the joint distribution of the random variables

\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}},\,x^{\ell}\rangle
, for
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, converges weakly, as
d\to\infty
, to a Gaussian distribution with mean vector and covariance matrix given by the following explicit polynomials in
g,g^{\prime},v_{1},v_{2},\dots
with nonnegative coefficients:

(65)

\lim_{d\to\infty}\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\!\left[\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}},\,x^{\ell-2}\rangle\right]=\frac{v_{1}^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}}{(\ell-1)}\!\times\!\frac{g^{\prime}\cdot\partial}{\partial g}\!\left(\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathbf{L}_{0}(\ell)}{\frac{1}{|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma)|}\prod_{i\geq 1}(i\cdot g)^{|\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\rightarrow}^{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\,v_{i}^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}}\right)\!,

(66)

\lim_{d\to\infty}\Cov^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}},\,x^{k-2}\rangle,\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}},\,x^{l-2}\rangle\right)=\\
=\frac{v_{1}^{-\frac{k+l}{2}}}{(k-1)(l-1)}\sum_{\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})\in\mathbf{L}^{\conn}(k,l)\atop|\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|=1}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma_{1})||\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma_{2})|}\prod_{i\geq 1}(i\cdot g)^{|\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\rightarrow}^{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\,i^{\,|\mathbf{P}_{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\,v_{i}^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}.

These formulas are valid for all
\ell,k,l=2,3,\cdots
. In (
66
), the sum is over ribbon paths
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})\in\mathbf{L}(k,l)
with exactly one pairing between a vertex from
\mathbf{S}(\Gamma_{1})
and a vertex from
\mathbf{S}(\Gamma_{2})
(for the definition of more general versions of the set
\mathbf{L}^{\conn}(k,l)
, see
Section

3.8.2
).

Remark 9
.

The third author studied in
[
Mol23
]
a process analogous to

\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}
for Jack measures
M^{(\alpha)}_{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}}
with
identical specializations
\rho_{1}=\rho_{2}
and proved a CLT.
His formula for the covariances of the Gaussian fluctuations are given
via a ‘welding operator’
\mathcal{K}
applied to quantities
determined by the LLN; for more general cases, he also gave certain formula involving signs, which
is very different from ours, e.g. observe that
Eq.

66

is a cancellation-free polynomial with nonnegative coefficients.
Our arguments also work verbatim for Jack
measures under the assumptions of Moll, and provide explicit formulas akin to (
66
)
after replacing Łukasiewicz ribbon paths by all ribbon paths.

3.8.
Proofs of Theorems
3.7
and
3.8

We use the following statistic on ribbon paths:

F_{a,b,\mathbf{v}}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}):=\prod_{i\geq 1}(i\cdot a)^{\!|\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}(i\cdot b)^{\!|\mathbf{P}_{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\,v_{i}^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|},\ \text{ for variables }a,b.

First we study the combinatorics of Nazarov–Sklyanin operators, which have been used before for random partitions in
[
Hua21
,
Mol23
]
; however, our setting and results are new.

3.8.1.
Step 1 – Expressing observables via Nazarov–Sklyanin operators

Consider the infinite row vector

P=(P_{1,{k}})_{{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}}
and column vector
P^{\dagger}=(P^{\dagger}_{{k},1})_{{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}}
, where
P_{1,{k}}:=p_{{k}}
, and

P^{\dagger}_{{k},1}:=\alpha\cdot k\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{k}}=:p_{-{k}}
, are regarded as operators on the algebra of symmetric
functions
\Symm=\mathbb{R}[p_{1},p_{2},\cdots]
.
Let
L=(L_{i,j})_{i,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}}
be the infinite matrix defined by

L_{i,j}:=p_{j-i}+\delta_{i,j}\,i(\alpha-1)
, for all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, with the convention that
p_{0}:=0
:

L=\begin{bmatrix}(\alpha-1)&p_{1}&p_{2}&\dots\\
p_{-1}&2(\alpha-1)&p_{1}&\ddots\\
p_{-2}&p_{-1}&3(\alpha-1)&\ddots\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots\end{bmatrix}.

Theorem 3.9

(Nazarov–Sklyanin
[
NS13
]
)
.

For all
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and all
\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}
:

PL^{\ell}P^{\dagger}J_{\lambda}(\mathbf{p})=B_{\ell+2}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;1)})\cdot J_{\lambda}(\mathbf{p}).

Proof.

Nazarov and Sklyanin proved
[
NS13
, Theorem 2]

that

\sum_{k\geq 0}z^{-k-1}PL^{k}P^{\dagger}J_{\lambda}(\mathbf{p})=\left(z-(z+\ell(\lambda))\prod_{i=1}^{\ell(\lambda)}\frac{z+i-1-\alpha\lambda_{i}}{z+i-\alpha\lambda_{i}}\right)J_{\lambda}(\mathbf{p}).

Comparing the RHS of the equation
above with the identity (
42
) and using
(
46
) we can rewrite this RHS as

\left(z-\frac{1}{G_{\mu_{T_{\alpha,1}\lambda}}(z)}\right)J_{\lambda}(\mathbf{p})=B_{\mu_{\Lambda_{(\alpha;1)}}}(z)J_{\lambda}(\mathbf{p}),

which finishes the proof.
∎

Theorem 3.10
.

Fix arbitrary
\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. The following expectation of products of Boolean cumulants, with respect to the Jack–Thoma measure
M_{u;\mathbf{v}}^{(\alpha)}
, is given by:

(67)

\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(B_{\ell_{1}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\cdots B_{\ell_{n}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\right)=\sum_{\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}\in\mathbf{L}(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n})\atop|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|=n}{F_{\,\frac{\alpha-1}{u},\,\frac{\alpha}{u^{2}},\,\mathbf{v}}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})}.

Proof.

First, observe that if some
\ell_{i}=1
, then
B_{\ell_{i}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})=0
, so the LHS of (
67
) vanishes. On the other hand, the set
\mathbf{L}(\ell_{1},\dots,1,\dots,\ell_{n})
is empty, because the only excursion of length
1
is
((0,0),(1,0))
, but Łukasiewicz ribbon paths are not allowed to have horizontal steps at height
0
.
As a result, the RHS side of (
67
) also vanishes.

In the remainder of the proof, assume that
\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}\geq 2
.
Note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(B_{\ell_{1}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\cdots B_{\ell_{n}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\right)=

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\frac{u^{2}\cdot v_{1}}{\alpha}\right)\sum_{\lambda}\frac{\left(B_{\ell_{1}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\cdots B_{\ell_{n}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\right)\cdot J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}(u\cdot\mathbf{v})\cdot u^{|\lambda|}}{j^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}}

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\frac{u^{2}\cdot v_{1}}{\alpha}\right)u^{-\ell_{1}-\cdots-\ell_{n}}PL^{\ell_{1}-2}P^{\dagger}\cdots PL^{\ell_{n}-2}P^{\dagger}\sum_{\lambda}\frac{J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}(\mathbf{p})\cdot u^{|\lambda|}}{j^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}}\bigg|_{\mathbf{p}=u\cdot\mathbf{v}}

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\frac{u^{2}\cdot v_{1}}{\alpha}\right)u^{-\ell_{1}-\cdots-\ell_{n}}PL^{\ell_{1}-2}P^{\dagger}\cdots PL^{\ell_{n}-2}P^{\dagger}\exp\left(\frac{u\cdot p_{1}}{\alpha}\right)\bigg|_{\mathbf{p}=u\cdot\mathbf{v}},

where the first equality comes from the definition of the expectation and the definition (
53
) of
\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)}
, the second one comes from
Theorem

3.9
and (
54
), and the last equality follows from the Cauchy identity (
36
).
The final desired equality

(68)

e^{-\frac{u^{2}\cdot v_{1}}{\alpha}}\cdot u^{-\ell_{1}-\cdots-\ell_{n}}\cdot PL^{\ell_{1}-2}P^{\dagger}\cdots PL^{\ell_{n}-2}P^{\dagger}\,\!\!\left(e^{\frac{u\cdot p_{1}}{\alpha}}\right)\!\bigg|_{\mathbf{p}=u\cdot\mathbf{v}}\!=\!\sum_{\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}\in\mathbf{L}(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}),\atop|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|=n}{\!F_{\,\frac{\alpha-1}{u},\,\frac{\alpha}{u^{2}},\,\mathbf{v}}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})}

follows from the following combinatorial interpretation of each operator
PL^{\ell_{i}-2}P^{\dagger}
in terms of excursions: for any integer
\ell\geq 2
, we have

PL^{\ell-2}P^{\dagger}=\sum_{\Gamma\colon\ell(\Gamma)=\ell,\atop|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma)|=1}{L(\Gamma)},

where the sum is over excursions
\Gamma=(w_{0}=(0,y_{0}),\dots,w_{\ell}=(\ell,y_{\ell}))
of length
\ell
,
and
L(\Gamma)
is the operator
L(\Gamma):=p_{y_{1}}L_{y_{1},y_{2}}\cdots L_{y_{\ell-2},y_{\ell-1}}p_{-y_{\ell-1}}
, and the condition
|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma)|=1
indicates that the excursion touches the
x
-axis only at the beginning
w_{0}=(0,0)
and the end
w_{\ell}=(\ell,0)
.
The product of operators
PL^{\ell_{1}-2}P^{\dagger}\cdots PL^{\ell_{n}-2}P^{\dagger}
in the LHS of (
68
)
then equals by concatenation to a sum of operators
L(\Gamma_{1})\cdots L(\Gamma_{n})
over excursions
\vec{\Gamma}=(\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{n})
of length

\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{n}
touching the
x
-axis at exactly the
n
prescribed
positions:
\ell_{1},\ell_{1}+\ell_{2},\dots,\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{n}
.

Next, we would like to express each operator
L(\Gamma_{1})\cdots L(\Gamma_{n})
in the
normal ordered form, i.e. we would like to express it as a linear
combination of the operators of the form
p_{i_{1}}\cdots p_{i_{k}}p_{-j_{1}}\cdots p_{-j_{k^{\prime}}}
, where
i_{1},\dots,i_{k},j_{1},\dots,j_{k^{\prime}}\geq 1
. Note that the commutation relation

[p_{j},p_{i}]=\delta_{i+j,0}\cdot i\alpha
, equivalently
p_{j}p_{i}=p_{i}p_{j}+\delta_{i+j,0}\cdot i\alpha
, allows to move all the
operators
p_{-i}
with a negative index to the right, and the only
nontrivial commutation relation is
p_{-i}p_{i}=p_{i}p_{-i}+i\alpha
, which occurs when we try to move
p_{-i}
past
p_{i}
.
These two operators are associated with a
down step and a following up step of the same increments, and we
assign to such pair a pairing. Marking all the possible pairings in

\vec{\Gamma}
and remembering that each of them produces two
terms, namely
p_{i}p_{-i}
and
i\alpha
, leads to the following combinatorial
interpretation of the operator
L(\Gamma_{1})\cdots L(\Gamma_{n})

in normal ordered form:

(69)

L(\Gamma_{1})\cdots L(\Gamma_{n})=\sum_{\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})}\prod_{i\geq 1}(i\cdot(\alpha-1))^{\!|\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}(i\cdot\alpha)^{\!|\mathbf{P}_{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\,p_{i}^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\prod_{i\geq 1}\,p_{-i}^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{-i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|},

where the sum is over all possible sets of pairings.
Finally, note that

\prod_{i\geq 1}\,p_{-i}^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{-i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\!\left(e^{\frac{u\cdot p_{1}}{\alpha}}\right)\!\bigg|_{\mathbf{p}=u\cdot\mathbf{v}}=\begin{cases}e^{\frac{u^{2}\cdot v_{1}}{\alpha}}u^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\searrow}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|},&\text{ if }\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\searrow}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})=\mathbf{S}_{-1}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}),\\
0,&\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

In particular, the ribbon paths
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}
with non-paired down steps
of degree
\geq 2
give null contributions, therefore the LHS of
(
68
) can be rewritten as a sum over the set

\mathbf{L}(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n})
of Łukasiewicz ribbon paths with the
associated operators written in the normal ordered form as in (
69
).
For
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}=(\vec{\Gamma},\,\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}))\in\mathbf{L}(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n})
,
with
\vec{\Gamma}=(\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{n})
, one has that

L(\Gamma_{1})\cdots L(\Gamma_{n})\left(e^{\frac{u\cdot p_{1}}{\alpha}}\right)\!\big|_{\mathbf{p}=u\cdot\mathbf{v}}
is equal to

\sum_{\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})}\prod_{i\geq 1}(i\cdot(\alpha-1))^{\!|\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}(i\cdot\alpha)^{\!|\mathbf{P}_{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\,p_{i}^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\prod_{i\geq 1}\,p_{-i}^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{-i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\!\left(e^{\frac{u\cdot p_{1}}{\alpha}}\right)\!\bigg|_{\mathbf{p}=u\cdot\mathbf{v}}\\
=\sum_{\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})}\prod_{i\geq 1}(i\cdot(\alpha-1))^{\!|\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}(i\cdot\alpha)^{\!|\mathbf{P}_{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\,v_{i}^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\cdot e^{\frac{u^{2}\cdot v_{1}}{\alpha}}u^{|\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\searrow}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|+|\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\nearrow}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\\
=\sum_{\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})}F_{\,\frac{\alpha-1}{u},\,\frac{\alpha}{u^{2}},\,\mathbf{v}}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})\cdot e^{\frac{u^{2}\cdot v_{1}}{\alpha}}u^{|\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\searrow}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|+|\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|+|\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\nearrow}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|+2|\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\\
=\sum_{\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})}F_{\,\frac{\alpha-1}{u},\,\frac{\alpha}{u^{2}},\,\mathbf{v}}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})\cdot e^{\frac{u^{2}\cdot v_{1}}{\alpha}}u^{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{n}}.

This leads to the expression in the RHS of (
68
), as claimed.
∎

3.8.2.
Step 2 – Formulas for cumulants of observables of Jack–Thoma measures

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be random variables on the same probability space.
We recall that the classical (joint) cumulant
\kappa_{n}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
is
defined combinatorially through the following moment-cumulant formula:

(70)

\mathbb{E}(X_{1}\cdots X_{n}):=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}([1\,..\,n])}\prod_{B\in\pi}\kappa(X_{b}\colon b\in B),

where
[1\,..\,n]:=\{1,2,\dots,n\}
, and for any finite set
X
we denote by

\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}(X)
the set of
set-partitions
of
X
,
namely
\pi\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}(X)
means that
\pi
is a set of disjoint subsets of
X
(called the
blocks of
X
)
whose union is the whole set
X
.

Next, we give a slightly refined notion of

connected ribbon paths
, originally introduced in
[
Mol23
]
.
Let
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}=(\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{n})
be a ribbon path on
n
sites and
\pi\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}([1\,..\,n])

be a set-partition. We define
G^{\pi}_{\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}}
as the following simple graph: the vertex
set is
\pi
(so that vertices are indexed by the blocks of
\pi
) and an edge joins

B,B^{\prime}\in\pi
if and only if there exists at least one pairing
p=(v,w)\in\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})

such that
v\in\mathbf{S}(\Gamma_{b}),w\in\mathbf{S}(\Gamma_{b^{\prime}})
,
or
w\in\mathbf{S}(\Gamma_{b}),v\in\mathbf{S}(\Gamma_{b^{\prime}})
,
for some
b\in B,\,b^{\prime}\in B^{\prime}
.
We say that a ribbon path
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}
is
\pi
-connected
if the associated graph
G^{\pi}_{\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}}
is connected. If
\pi=\{\{1\},\dots,\{n\}\}
and
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}
is
\pi
-connected, we simply say that
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}
is
connected
.
See
Fig.

6
for some examples.

Figure 6
.
The first graph depicts
G^{\{\{1\},\{2\},\{3\},\{4\}\}}_{\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}}
for the ribbon path

\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}
from
Fig.

5
; observe that

\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}
is not connected. The next two graphs
G^{\pi}_{\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}}

correspond to other set-partitions
\pi\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}([1\,..\,4])
; in both cases,
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}
is
\pi
-connected.

Recall that
\mathbf{L}(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n})
denotes the set of
Łukasiewicz ribbon paths on
n
sites of lengths

\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}
. We denote by
\mathbf{L}^{\pi-\conn}(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n})

(resp.
\mathbf{L}^{\conn}(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n})
) the subset of

\mathbf{L}(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n})
of those Łukasiewicz ribbon paths
that are
\pi
-connected (resp. connected).

Proposition 3.11
.

Fix arbitrary
\ell^{1}_{1},\dots,\ell^{1}_{k_{1}},\ell^{2}_{1},\dots,\ell^{2}_{k_{2}},\dots,\ell^{n}_{1},\dots,\ell^{n}_{k_{n}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.
We have the following formula for a cumulant with respect to the Jack–Thoma measure
M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}
:

(71)

\kappa^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\!\left(\!B_{\ell^{1}_{1}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\cdots B_{\ell^{1}_{k_{1}}}\!\!(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)}),\dots,B_{\ell^{n}_{1}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\cdots B_{\ell^{n}_{k_{n}}}\!\!(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\!\right)\!=\!\sum_{\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}}{F_{\frac{\alpha-1}{u},\frac{\alpha}{u^{2}},\mathbf{v}}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})}.

The sum above is over
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}\in\mathbf{L}^{\pi-\conn}(\ell^{1}_{1},\dots,\ell^{1}_{k_{1}},\dots,\ell^{n}_{1},\dots,\ell^{n}_{k_{n}})
, with
|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|=k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n}
, and where
\pi\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}([1\,..\,k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n}])
is the set-partition with
n
blocks defined by
\pi:=\{[1\,..\,k_{1}],[k_{1}+1\,..\,k_{1}+k_{2}],\dots,[k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n-1}+1\,..\,k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n}]\}
, and we employed the usual notation
[a\,..\,b]:=\{a,a+1,\dots,b\}
, for any integers
a\leq b
.

Proof.

Fix the set-partition
\pi:=\{[1\,..\,k_{1}],[k_{1}+1\,..\,k_{1}+k_{2}],\dots,[k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n-1}+1\,..\,k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n}]\}
of
[1\,..\,k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n}]
.
Let
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}\in\mathbf{L}(\ell^{1}_{1},\dots,\ell^{1}_{k_{1}},\dots,\ell^{n}_{1},\dots,\ell^{n}_{k_{n}})
be any Łukasiewicz ribbon path.
Then we can define
\sigma=\sigma(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}(\pi)
as the set-partition whose blocks are
sets of vertices of the connected components of

G^{\,\pi}_{\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}}
. For each
B\in\sigma
, let

\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}_{B}
be the concatenation of excursions

\Gamma_{b}
,
b\in\bigcup B
, with respect to the natural order. Since
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}(\pi)
is isomorphic
to
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}([1\,..\,n])
, we will denote by
\tilde{B}
the image of a
block
B\in\sigma
by the natural map

[k_{1}+\cdots+k_{i-1}+1\,..\,k_{1}+\cdots+k_{i}]\to i
.
By construction, it is evident that each

\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}_{B}
is a well-defined,
\pi|_{B}
-connected Łukasiewicz
ribbon path
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}_{B}\in\mathbf{L}^{\pi|_{B}-\conn}(\ell^{b}_{1},\dots,\ell^{b}_{k_{b}}\colon b\in\tilde{B})
.

Conversely, let
\sigma\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}(\pi)
be arbitrary, and for any block
B\in\sigma

assume that we have a
\pi|_{B}
-connected Łukasiewicz ribbon path
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}_{B}\in\mathbf{L}^{\pi|_{B}-\conn}(\ell^{b}_{1},\dots,\ell^{b}_{k_{b}}\colon b\in\tilde{B})
.
Then one can construct a (non-necessarily
\pi
-connected)
Łukasiewicz ribbon path
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}\in\mathbf{L}(\ell^{1}_{1},\dots,\ell^{1}_{k_{1}},\dots,\ell^{n}_{1},\dots,\ell^{n}_{k_{n}})
as a concatenation of
the excursions of lengths
\ell_{1}^{1},\dots,\ell_{k_{n}}^{n}
that make up the
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}_{B}
’s,
and the set of pairings of
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}
is defined as the union of sets of pairings for
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}_{B}
’s, for

B\in\sigma
.

Note that
F_{\,\frac{\alpha-1}{u},\,\frac{\alpha}{u^{2}},\,\mathbf{v}}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})=\prod_{B\in\sigma}{F_{\,\frac{\alpha-1}{u},\,\frac{\alpha}{u^{2}},\,\mathbf{v}}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}_{B})}
.
Using
Theorem

3.10
, this gives:

(72)

\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(B_{\ell^{1}_{1}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\cdots B_{\ell^{1}_{k_{1}}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\cdots B_{\ell^{n}_{1}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\cdots B_{\ell^{n}_{k_{n}}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\right)\\
=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}(\pi)}\prod_{B\in\sigma}\sum_{\,\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}_{B}\in\mathbf{L}^{\pi|_{B}-\conn}(\ell^{b}_{1},\dots,\ell^{b}_{k_{b}}\colon b\in\tilde{B})\atop|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}_{B})|=\sum_{b\in\tilde{B}}k_{b}}{F_{\,\frac{\alpha-1}{u},\,\frac{\alpha}{u^{2}},\,\mathbf{v}}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}_{B})}.

Comparing this equation with (
70
) that defines cumulants, we conclude the proof.
∎

Corollary 3.12
.

For arbitrary
\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, we have

(73)

\kappa^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(S_{\ell_{1}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)}),\dots,S_{\ell_{n}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\right)=\sum_{\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}\in\mathbf{L}^{\conn}(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n})\atop\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}=(\Gamma_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{n})}\,\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma_{j})|}\cdot F_{\,\frac{\alpha-1}{u},\,\frac{\alpha}{u^{2}},\,\mathbf{v}}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}).

Proof.

It follows immediately from
Proposition

3.11
by using
multilinearity of the cumulant
\kappa^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}
and (
50
).
∎

3.8.3.
Step 3 – Concluding the proofs of Theorems
3.7
and
3.8

We start with the following:

Claim 3.13
.

Any Łukasiewicz ribbon path
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}\in\mathbf{L}(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n})
stays below height
\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{n}
, i.e.
\mathbf{S}^{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})=\emptyset
, whenever
i\geq\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{n}
.
Moreover,
\mathbf{L}(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n})
is finite.

Proof.

The first statement easily follows from the fact that the non-paired
down steps of any Łukasiewicz ribbon path have degree
1
, and we leave the details to the reader. The first statement implies that the set
\mathbf{L}(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n})
has cardinality upper bounded by
\big(2(\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{n})+1\big)^{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{n}}
, and in particular is finite.
∎

End of proof of
Theorem

3.7
.

For the LLN, i.e.
Eq.

63
, we first prove a general result that might be useful for studying the asymptotic behavior of random partitions in other contexts.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{<\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be the set of positive finite measures on

\mathbb{R}
that have finite moments of all orders. Recall that
M_{\ell}(\mu):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}{x^{\ell}d\mu(x)}
denotes the
\ell
-th moment of the measure
\mu
.

Theorem 3.14

(
[
Bil95
]
, Thm. 30.2)
.

Let
(\mu_{d})_{d\geq 1}\subset\mathcal{M}_{<\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be any sequence and
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{<\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be a measure that is uniquely determined by its moments. Suppose that
\lim_{d\to\infty}M_{\ell}(\mu_{d})=M_{\ell}(\mu)
for every
\ell\geq 0
, then
\mu_{d}
converges to
\mu
weakly.

Remark 10
.

The above theorem is stated in
[
Bil95
]
for
probability measures, but the proof in
[
Bil95
]

works for
\mathcal{M}_{<\infty}(\mathbb{R})
in the same way.

Theorem 3.15
.

Let
(f_{d})_{d\geq 1}
be a sequence of random
C
-Lipschitz
functions
f_{d}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that
f_{d}(x)dx\in\mathcal{M}_{<\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Suppose that

(a)

for every
\epsilon>0
, there exists
N>0
such that
\lim_{d\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{d}\left(\|f_{d}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\setminus[-N,N])}>\epsilon\right)=0
,

(b)

there exists a measure
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{<\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, uniquely determined by its moments, such that
f_{d}(x)dx
converges to
\mu
in moments, in probability, i.e. for every
\epsilon>0
and
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we have

\lim_{d\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}_{d}\left(\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}{x^{\ell}f_{d}(x)dx}-M_{\ell}(\mu)\right|>\epsilon\right)}=0.

Then,
(f_{d})_{d\geq 1}
has a deterministic limit
f\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
in the supremum norm, in probability:

\forall\epsilon>0,\qquad\lim_{d\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{d}\left(\|f_{d}-f\|_{\infty}>\epsilon\right)=0.

The function
f
is
C
-Lipschitz, and is a probability density of the measure
\mu
.

Proof.

Step 1.
Let
\epsilon>0,\,\psi\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R})
be arbitrary.
In this step, we show that (b) implies

(74)

\lim_{d\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}_{d}\left(\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\psi(x)f_{d}(x)dx}-\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\psi(x)d\mu(x)}\right|\leq\epsilon\right)}=1.

Consider the following metric
\pi
on
\mathcal{M}_{<\infty}(\mathbb{R})
:

\pi(\nu_{1},\nu_{2}):=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{\min\left(1,\,|M_{i}(\nu_{1})-M_{i}(\nu_{2})|\right)}{2^{i}}.

From the definition of
\pi
, it follows that for any
\epsilon>0
, there exist
\epsilon^{\prime}>0,\,k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
such that:

\text{ if $\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{<\infty}(\mathbb{R})$ satisfies }\ |M_{1}(\nu)-M_{1}(\mu)|,\dots,|M_{k}(\nu)-M_{k}(\mu)|\leq\epsilon^{\prime}\Longrightarrow\pi(\nu,\mu)\leq\epsilon.

As a result,
\pi
metrizes convergence in moments, so that the assumption (b) is equivalent to the following condition:
for every
\epsilon>0
, we have

(75)

\lim_{d\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}_{d}\left(\pi(f_{d}(x)dx,\mu)\leq\epsilon\right)}=1.

A classical theorem of Lev́y says that the weak convergence in

\mathcal{M}_{<\infty}(\mathbb{R})
is metrizable (see
[
Var58
]

for a more general result); let
\tilde{\pi}
denote the corresponding
metric. In other terms, for any sequence of measures

(\nu_{n})_{n\geq 1}\subset\mathcal{M}_{<\infty}(\mathbb{R})
one has
\nu_{n}\to\mu
weakly, if and only if

\tilde{\pi}(\nu_{n},\mu)\to 0
, as
n\to\infty
. Consequently, (
74
) would be implied if for every
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
, we have

(76)

\lim_{d\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}_{d}\left(\tilde{\pi}(f_{d}(x)dx,\mu)\leq\epsilon^{\prime}\right)}=1.

Note that
Theorem

3.14

implies that for any
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
, there exists
\epsilon>0
such that

\pi(\nu,\mu)\leq\epsilon\Longrightarrow\tilde{\pi}(\nu,\mu)\leq\epsilon^{\prime}
.
Therefore
\mathbb{P}_{d}\left(\pi(f_{d}(x)dx,\mu)\leq\epsilon\right)\leq\mathbb{P}_{d}\left(\tilde{\pi}(f_{d}(x)dx,\mu)\leq\epsilon^{\prime}\right)
, and
(
75
) implies the desired limit (
76
).

Step 2.
Fix
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and let
\psi_{\epsilon}^{x_{0}}\in C_{c}(\mathbb{R})
be a nonnegative function supported on
[x_{0}-\epsilon,x_{0}+\epsilon]
with
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi_{\epsilon}^{x_{0}}(x)dx=1
.
For any
C
-Lipschitz function
g
, one has

\left|g(x_{0})-\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\psi_{\epsilon}^{x_{0}}(x)g(x)dx}\right|\leq\!\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\psi_{\epsilon}^{x_{0}}(x)|g(x_{0})-g(x)|dx}\leq C\epsilon,

therefore

\left|g(x_{0})-\!\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi_{\epsilon}^{x_{0}}(x)d\mu(x)\right|\leq C\epsilon+\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\psi_{\epsilon}^{x_{0}}(x)g(x)dx}-\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\psi_{\epsilon}^{x_{0}}(x)d\mu(x)}\right|.

When we take
g=f_{d}
we obtain

\mathbb{P}_{d}\left(\left|f_{d}(x_{0})-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi_{\epsilon}^{x_{0}}(x)d\mu(x)\right|\leq(C+1)\epsilon\!\right)\\
\geq\mathbb{P}_{d}\left(\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi_{\epsilon}^{x_{0}}(x)f_{d}(x)dx-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi_{\epsilon}^{x_{0}}(x)d\mu(x)\right|\leq\epsilon\right).

Step 1 shows that this RHS above converges to
1
, as
d\to\infty
.
Consequently, for any
\epsilon>0
, we have:

\lim_{d\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{d}\left(|f_{d}(x_{0})-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi_{\epsilon}^{x_{0}}(x)d\mu(x)|\leq(C+1)\epsilon\right)=1
. Passing to the limit
\epsilon\to 0
, we get that

\lim_{d\to\infty}f_{d}(x_{0})=f(x_{0}):=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi_{\epsilon}^{x_{0}}(x)d\mu(x),

where the first equality holds in probability.
Since
x_{0}
was arbitrary, we have a pointwise convergence
f_{d}\to f
, in probability. This pointwise convergence can be extended to the
uniform convergence on any
closed interval, in probability, by a standard
argument of covering a closed interval by finitely many small
intervals, and the fact that that the supremum norm of
|f_{d}-f|
on a
small interval is small by the Lipschitz condition. Therefore for every
N,\epsilon>0

\lim_{d\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{d}\left(\|f_{d}-f\|_{L^{\infty}[-N,N]}>\epsilon\right)=0.

Finally, fix any
\epsilon>0
and take
N>0
as in condition (a) from the hypothesis; then

\mathbb{P}_{d}\left(\|f_{d}-f\|_{\infty}>\epsilon\right)\leq\mathbb{P}_{d}\left(\|f_{d}-f\|_{L^{\infty}[-N,N]}>\epsilon\right)+\mathbb{P}_{d}\left(\|f_{d}-f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\setminus[-N,N])}>\epsilon\right)\to 0

as
d\to\infty
. Since the functions
f_{d}
are
\geq 0
and
C
-Lipschitz,
f
has the same properties.

Step 3.
It remains to show that
f
is the density of
\mu
. As

\mu
is uniquely determined by its moments, it suffices to verify

\int_{\mathbb{R}}{x^{\ell}f(x)dx}=M_{\ell}(\mu)
, for all
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Fix
\ell\geq 0
and
R>0
,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{|x|\geq R}{\!x^{\ell}f_{d}(x)dx}\right|\leq\int_{|x|\geq R}{\!|x|^{\ell}f_{d}(x)dx}\leq\frac{1}{R^{\ell+2}}\!\int_{|x|\geq R}{\!x^{2\ell+2}f_{d}(x)dx}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{R^{\ell+2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}{x^{2\ell+2}f_{d}(x)dx}\leq\frac{1}{R^{\ell+2}}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}{x^{2\ell+2}f_{d}(x)dx}-M_{2\ell+2}(\mu)\right|+\frac{M_{2\ell+2}(\mu)}{R^{\ell+2}}.

This inequality and condition (b) show that for any
\epsilon>0
, there exists
R_{0}>0
such that

(77)

\lim_{d\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}_{d}\left(\left|\int_{|x|\geq R}{x^{\ell}f_{d}(x)dx}\right|<\epsilon\right)}=1,\quad\forall\,R>R_{0}.

Note that
\left|M_{\ell}(\mu)-\int_{|x|\leq R}{x^{\ell}f(x)dx}\right|
is bounded from above by

\bigg|M_{\ell}(\mu)-M_{\ell}(f_{d}(x)dx)\bigg|+\left|\int_{|x|\geq R}{x^{\ell}f_{d}(x)dx}\right|+\left|\int_{-R}^{R}x^{\ell}(f_{d}(x)-f(x))dx\right|.

For any
\epsilon>0
, there exists
R_{0}>0
such that for every
R>R_{0}

the probability
\mathbb{P}_{d}
that each of these terms is smaller than

\epsilon
tends to
1
, as
d\to\infty
. Indeed, for the first term this is condition
(b), for the second this is (
77
), and for the third
one this is convergence of
f_{d}\to f
uniformly on
[-R,R]
in
probability, proved in Step 2. Taking the limit as
R\to\infty
, it follows that the integral
\int_{\mathbb{R}}{x^{\ell}f(x)dx}
converges and equals
M_{\ell}(\mu)
, as desired.
∎

We are going to prove
Theorem

3.7
by
applying
Theorem

3.15
to the collection of functions

f_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}(x):=\frac{\omega_{\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}}(x)-|x|}{2},\quad x\in\mathbb{R},

that are parametrized by an integer
d\geq 1
(recall that
\alpha=\alpha(d),\,u=u(d)
).
In our setting,
\lambda
is an
M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}
-distributed random partition, and

\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}:=T_{\frac{\alpha}{u\sqrt{v_{1}}},\frac{1}{u\sqrt{v_{1}}}}\lambda
, therefore
(f_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})})_{d\geq 1}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
is a collection of random
1
–Lipschitz compactly supported functions. Let us verify the two conditions required by
Theorem

3.7
.

Step 1: For every
\epsilon>0
, there exists
N>0
such that

\lim_{d\to\infty}{M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(\|f_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\setminus[-N,N])}>\epsilon\right)}=0.

Let
U=U(d):=\frac{u^{2}\cdot v_{1}}{\alpha}
. Note that if the point
(x_{0},y_{0})

in the first quadrant belongs to the rescaled diagram
\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}
, then the whole rectangle

(0,0)
–
(x_{0},0)
–
(x_{0},y_{0})
–
(0,y_{0})
is contained in

\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}
, therefore
x_{0}\cdot y_{0}\leq|\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}|=\frac{|\lambda|}{U}
. Applying this inequality to
x_{0}=(\omega_{\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}}(x)+|x|)/2\,
and
y_{0}=(\omega_{\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}}(x)-|x|)/2
, we obtain

|x|\leq\omega_{\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}}(x)\leq\sqrt{x^{2}+4\cdot\frac{|\lambda|}{U}},

and consequently

(78)

0\leq f_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}(x)\leq\frac{\sqrt{x^{2}+4\cdot\frac{|\lambda|}{U}}-|x|}{2}\leq\frac{\sqrt{x^{2}+8e}-|x|}{2}=\frac{4e}{\sqrt{x^{2}+8e}+|x|},

whenever
|\lambda|\leq 2e\cdot U
. Note that for every
\epsilon>0
, there exists
N>0
such that the RHS of
(
78
) is smaller or equal to
\epsilon
, for all
|x|\geq N
. Therefore it is enough to prove that

(79)

\lim_{d\to\infty}M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(|\lambda|>2e\cdot U\right)=0.

By looking at the coefficient of
t^{i}
in the Cauchy identity (
36
), we deduce that the measure
M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}
gives
\mathbb{Y}_{i}
a mass of
e^{-U}\cdot\frac{U^{i}}{i!}
, therefore for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, one has

M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(|\lambda|>k\right)\leq\exp(-U)\sum_{i=k}^{\infty}\frac{U^{i}}{i!}\leq\frac{U^{k}}{k!}=O\left(\left(\frac{e\cdot U}{k}\right)^{\!k}\cdot k^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right)\!,\text{ as }k\to\infty,

where the last equality follows from the Stirling’s formula.
Observe that our assumptions (
61
) imply that
U\to\infty
as
d\to\infty
, therefore if we choose
k:=\lfloor 2e\cdot U\rfloor
, then also
k\to\infty
as
d\to\infty
.
We conclude that (
79
) holds true, and this finishes the proof of this step.

Step 2: There exists a probability measure
P_{\infty}\in\mathcal{M}_{<\infty}(\mathbb{R})
such that
f_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}(x)dx
converges to
P_{\infty}
in moments, in probability.

Consider the function
\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}[f_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}(x)]
, which for simplicity we denote
\mathbb{E}[f(x)]
.
Eq.

56
shows that, for any
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we have

(80)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}{x^{\ell}\,\mathbb{E}[f(x)]dx}=\mathbb{E}\left[\!\int_{\mathbb{R}}{x^{\ell}f(x)dx}\right]\!=\!\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[S_{\ell+2}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})})\right]}{\ell+1}.

When
\ell=0
, this shows
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\mathbb{E}[f(x)]dx}=\mathbb{E}[S_{2}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})})]=v_{1}^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{E}[S_{2}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})]
and this equals
1
, by
Eq.

73
. Therefore
\mathbb{E}[f(x)]
is the density of a probability measure,
10
10

10

We point out that this is why we’ve considered the rescaled partition
\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}
with
u\sqrt{v_{1}}
instead of
u
.
to be denoted by
P_{d}
.

Since
\mathbf{L}^{\conn}(\ell)=\mathbf{L}(\ell)
is finite and
S_{\ell}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})})=v_{1}^{-\ell/2}\cdot S_{\ell}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})
, then
Eq.

73
shows that the following limits exist:

(81)

\lim_{d\to\infty}\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left[S_{\ell}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})})\right]=v_{1}^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\lim_{d\to\infty}\kappa^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(S_{\ell}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\right)\\
=v_{1}^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathbf{L}_{0}(\ell)}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma)|}\prod_{i\geq 1}(i\cdot g)^{|\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\Gamma)|}\,v_{i}^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\Gamma)|},

for all
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. Note that since
\alpha/u^{2}\to 0
, then the only
paths that survive from (
73
) are the ones with
|\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|=0
;
this explains why after taking the limit as
d\to\infty
, the sum above is over

\Gamma\in\mathbf{L}_{0}(\ell)
(with no pairings) and not over
\Gamma\in\mathbf{L}(\ell)
.
Similarly, note that any Łukasiewicz ribbon path
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}\in\mathbf{L}^{\conn}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})

must have at least
1
pairing for the connectivity condition to hold,
i.e.
|\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|=\sum_{i\geq 1}{|\mathbf{P}_{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\geq 1
,
therefore
Eq.

73
also implies

(82)

\lim_{d\to\infty}\text{Var}^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left[S_{\ell}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})})\right]=v_{1}^{-\ell}\lim_{d\to\infty}{\kappa^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(S_{\ell}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)}),S_{\ell}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\right)}=0.

From (
81
), (
82
), and Chebyshev’s inequality,
we have that for any
\epsilon>0
and any
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
,

(83)

\lim_{d\to\infty}{M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(|S_{\ell}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})})-s_{\ell}|\geq\epsilon\right)}=0,

where

(84)

s_{\ell}:=v_{1}^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathbf{L}_{0}(\ell)}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma)|}\prod_{i\geq 1}(i\cdot g)^{|\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\Gamma)|}\,v_{i}^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\Gamma)|}.

Next, observe that (
80
), (
81
) and
(
82
) imply that the moments and variances of the
measures
P_{d}
are uniformly bounded. Thus
(P_{d})_{d\geq 1}
is a tight
sequence of probability measures, and thus it contains a subsequence

(P_{d_{k}})_{k\geq 1}
that converges weakly to some probability measure

P_{\infty}
. The limit (
81
) shows further that the

\ell
-th moment of
P_{\infty}
is
\frac{s_{\ell+2}}{\ell+1}
, for all

\ell\geq 0
. Finally (
80
) and (
83
)
imply that for any
\epsilon>0
and any
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, we have that

\lim_{d\to\infty}{M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}{x^{\ell}f_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}(x)dx}-\int_{\mathbb{R}}{x^{\ell}P_{\infty}(dx)}\right|\geq\epsilon\right)}=0.

In
Section

6
, we show that
P_{\infty}
is uniquely
determined by its moments (see
Corollary

6.3
), which finishes the proof.

Step 3: Conclusion of the argument.

Steps 1 and 2 prove that the two conditions in
Theorem

3.15
are satisfied for the sequence
(f_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})})_{d\geq 1}
and
\mu=P_{\infty}
.
Consequently, there exists a deterministic function
f_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, which is a probability density function of
P_{\infty}
, such that for every
\epsilon>0
:

\lim_{d\to\infty}M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(\|f_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}-f_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}\|_{\infty}>\epsilon\right)=0.

Consequently, setting
\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}(x):=2f_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}(x)+|x|
, we conclude the convergence of
\omega_{\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}}
to

\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}
in the supremum norm, in probability.

Finally, the fact that formula (
64
) gives the moments of the
associated transition measure
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
follows from Markov–Krein correspondence (
Theorem

3.3
) and
Proposition

3.2
.

End of proof of
Theorem

3.8
.
For the CLT, let us first prove the following limits:

(85)

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\

\displaystyle\kappa^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}},x^{k-2}\rangle,\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}},x^{l-2}\rangle\right)=\frac{v_{1}^{-\frac{k+l}{2}}}{(k-1)(l-1)}\times

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\sum_{\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})\in\mathbf{L}^{\conn}(k,l)\atop|\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|=1}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma_{1})||\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma_{2})|}\prod_{i\geq 1}(i\cdot g)^{|\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\rightarrow}^{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\,i^{\,|\mathbf{P}_{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\,v_{i}^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|},\quad\forall\,k,l\geq 2,

(86)

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}\

\displaystyle\kappa^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}},x^{\ell_{1}-2}\rangle,\dots,\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}},x^{\ell_{n}-2}\rangle\right)=0,\quad\forall\,n>2,\ \forall\,\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{n}\geq 2.

In order to compute

\lim_{d\to\infty}\kappa^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}},x^{\ell_{1}-2}\rangle,\dots,\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}},x^{\ell_{n}-2}\rangle\right)

for
n\geq 2
,
first use the fact that the cumulant
\kappa^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}
of order
n\geq 2
is invariant by any translation
of any argument by a scalar, so that using also (
56
) we
have the following equalities

(87)

\kappa^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}},x^{\ell_{1}-2}\rangle,\dots,\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}},x^{\ell_{n}-2}\rangle\right)\\
=\left(\frac{u}{\sqrt{\alpha}}\right)^{\!n}\cdot\,\kappa^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(\langle f_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})},x^{\ell_{1}-2}\rangle,\dots,\langle f_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})},x^{\ell_{n}-2}\rangle\right)\\
=\left(\frac{u}{\sqrt{\alpha}}\right)^{\!n}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{(\ell_{i}-1)}\cdot\kappa^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(S_{\ell_{1}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}),\dots,S_{\ell_{n}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})})\right).

Next, use the scaling property (
54
) that shows

S_{\ell}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})})=v_{1}^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\cdot S_{\ell}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;\sqrt{v_{1}})})
,
and then (
73
) from
Corollary

3.12
.
Note that the sum in the last equation is over connected Łukasiewicz ribbon paths

\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}\in\mathbf{L}^{\conn}(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n})
on
n
sites, and
each such
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}
must have at least
(n-1)
pairings for it to be connected,
i.e.
|\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|=\sum_{i\geq 1}{|\mathbf{P}_{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\geq n-1
.
As a result:

(88)

\kappa^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(S_{\ell_{1}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})}),\dots,S_{\ell_{n}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})})\right)=\bigg(\frac{\alpha}{u^{2}}\bigg)^{\!n-1}\cdot v_{1}^{-\frac{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{n}}{2}}\times\\
\times\Bigg(\sum_{\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}\in\mathbf{L}^{\conn}(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}),\atop|\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|=n-1}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma_{j})|}\prod_{i\geq 1}\bigg(i\cdot\frac{\alpha-1}{u}\bigg)^{\!|\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}i^{\,|\mathbf{P}_{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\,v_{i}^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}+\frac{\alpha}{u^{2}}\cdot X_{n}\Bigg),

where
X_{n}
is some polynomial in
\mathbb{Q}[\frac{\alpha}{u^{2}},\frac{\alpha-1}{u},v_{1},v_{2},\dots]
.
By combining (
87
) and (
88
), we have an expression of the form

\kappa^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}},x^{\ell_{1}-2}\rangle,\dots,\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}},x^{\ell_{n}-2}\rangle\right)=\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}}{u}\bigg)^{\!n-2}\cdot\frac{v_{1}^{-\frac{\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{n}}{2}}}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}{(\ell_{i}-1)}}\cdot\left(Y_{n}+\frac{\alpha}{u^{2}}\cdot X_{n}\right),

where
Y_{n}
is the sum in the RHS of
Eq.

88
.
Notice that
X_{n}
and
Y_{n}
have finite limits as
d\to\infty
: in fact, just replace
\frac{\alpha-1}{u}\mapsto g,\,\frac{\alpha}{u^{2}}\mapsto 0
in their expressions.
By taking the
d\to\infty
limit of this equation, we obtain
(
85
) and (
86
).

To conclude the theorem, we show that
\,\lim_{d\to\infty}{\kappa^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}(\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}},x^{\ell-2}\rangle)}
is equal to the RHS of
Eq.

65
.
Let us use the notations inside the proof of
Theorem

3.7
. By definition,

\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}=\frac{u}{2\sqrt{\alpha}}(\omega_{\Lambda_{(\alpha;\,u\sqrt{v_{1}})}}-\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}})=\frac{u}{\sqrt{\alpha}}(f_{(\alpha;\,u\sqrt{v_{1}})}-f_{g;\mathbf{v}})
.
Together with (
56
) and (
54
), we obtain

\displaystyle\kappa^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}},x^{\ell-2}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{u}{\sqrt{\alpha}}\cdot\left\{\kappa^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(\langle f_{(\alpha;u\sqrt{v_{1}})},x^{\ell-2}\rangle\right)-\langle f_{g;\mathbf{v}},x^{\ell-2}\rangle\right\}

(89)

\displaystyle=\frac{u}{\sqrt{\alpha}}\cdot\left\{\frac{v_{1}^{-\ell/2}}{\ell-1}\cdot\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left[S_{\ell}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\right]-\frac{s_{\ell}}{\ell-1}\right\}.

By (
73
) and (
84
),
the expression inside brackets in (
89
) can be written as a sum of
weighted ribbon paths in
\mathbf{L}(\ell)
.
Let
U
be the sum over those
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}\in\mathbf{L}(\ell)

with
|\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|\geq 1
, and
V
the sum over those
with
|\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|=0
.
Note that
U=\frac{\alpha}{u^{2}}\cdot U^{\prime}
, for some

U^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}[\frac{\alpha-1}{u},\frac{\alpha}{u^{2}},v_{1}^{-1/2},\{v_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}]
.
On the other hand,
V=\widetilde{V}(\frac{\alpha-1}{u})-\widetilde{V}(g)
,
where
\widetilde{V}(g)
is the
polynomial expression for
\frac{s_{\ell}}{\ell-1}
, see (
84
), and therefore

V=\left(\frac{\alpha-1}{u}-g\right)\cdot V^{\prime}
, for some

V^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}[\frac{\alpha-1}{u},g,v_{1}^{-1/2},\{v_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}]
.

Then the expression in brackets equals
\frac{\alpha}{u^{2}}\cdot U^{\prime}+\left(\frac{\alpha-1}{u}-g\right)\cdot V^{\prime}
, and it follows that

(90)

\kappa^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}},x^{\ell-2}\rangle\right)=\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}}{u}\cdot U^{\prime}+\frac{u}{\sqrt{\alpha}}\left(\frac{\alpha-1}{u}-g\right)\cdot V^{\prime}.

Both
U^{\prime}
and
V^{\prime}
have finite limits as
d\to\infty
and moreover, by the refined version of
3.6
,
\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}}{u}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}(1+o(1)),\,\frac{u}{\sqrt{\alpha}}=\sqrt{d}(1+o(1)),\,\frac{\alpha-1}{u}-g=\frac{g^{\prime}}{\sqrt{d}}(1+o(1))
; they imply that

\,\lim_{d\to\infty}{\kappa^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}(\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}},x^{\ell-2}\rangle)}=g^{\prime}\cdot\lim_{d\to\infty}{V^{\prime}}
. But we also have
\lim_{d\to\infty}{V^{\prime}}=\lim_{\frac{\alpha-1}{u}\to g}{(\widetilde{V}(\frac{\alpha-1}{u})-\widetilde{V}(g))(\frac{\alpha-1}{u}-g)^{-1}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial g}\widetilde{V}(g)
.
Hence, we have finally obtained the RHS of
Eq.

65
, and have thus concluded the proof of the CLT.

4.
Parameters for Jack–Thoma measures

In this section, we investigate possible choices of parameters
u>0,\,\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
, for which the Jack–Thoma measures
M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}
from
Definition

3.5
are well-defined probability measures, both for fixed
\alpha>0
, and for all
\alpha>0
.
Moreover, we find large families of parameters for which
3.6
is satisfied.

4.1.
Jack-positive specializations

A
specialization
of the
\mathbb{R}
-algebra of symmetric functions
\Symm
is by definition a unital algebra
homomorphism
\rho:\Symm\to\mathbb{R}
.
Since
\Symm=\mathbb{R}[p_{1},p_{2},\cdots]
, then

\rho
is uniquely determined by the values
\rho(p_{1}),\rho(p_{2}),\cdots
.
If we set
v_{k}:=\rho(p_{k})
, for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, and
\mathbf{v}:=(v_{1},v_{2},\cdots)
,
we say that
\mathbf{v}
determines the specialization
\rho
.
For any
f\in\Symm
, we denote its image under
\rho
by either
\rho(f)
or
f(\mathbf{v})
.

Definition 4.1
.

The specialization
\rho:\Symm\to\mathbb{R}
is an
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
-Jack-positive specialization
if

\rho\left(J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}\right)\geq 0\quad\forall\,\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}.

Observe that the previous definition depends on the value of
\alpha>0
.

Theorem 4.2

(Kerov–Okounkov–Olshanski
[
KOO98
]
)
.

Assume that
\alpha>0
is fixed. The set of
\alpha
-Jack-positive specializations

\rho:\Symm\to\mathbb{R}
is in bijection with the
Thoma cone

\Omega=\{(a,b,c)\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}\times\mathbb{R}^{\infty}\times\mathbb{R}\mid a=(a_{1},a_{2},\cdots),\quad b=(b_{1},b_{2},\cdots),\\
a_{1}\geq a_{2}\geq\cdots\geq 0,\quad b_{1}\geq b_{2}\geq\cdots\geq 0,\quad\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{(a_{i}+b_{i})}\leq c\}.

For any
\omega=(a,b,c)\in\Omega
, the corresponding
\alpha
-Jack-positive
specialization
\rho_{\omega}\!:\Symm\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by

\rho_{\omega}(p_{1})=c,\qquad\rho_{\omega}(p_{k})=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{a_{i}^{k}}+(-\alpha)^{1-k}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{b_{i}^{k}},\quad k=2,3,\cdots.

Example 4
.

Given
u>0
, the
Plancherel
u
-specialization

\tau_{u}:\Symm\to\mathbb{R}
is
defined by

\tau_{u}(p_{1})=u,\qquad\tau_{u}(p_{2})=\tau_{u}(p_{3})=\cdots=0.

This is the
\alpha
-Jack-positive specialization corresponding to
c=u
, and
a_{i}=b_{i}=0,\ \forall\,i\geq 1
, in Theorem
4.2
.

Example 5
.

For any infinite tuple
\mathbf{v}=(v_{1},v_{2},\cdots)\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
and
u\in\mathbb{R}
, recall that
u\cdot\mathbf{v}:=(uv_{1},uv_{2},\cdots)
.
We claim that if
\mathbf{v}=(v_{1},v_{2},\cdots)\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
determines an

\alpha
-Jack-positive specialization and if
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, then
m\cdot\mathbf{v}
determines
another
\alpha
-Jack-positive specialization. In fact, let
(a,b,c)\in\Omega
,

a=(a_{1},a_{2},\cdots)
,
b=(b_{1},b_{2},\cdots)
, be the triple corresponding
to
\mathbf{v}
, as given by Theorem
4.2
.
Then
m\cdot\mathbf{v}
corresponds to the triple

(a^{\otimes m},b^{\otimes m},m\cdot c)\in\Omega
, where

a^{\otimes m}:=(\underbrace{a_{1},\cdots,a_{1}}_{m\text{ times}},\underbrace{a_{2},\cdots,a_{2}}_{m\text{ times}},a_{3},\cdots),\quad b^{\otimes m}:=(\underbrace{b_{1},\cdots,b_{1}}_{m\text{ times}},\underbrace{b_{2},\cdots,b_{2}}_{m\text{ times}},b_{3},\cdots).

Example 6
.

For any infinite tuple
\mathbf{v}=(v_{1},v_{2},\cdots)\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
, define

\pm\mathbf{v}:=(v_{1},-v_{2},v_{3},-v_{4},\cdots).

We claim that if
\pm\mathbf{v}
determines a

(1/\alpha)
-Jack-positive specialization, and if
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,
then
(\alpha\cdot m)\cdot\mathbf{v}
determines an
\alpha
-Jack-positive specialization.
Recall that the automorphism
\omega_{1/\alpha}
of
\Symm
defined by

\omega_{1/\alpha}(p_{r})=(-1)^{r-1}\alpha^{-1}p_{r}
has the following
property (
17
):

\omega_{1/\alpha}(J^{(1/\alpha)}_{\mu})=\alpha^{-|\mu|}J^{(\alpha)}_{\mu^{\prime}}\quad\forall\,\mu\in\mathbb{Y}.

We show that
J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}((\alpha\cdot m)\cdot\mathbf{v})\geq 0
, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}
.

\displaystyle J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}((\alpha\cdot m)\cdot\mathbf{v})

\displaystyle=J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}(\alpha\cdot m\cdot v_{1},\,\alpha\cdot m\cdot v_{2},\,\alpha\cdot m\cdot v_{3},\,\alpha\cdot m\cdot v_{4},\cdots)

\displaystyle=\alpha^{|\lambda|}\cdot(\omega_{1/\alpha}J_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{(1/\alpha)})(\alpha\cdot m\cdot v_{1},\,\alpha\cdot m\cdot v_{2},\,\alpha\cdot m\cdot v_{3},\,\alpha\cdot m\cdot v_{4},\cdots)

\displaystyle=\alpha^{|\lambda|}\cdot J_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{(1/\alpha)}(m\cdot v_{1},\,-m\cdot v_{2},\,m\cdot v_{3},\,-m\cdot v_{4},\cdots)

\displaystyle=\alpha^{|\lambda|}\cdot J_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{(1/\alpha)}(m\cdot(\pm\mathbf{v})).

Since
\pm\mathbf{v}
determines a
(1/\alpha)
-Jack positive
specialization, and
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, then
m\cdot(\pm\mathbf{v})

determines a
(1/\alpha)
-Jack positive specialization, by Example

5
. Therefore
J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}((\alpha\cdot m)\cdot\mathbf{v})=\alpha^{|\lambda|}\cdot J_{\lambda^{\prime}}^{(1/\alpha)}(m\cdot(\pm\mathbf{v}))\geq 0
, as claimed.

4.2.
Totally Jack-positive specializations

Definition 4.3
.

The specialization
\rho:\Symm\to\mathbb{R}
is a
totally Jack-positive specialization
if

\rho\left(J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}\right)\geq 0,\quad\forall\,\lambda\in\mathbb{Y},\ \forall\,\alpha>0.

Note that the condition is imposed for all
\alpha>0
.

Theorem 4.4

(Classification of totally Jack-positive specializations)
.

The specialization
\rho:\Symm\to\mathbb{R}
is totally Jack-positive if and only if there exist

c\geq 0,\ a_{1}\geq a_{2}\geq\cdots\geq 0
, with
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{a_{i}}\leq c
, such that

\rho(p_{1})=c,\qquad\rho(p_{k})=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{a_{i}^{k}},\quad\forall\,k=2,3,\cdots.

Proof.

Let
\rho:\Symm\to\mathbb{R}
be a specialization of
\Symm
.

Part 1.
In this first part of the proof, assume that
\rho
is a totally Jack-positive specialization, and let us prove the existence of
c,a_{1},a_{2},\cdots\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, satisfying the conditions of the theorem.

Set
c:=\rho(p_{1})
. The Jack symmetric function
J_{(1)}^{(\alpha)}
associated to the
partition
(1)
is
p_{1}
. Since
\rho
is totally Jack-positive,
it follows that
c=\rho(J_{(1)}^{(\alpha)})\geq 0
.
Theorem
4.2
shows that for any
\alpha>0
, there exist reals

a_{1;\,\alpha}\geq a_{2;\,\alpha}\geq\cdots\geq 0,\ b_{1;\,\alpha}\geq b_{2;\,\alpha}\geq\cdots\geq 0
,
with

(91)

c\geq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{(a_{i;\,\alpha}+b_{i;\,\alpha})},

such that

(92)

\rho(p_{k})=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{(a_{i;\,\alpha})^{k}}+(-\alpha)^{1-k}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{(b_{i;\,\alpha})^{k}},\quad\forall\,k\geq 2.

By virtue of inequality (
91
), both sequences
\{a_{i;\,\alpha}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}

and
\{b_{i;\,\alpha}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
are uniformly bounded over all
\alpha>0
.
Thus, using Cantor’s diagonal extraction, we can find a sequence

\{\alpha_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
such that the following limits exist

(93)

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\alpha_{n}}=\infty,\qquad\lim_{n\to\infty}{a_{j;\,\alpha_{n}}}=a_{j},\ \forall j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}.

Fix an arbitrary integer
k\geq 2
.
As each
\{b_{i;\,\alpha_{n}}\}_{i\geq 1}
is a sequence of nonnegative reals whose
sum is bounded above by
c
, we have

0\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{(b_{i;\,\alpha_{n}})^{k}}\leq\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{b_{i;\,\alpha_{n}}}\right)^{k}\leq c^{k},\quad\forall\,n\geq 1.

Consequently, by (
93
),

(94)

\lim_{n\to\infty}{(-\alpha_{n})^{1-k}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{(b_{i;\,\alpha_{n}})^{k}}}=0.

The previous reasoning also shows

0\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{(a_{i;\,\alpha_{n}})^{k}}\leq c^{k}
, for all
n\geq 1
.
Then we can apply the dominated convergence theorem; due to (
93
), we have

(95)

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{(a_{i;\,\alpha_{n}})^{k}}}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{a_{i}^{k}}.

By combining the relations (
92
), (
94
) and (
95
), we conclude

\rho(p_{k})=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{a_{i}^{k}},\quad\forall\,k\geq 2.

Finally, since
a_{1;\,\alpha_{n}}\geq a_{2;\,\alpha_{n}}\geq\cdots\geq 0
, it follows by (
93
) that

a_{1}\geq a_{2}\geq\cdots\geq 0
. Likewise,
c\geq\sum_{i\geq 1}{a_{i;\,\alpha_{n}}}

implies
c\geq\sum_{i\geq 1}{a_{i}}
. This ends the proof of the “only if" direction.

Part 2.

In this second part, we prove the “if” direction of the theorem. Assume that

\rho(p_{1})=c
, and
\rho(p_{k})=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{a_{i}^{k}},\ k\geq 2
,
for some parameters
c\geq 0,\ a_{1}\geq a_{2}\geq\cdots\geq 0
, with

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}{a_{i}}\leq c
.
Then for any
\alpha>0
, we claim that
\rho
is an
\alpha
-Jack-positive specialization.
Indeed, this is an immediate consequence of Theorem
4.2

(the parameters
b_{1},b_{2},\cdots
are all equal to zero).
Hence
\rho
is a totally Jack-positive specialization.
∎

Definition 4.5
.

A pair
(g,\mathbf{v})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
is called an
admissible pair
if one of the following two conditions is satisfied:

•

g>0
, and
\mathbf{v}=(v_{j})_{j\geq 1}
determines a totally Jack-positive specialization.

•

g<0
, and
\pm\mathbf{v}=\left((-1)^{j-1}v_{j}\right)_{j\geq 1}
determines a totally Jack-positive specialization.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
(g,\mathbf{v})
be an admissible pair. Then there exist sequences
(\alpha(d))_{d\geq 1}
,
(u(d))_{d\geq 1}
of positive real numbers such that the refined version of
3.6
is satisfied with
g^{\prime}=0
; explicitly:

(1)

If
g>0
, then we can choose
\alpha(d)=g^{2}d
,
u(d)=\lceil gd\rceil
.

(2)

If
g<0
, then we can choose
\alpha(d)=\frac{1}{g^{2}d}
,
u(d)=\frac{\lceil-gd\rceil}{g^{2}d}
.

Proof.

In both cases, we first observe that the conditions in
(
62
) with
g^{\prime}=0
are readily verified. It remains to verify that
M^{(\alpha(d))}_{u(d);\,\mathbf{v}}(\lambda)\geq 0
, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{Y},\,d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. By
Definition

3.5
of Jack–Thoma measures, the inequality to verify is equivalent to:

(96)

J^{(\alpha(d))}_{\lambda}(u(d)\cdot\mathbf{v})\geq 0,\quad\forall\,\lambda\in\mathbb{Y},\ \forall\,d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}.

Let us begin with case (1). Since
\mathbf{v}
is totally Jack-positive and
u(d)=\lceil gd\rceil\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, then
u(d)\cdot\mathbf{v}
is also totally Jack-positive, by virtue of
Example

5
, and hence (
96
) follows.

In case (2), since
\pm\mathbf{v}
is totally Jack-positive and
\lceil-gd\rceil\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, then
(\alpha(d)\lceil-gd\rceil)\cdot\mathbf{v}
is
\alpha(d)
-Jack-positive, by virtue of
Example

6
. Notice that
\alpha(d)\lceil-gd\rceil=\frac{\lceil-gd\rceil}{g^{2}d}=u(d)
, so the claim just verified is that
u(d)\cdot\mathbf{v}
is
\alpha(d)
-Jack-positive, which proves (
96
).
∎

As a consequence of this proposition, note that Theorems
3.7
and
3.8
can be applied to show limit theorems for the measures
M^{(\alpha(d))}_{u(d);\,\mathbf{v}}
.
In particular, for any admissible pair
(g,\mathbf{v})
, there exists a probability measure
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
with moments given by (
64
).
This proposition also played a role in the construction of the most general examples from Sections
2.6
and
3.2
.

5.
Universal formulas for the global asymptotics of Jack-deformed random Young diagrams

Recall that
Theorem

2.4
states that random Young diagrams
sampled w.r.t. a family of characters
\chi_{d}
with the AFP concentrate
around a limit shape
\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}}
.
In this section, we prove a
universality
result: the formula (
64
)
for the moments of the transition measure
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
arising from the
Jack–Thoma measures also computes the moments of the
transition measure
\mu_{\Lambda_{\infty}}
associated to a general limit shape

\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}}
(and such moments uniquely determine the limit shape).
Further, we prove that the combinatorics of Łukasiewicz ribbon paths from
Section

3
yields universal formulas for the covariances of
Gaussian fluctuations around
\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}}
.

Theorem 5.1

(Universality of the transition measure of the limit shape)
.

Assume that the sequence
\alpha=\alpha(d)
satisfies (
10
),
for some
g,g^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
. Further, assume that
\chi_{d}\colon\mathbb{Y}_{d}\to\mathbb{R}
are Jack characters
such that the sequence
(\chi_{d})_{d\geq 1}
fulfills the AFP and let
v_{2},v_{3},\dots

be the associated parameters given by
Definition

2.2
.
Let
\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}}
be the corresponding limit shape given by

Theorem

2.4
. Then
\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}}
is exactly equal to
\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}
, the limit shape for Jack–Thoma measures obtained in
Theorem

3.7
.
Equivalently, the transition measure
\mu_{\Lambda_{\infty}}
associated to

\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}}
is equal to
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
, and therefore it is
uniquely determined by its moments

(97)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}{x^{\ell}\mu_{\Lambda_{\infty}}(dx)}=\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathbf{L}_{0}(\ell)}\prod_{i\geq 1}{(i\cdot g)^{|\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\rightarrow}^{i}(\Gamma)|}\,v_{i}^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\Gamma)|}},\quad\forall\,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1},

where we set
v_{1}:=1
.

Remark 11
.

In the special case of fixed
\alpha=1
, our formula (
97
) is equivalent to a result of Biane
[
Bia98
,
Bia01
]
that
connects asymptotic representation theory of symmetric groups
and free probability. He proved that
the asymptotic behavior of characters with the AFP evaluated on
long cycles is given by free cumulants of the limiting
transition measure
\mu_{\Lambda_{\infty}}
:

(98)

R_{1}^{\mu_{\Lambda_{\infty}}}=0,\ \ R_{i+1}^{\mu_{\Lambda_{\infty}}}=v_{i},\ \ \ i\geq 1.

If
\alpha=1
is fixed, then we are in the fixed temperature
regime and
g=0
. If we set
v_{0}:=0
, our formula (
97
)
can be rewritten as

(99)

M_{\ell}^{\mu_{\Lambda_{\infty}}}=\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathbf{L}_{0}(\ell)}\prod_{i\geq 0}v_{i}^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\Gamma)|}.

On the other hand, there is a well known relation between the free cumulants
and moments of a probability measure, see e.g.
[
NS06
, Lecture 11]
.
It expresses the moment
M_{\ell}^{\mu_{\Lambda_{\infty}}}
as a polynomial
on the free cumulants
R_{j}^{\mu_{\Lambda_{\infty}}}
, in terms of the
non-crossing set-partitions of
[1\,..\,\ell]
. By using the classical bijection between
non-crossing set-partitions of
[1\,..\,\ell]
and Łukasiewicz paths of length
\ell

(see
[
NS06
, Lecture 9]
), the resulting relations are

(100)

M_{\ell}^{\mu_{\Lambda_{\infty}}}=\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathbf{L}_{0}(\ell)}\prod_{i\geq 0}\left(R_{i+1}^{\mu_{\Lambda_{\infty}}}\right)^{\!|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\Gamma)|}.

By comparing the equations (
99
) and (
100
),
for all
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, we give a new proof of Biane’s result (
98
).

Remark 12
.

In
Section

4
we showed that the Jack–Thoma measures
are parametrized by
\Omega
, and
Footnote

6
shows that
one can construct the associated Jack characters with the AFP with
the same sequence
\mathbf{v}
. It is natural to ask if the
parameters
\mathbf{v}
must be the same in both models. The answer is no — there are Jack characters with the
AFP whose limit shape will not appear as the limit shape of Jack–Thoma measures.
The easiest example is given by the following construction
described by Śniady in
[
Śni06
]
. Let
\alpha=1
, and

\lambda_{d}
be a sequence of Young diagrams of size
d
whose shapes
tend to a fixed limit
\lambda
(i.e.
\omega_{\Lambda^{(\alpha=1)}}\to\omega_{\lambda}
). Then
\chi_{\lambda^{d}}
is a sequence
of characters with the AFP and
\Lambda_{\infty}=\lambda
. Now, one can choose
\lambda
to be any
shape whose moments are not given by the Jack-positive specialization, for instance
\lambda

can be a square of size
1
. We will discuss more examples of Jack
characters with the AFP in the forthcoming work.

In this section, we shall use the notions from
Section

3.6
.
Additionally, it will be useful to define the partition
\mu(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})
that encodes the number of
up steps of each degree in a given Łukasiewicz ribbon path
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}
(recall that the paired up steps are not counted):

\mu(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}):=(1^{|\mathbf{S}_{1}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|},2^{|\mathbf{S}_{2}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|},3^{|\mathbf{S}_{3}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|},\dots).

Moreover, for any partition
\mu
, we use the standard notation
v_{\mu}:=\prod_{i\geq 1}{v_{\mu_{i}}}
.
In particular,

v_{\mu(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})}:=\prod_{j\geq 1}v_{j}^{|\mathbf{S}_{j}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}.

We need some extra notations for the next theorem. If
\Gamma=(w_{0},w_{1},\dots,w_{\ell})
,
\,w_{i}=(i,y_{i})
, is a Łukasiewicz path, denote
\mathbf{S}_{\neq 0}(\Gamma):=\mathbf{S}(\Gamma)\setminus\mathbf{S}_{0}(\Gamma)
the set of vertices
w_{i},\,i\geq 1
, that are not preceded by a horizontal step.
Further, if
s=w_{j}\in\mathbf{S}_{\neq 0}(\Gamma)
, let
\deg(s)\in\{-1,0,1,2,\dots\}
be the difference
y_{j}-y_{j-1}
, i.e. if the step preceding
s
is an up step, then
\deg(s)
is the degree of that up step, and if the step preceding
s
is a down step, then
\deg(s)=-1
.

Theorem 5.2

(Universality of the Gaussian fluctuations around the limit shape)
.

Assume that
\alpha=\alpha(d)
satisfies (
10
), for some
g,g^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
, also assume that
(\chi_{d})_{d\geq 1}
fulfills the enhanced AFP, and let
(v_{k},v_{k}^{\prime})_{k\geq 2},\,(v_{(k|l)})_{k,l\geq 2}
be the associated parameters given by
Definition

2.2
.

Recall that
Theorem

2.5
shows that the random functions
\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{d}=\frac{\sqrt{d}}{2}\cdot(\omega_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}-\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}})
converge to a Gaussian process
\Delta_{\infty}
, in the sense that
\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{d},x^{m}\rangle
converge weakly to
\langle\Delta_{\infty},x^{m}\rangle
, as
d\to\infty
.
Then the means and covariances of polynomial observables of
\Delta_{\infty}
have explicit combinatorial formulas, given by the following polynomials in
g,g^{\prime},\,(v_{k},v_{k}^{\prime})_{k},\,(v_{(k|l)})_{k,l}\,
:

(101)

\mathbb{E}[\langle\Delta_{\infty},\,x^{\ell-2}\rangle]=\frac{1}{\ell-1}\!\left(g^{\prime}\frac{\partial}{\partial g}+\sum_{i\geq 2}{v_{i}^{\prime}\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{i}}}\right)\!\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathbf{L}_{0}(\ell)}{\frac{1}{|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma)|}\prod_{i\geq 1}(i\cdot g)^{|\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\rightarrow}^{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\,v_{i}^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}},

(102)

\Cov\left(\langle\Delta_{\infty},\,x^{k-2}\rangle,\langle\Delta_{\infty},\,x^{l-2}\rangle\right)=\frac{1}{(k-1)(l-1)}\times\\
\times\left(\sum_{\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})\in\mathbf{L}^{\conn}(k,l)\atop|\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|=1}\frac{v_{\mu(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})}}{|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma_{1})|\cdot|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma_{2})|}\cdot\prod_{i\geq 1}(i\cdot g)^{|\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\,i^{\,|\mathbf{P}_{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\,+\right.\\
\left.+\sum_{\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})\in\mathbf{L}(k,l)\atop|\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|=0}\sum_{s_{1}\in\mathbf{S}_{\neq 0}(\Gamma_{1})\atop s_{2}\in\mathbf{S}_{\neq 0}(\Gamma_{2})}v_{(\deg(s_{1})|\deg(s_{2}))}\cdot\frac{v_{\mu(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})\setminus\{\deg(s_{1}),\deg(s_{2})\}}}{|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma_{1})|\cdot|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma_{2})|}\prod_{i\geq 1}(i\cdot g)^{|\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\right),

where
v_{1}:=1
. Both are valid for all
\ell,k,l\geq 2
.
For (
102
), we additionally used the following conventions:

\,v_{(-1|-1)}:=-1
, and
\,v_{(-1|j)}=v_{(j|-1)}=v_{(1|j)}=v_{(j|1)}:=0
, for all
j\geq 1
.

Remark 13
.
