Let us make some clarifications about the last summation in the RHS of (
102
).
Since
v_{(-1|j)}=v_{(j|-1)}=v_{(1|j)}=v_{(j|1)}:=0
, for all
j\geq 1
, the inner sum is over pairs
(s_{1},s_{2})
with
\deg(s_{1}),\,\deg(s_{2})\geq 2
, or pairs with
\deg(s_{1})=\deg(s_{2})=-1
.
In the former case,
v_{(\deg(s_{1})|\deg(s_{2}))}
is obtained from
Eq.

24
of
Definition

2.2
, and the partition
\mu(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})\setminus\{\deg(s_{1}),\deg(s_{2})\}
has exactly two parts less than
\mu(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})
. On the other hand, if
\deg(s_{1})=\deg(s_{2})=-1
, then
v_{(\deg(s_{1})|\deg(s_{2}))}\cdot v_{\mu(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})\setminus\{\deg(s_{1}),\deg(s_{2})\}}=v_{(-1|-1)}\cdot v_{\mu(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})}=-v_{\mu(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})}
.

Remark 14
.

Similarly as
Theorem

5.1
recovers Biane’s result
from (
98
) in the special case of a fixed
\alpha=1
,
Theorem

5.2
recovers Śniady’s result
from
[
Śni06
]
(see also
Remark

6
). In
particular, the special case of our formula for the
covariance (
102
) at
\alpha=1
extends the formula for the case of the Plancherel measure found
in
[
IO02
]
to the general setting of random Young diagrams studied by Śniady
in
[
Śni06
]
. Our formula does not appear
in
[
Śni06
]
, where instead the
formulae for the covariance of free cumulants and characters was
given
[
Śni06
, Eq. (3.6)]
; (
102
) can be used to
recover these formulae.

The proofs consist of two steps. In the first step, we show that the random Young diagrams
sampled by the specific Jack characters constructed in
Footnote

6
are the “depoissonized”
versions of the random Young diagrams sampled with respect to the Jack–Thoma measures,
and prove that they both have the same asymptotic behavior.
The theme of
depoissonization
has been explored many times in the literature,
e.g. see
[
Joh98
,
BOO00
]
.
In the second step, we show that the observables of interest in our model are polynomials in
the variables
v_{k},\,v_{(k|l)}
; this will finish the proof after combining it with the observation
that if two polynomials agree on a large enough set, then the polynomials must coincide.

5.1.
Depoissonization of Jack–Thoma measures

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
\alpha>0,\,d\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
be fixed.
Take any sequence
\mathbf{v}=(1,v_{2},v_{3},\dots)\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
, and let

\chi_{d}:\mathbb{Y}_{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be the function defined by
\chi_{d}(\mu):=v_{\mu}
, for all
\mu\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}
.
Let
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}
be the (signed) measure on
\mathbb{Y}_{d}

associated to
\chi_{d}
, according to
Definition

2.1
.
Recall also that for any
u>0
,
Definition

3.5
gives
M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}

as a (signed) measure on
\mathbb{Y}
.

The measure
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(\cdot)
coincides with the
measure
M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}_{u,\alpha}}(\cdot\,|\,\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}_{d})
,
conditioned on the size of
\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}
, where
u>0
is arbitrary, and

\mathbf{v}_{u,\alpha}:=\left(\left(\frac{u}{\sqrt{\alpha}}\right)^{0}\!,\,v_{2}\cdot\left(\frac{u}{\sqrt{\alpha}}\right)^{1}\!,\,v_{3}\cdot\left(\frac{u}{\sqrt{\alpha}}\right)^{2},\dots\right).

In particular, this is a probability measure if the infinite tuple

(\sqrt{\alpha},\,\sqrt{\alpha}\cdot v_{2},\,\sqrt{\alpha}\cdot v_{3},\dots)

determines an
\alpha
-Jack-positive specialization.

Proof.

Using definition (
14
) of the
irreducible Jack character
\chi^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}
, the fact that the
power sums
\{p_{\mu}\}_{\mu\in\mathbb{Y}}
form an orthogonal basis of
\SSym

with respect to the
\alpha
-deformed Hall scalar product, and formula (
37
)
for the norms of the power sums, we have

\chi^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}(\mu)=\alpha^{-(d-\ell(\mu))/2}\cdot\frac{z_{\mu}}{d!}\cdot\frac{\langle p_{\mu},J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}\rangle}{\langle p_{\mu},p_{\mu}\rangle}=\frac{\alpha^{-(d+\ell(\mu))/2}}{d!}\cdot\langle p_{\mu},J^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}\rangle,

for all
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}
.
Then use this equation and the fact that the Jack symmetric functions
J^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}
form an orthogonal basis with norms given by (
38
) in order to obtain

(103)

p_{\mu}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}}{\frac{\langle p_{\mu},J^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}\rangle}{\langle J^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda},J^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}\rangle}\,J^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}}{\frac{\langle p_{\mu},J^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}\rangle}{j^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}}\,J^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}}=\alpha^{(d+\ell(\mu))/2}\cdot d!\cdot\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}}{\frac{\chi^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}(\mu)}{j^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}}\,J^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}}.

Let
\rho:\SSym\to\mathbb{R}
be the specialization determined by the infinite tuple
u\cdot\mathbf{v}_{u,\alpha}
, i.e.

\rho(p_{k}):=u\cdot(\mathbf{v}_{u,\alpha})_{k}=u\cdot v_{k}\cdot\left(\frac{u}{\sqrt{\alpha}}\right)^{k-1}=v_{k}\cdot u^{k}\cdot\alpha^{(1-k)/2},\quad\forall\,k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1},

where we are setting
v_{1}:=1
.
Then
\rho(p_{\mu})=v_{\mu}\cdot u^{d}\cdot\alpha^{(\ell(\mu)-d)/2}
and, by our usual notation,
\rho(J^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda})=J^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}(u\cdot\mathbf{v}_{u,\alpha})
.
Then applying
\rho
to both sides of
Eq.

103
, using the assumption that
\chi_{d}(\mu)=v_{\mu}
, and rearranging terms we obtain

(104)

\chi_{d}(\mu)=\frac{\alpha^{d}d!}{u^{d}}\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}}\frac{J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}(u\cdot\mathbf{v}_{u,\alpha})}{j^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}}\cdot\chi^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}(\mu).

Recall that the Jack–Thoma measure
M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}_{u,\alpha}}
from
Definition

3.5
is the
Jack measure with specializations
\rho_{1}(p_{k})=u\cdot(\mathbf{v}_{u,\alpha})_{k}
and
\rho_{2}(p_{k})=u\cdot\delta_{1,k}
,
as observed in
Section

3.2.4
.
Thus, as a direct consequence of Cauchy’s identity (
36
), the measure

M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}_{u,\alpha}}
assigns
\mathbb{Y}_{d}\,
a mass of

\,\exp\left(-\frac{u^{2}}{\alpha}\right)\cdot\frac{u^{2d}}{\alpha^{d}d!}
.
Hence, the Jack–Thoma measure, conditioned on the size of
\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}
, is given by:

M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}_{u,\alpha}}(\lambda\,|\,\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}_{d})=\frac{\alpha^{d}d!}{u^{2d}}\cdot\frac{J_{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}(u\cdot\mathbf{v}_{u,\alpha})\cdot u^{d}}{j^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}}.

Plugging back into (
104
) gives

(105)

\chi_{d}(\mu)=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}}M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}_{u,\alpha}}(\lambda\,|\,\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}_{d})\ \chi^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}(\mu).

This last equation is precisely the definition of the measure

\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}
(compare
Definition

2.1
and (
105
)), therefore we conclude the desired equality of measures:
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(\cdot)=M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}_{u,\alpha}}(\cdot\,|\,\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}_{d})
.

Finally, if
(\sqrt{\alpha},\sqrt{\alpha}\cdot v_{2},\sqrt{\alpha}\cdot v_{3},\dots)

determines an
\alpha
-Jack-positive specialization, then so does
u\cdot\mathbf{v}_{u,\alpha}
,
since they both belong to the same ray of the Thoma cone (see
Theorem

4.2
).
Then
Definition

3.5
shows that
M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}_{u,\alpha}}
is a probability
(not just a signed) measure, so the same is true for the conditional version

M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}_{u,\alpha}}(\cdot\,|\,\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}_{d})
. The proof is then finished.
∎

Let
g\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
and
\mathbf{v}=(v_{1},v_{2},\cdots)\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
be such that

(g,\mathbf{v})
is an admissible pair, in the sense of
Definition

4.5
,
with
v_{1}=1
. Motivated by
Footnote

6
, consider the following functions

\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}}\colon\mathbb{Y}_{d}\to\mathbb{R}
:

(106)

\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}}(\mu):=\left(\frac{g^{2}d}{\big\lceil|g|d\big\rceil^{2}}\right)^{\!\frac{\|\mu\|}{2}}\cdot v_{\mu}.

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
(g,\mathbf{v})
be an admissible pair with
v_{1}=1
, and let

(\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}}\colon\mathbb{Y}_{d}\to\mathbb{R})_{d\geq 1}
be the sequence of functions
given by (
106
). Then
\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}}
is a
Jack character when
\alpha
is equal to

(107)

\alpha=\begin{cases}g^{2}d,&\text{ if }g>0,\\
\frac{1}{g^{2}d},&\text{ if }g<0.\end{cases}

This sequence
\alpha=\alpha(d)
satisfies condition
(
10
) with
g^{\prime}=0
.
Moreover, the sequence
(\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}})_{d\geq 1}
satisfies the enhanced AFP with
v_{2},v_{3},\cdots
being the associated paramaters in

Eq.

22
from

Definition

2.2
., while the other parameters are
v_{k}^{\prime}=v_{(k|l)}=0
, for all
k,l\geq 2
.

Let
\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}}
be the limit shape given by
Theorem

2.4
,
and let
\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}
be the limit shape given by
Theorem

3.7
(for Jack–Thoma measures).
Then

\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}}=\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}.

Furthermore, the Gaussian process
\Delta_{\infty}
, given
by
Theorem

2.5
is determined by the explicit combinatorial formulas.

\mathbb{E}[\langle\Delta_{\infty},\,x^{k-2}\rangle]
, for all
k\geq 2
, is equal to the RHS of
Eq.

65
where we set
v_{1}=1
. As for the covariances, we have the following formula, valid for all
k,l\geq 2
:

\Cov\left(\langle\Delta_{\infty},\,x^{k-2}\rangle,\langle\Delta_{\infty},\,x^{l-2}\rangle\right)=\\
\frac{1}{(k-1)(l-1)}\Bigg(\sum_{\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})\in\mathbf{L}^{\conn}(k,l)\atop|\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|=1}\frac{v_{\mu(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})}}{|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma_{1})|\cdot|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma_{2})|}\prod_{i\geq 1}(i\cdot g)^{|\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\,i^{\,|\mathbf{P}_{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\\
-\sum_{\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})\in\mathbf{L}(k,l)\atop|\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|=0}\frac{|\mathbf{S}_{-1}(\Gamma_{1})|\cdot|\mathbf{S}_{-1}(\Gamma_{2})|\cdot v_{\mu(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})}}{|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma_{1})|\cdot|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma_{2})|}\prod_{i\geq 1}(i\cdot g)^{|\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\Bigg).

Proof.

It is evident that the sequence
(\alpha(d))_{d\geq 1}
satisfies the
assumption (
10
).
Let us verify that
\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}}
is a Jack character. Due to

Proposition

2.3
, it is enough to verify it in case
g>0
. Let
u(d):=\lceil gd\rceil
.
As usual, denote
\alpha:=\alpha(d),\,u:=u(d)
.

Proposition

4.6
shows that
M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}
is a probability measure.
On the other hand, if we set
v^{(d)}_{k}:=(\sqrt{\alpha}/u)^{k-1}\cdot v_{k}
,
then the function
\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}}
can be expressed as

(108)

\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}}(\mu)=v^{(d)}_{\mu},\quad\textrm{for all }\mu\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}.

Then
Proposition

5.3
implies that the measure

\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}}}(\cdot)
associated to
\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}}
is equal
to the conditional probability measure
M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}(\cdot\,|\,\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}_{d})
;
therefore
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}}}

is also a probability measure, and consequently
\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}}
is a Jack character.

To check that
(\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}})_{d\geq 1}
satisfies the enhanced AFP,
note
that each
\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}}
is multiplicative, meaning that

\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}}(\mu^{1}\cdots\mu^{k})=\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}}(\mu^{1})\cdots\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}}(\mu^{k})
.
Therefore all cumulants
\kappa_{n}^{\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}}}(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})

with
n\geq 2
vanish, in particular, (
24
) holds with

v_{(k|l)}=0
. Recall from
Section

2.4
that in order
to make sense of the cumulants
\kappa_{n}^{\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}}}(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})
,
for
k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n}<d
, we need to add an appropriate number of

1
’s to construct partitions of size
d
. In particular,

\kappa_{1}^{\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}}}(k)\cdot d^{\frac{k-1}{2}}=\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}}(k,1^{d-k})\cdot d^{\frac{k-1}{2}}=v_{k}^{(d)}(v_{1}^{(d)})^{d-k}\cdot d^{\frac{k-1}{2}}=(\sqrt{\alpha d}/u)^{k-1}\cdot v_{k},

where the second equality is due to (
108
). Therefore

\kappa_{1}^{\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}}}(k)\cdot d^{\frac{k-1}{2}}=v_{k}+o(d^{-1/2}),

for all
k\geq 2
, because
\sqrt{\alpha d}/u=1+o(d^{-1/2})
for our choices of
\alpha,\,u
. Then (
23
) is satisfied with
v_{k}^{\prime}=0
.

Denote the expectation w.r.t. the conditional probability measure
M^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}(\cdot\,|\,\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}_{d})
by
\mathbb{E}^{\alpha;d}_{u;\mathbf{v}}
. We can compute

\mathbb{E}^{\alpha,d}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(B_{\ell_{1}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\cdots B_{\ell_{n}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\right)
in the same way we
computed
\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(B_{\ell_{1}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\cdots B_{\ell_{n}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\right)

in the proof of
Theorem

3.10
, except that

\exp\!\left(-\frac{u^{2}}{\alpha}\right)
and
\exp\!\left(\frac{u\cdot q_{1}}{\alpha}\right)

should be replaced by
d!\!\cdot\!\left(\frac{\alpha}{u^{2}}\right)^{d}
and

\left(\frac{u}{\alpha}\right)^{d}\!\cdot\!\frac{q_{1}^{d}}{d!}
, respectively.
This gives

(109)

\mathbb{E}^{\alpha;d}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(B_{\ell_{1}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\cdots B_{\ell_{n}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\right)=\\
\sum_{\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}\in\mathbf{L}(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n})\atop|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|=n}d_{\overline{|\mathbf{S}_{-1}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}}\cdot\bigg(\frac{\alpha}{u^{2}}\bigg)^{\!|\mathbf{S}_{-1}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\cdot{\prod_{i\geq 1}\bigg(i\cdot\frac{\alpha-1}{u}\bigg)^{\!|\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\bigg(\frac{i\alpha}{u^{2}}\bigg)^{\!|\mathbf{P}_{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\,v_{i}^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}},

where
d_{\overline{k}}:=d(d-1)\cdots(d-k+1)
is the falling factorial.
Since
d_{\overline{|\mathbf{S}_{-1}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}}=d^{|\mathbf{S}_{-1}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}(1+O(d^{-1}))
,
\,\frac{\alpha-1}{u}=g(1+O(d^{-1}))
, and
\,\frac{\alpha}{u^{2}}=d^{-1}(1+O(d^{-1}))
,
then the asymptotics of (
109
) is

(110)

\mathbb{E}^{\alpha;d}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(B_{\ell_{1}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\cdots B_{\ell_{n}}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\right)=B^{\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}}_{\ell_{1}}\cdots B_{\ell_{n}}^{\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}}(1+O(d^{-1})).

Recall that
\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}=T_{\sqrt{\alpha/d},\,1/\sqrt{\alpha d}}{\,\lambda}\,
and

\,\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)}=T_{\alpha/u,\,1/u}{\,\lambda}
, for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}
. Then

\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}=T_{u/\sqrt{\alpha d},\,u/\sqrt{\alpha d}}{\,\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)}}
,
so that the scaling property (
52
) implies

B_{\ell}(\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d})=\left(\frac{u}{\sqrt{\alpha d}}\right)^{\!\ell}\cdot B_{\ell}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)}),\ \text{ for all }\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}.

Since
\frac{u}{\sqrt{\alpha d}}=1+O(d^{-1})
in our case,

Eq.

110
shows

\lim_{d\to\infty}\mathbb{E}^{\alpha;d}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(B_{\ell_{1}}(\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d})\cdots B_{\ell_{n}}(\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d})\right)=B_{\ell_{1}}^{\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}}\cdots B_{\ell_{n}}^{\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}}.

By
Proposition

3.2
, it follows that for any
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, we have

(111)

\lim_{d\to\infty}\mathbb{E}^{\alpha;d}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(M_{\ell}(\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d})\right)=M_{\ell}^{\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}}.

Recall that the transition measure
\mu_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}
of
\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}
has moments
M_{\ell}(\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d})
.
Since
\mu_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}
is a random measure, we can define the average
\mathbb{E}^{\alpha;d}_{u;\mathbf{v}}[\mu_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}]
, or simply
\mathbb{E}[\mu_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}]
; this is a deterministic probability measure with moments
\mathbb{E}^{\alpha;d}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(M_{\ell}(\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d})\right)
.
The limits (
111
) then show the convergence
\mathbb{E}[\mu_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}]\to\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
in the sense of moments. But since
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
is uniquely determined by its moments, as shown in
Proposition

6.1
, then the convergence
\mathbb{E}[\mu_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}]\to\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
holds weakly, by virtue of
Theorem

3.14
.

On the other hand,
Theorem

2.4
proves that the profiles
\omega_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}
converge to the limit shape
\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}}
uniformly, in probability. Because of the homeomorphism given by the Markov-Krein correspondence, namely
Theorem

3.3
, the previous convergence implies
\mu_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}\to\mu_{\Lambda_{\infty}}
weakly, in probability. This implies the weak convergence
\mathbb{E}[\mu_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}]\to\mu_{\Lambda_{\infty}}
. But we already knew that
\mathbb{E}[\mu_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}]\to\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
weakly, therefore
\mu_{\Lambda_{\infty}}=\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
. By the Markov-Krein correspondence, we conclude
\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}}=\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}
, as desired.

Next compute
\Cov(\langle\Delta_{\infty},x^{k-2}\rangle,\langle\Delta_{\infty},x^{l-2}\rangle)=\lim_{d\to\infty}{\Cov^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}}}(\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{d},x^{k-2}\rangle,\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{d},x^{l-2}\rangle)}
.
We follow the same ideas as in
Theorem

3.8
, but now use (
109
) instead of the formula from
Theorem

3.10
.
The result will differ slightly because of the asymptotic identity:

d_{\overline{k+l}}-d_{\overline{k}}\cdot d_{\overline{l}}=-k\cdot l\cdot d^{k+l-1}+O(d^{k+l+2})
.
This implies that the limiting covariance will be the same as the one found in
Theorem

3.8
, but corrected by the term which comes from the limit

\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{d_{\overline{|\mathbf{S}_{-1}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}}\,-\,d_{\overline{|\mathbf{S}_{-1}(\Gamma_{1})|}}\cdot d_{\overline{|\mathbf{S}_{-1}(\Gamma_{2})|}}}{d^{|\mathbf{S}_{-1}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}}=-|\mathbf{S}_{-1}(\Gamma_{1})||\mathbf{S}_{-1}(\Gamma_{2})|

that accompanies the Łukasiewicz ribbon paths
\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}
without pairings, i.e. with
\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})=\emptyset
:

(k-1)(l-1)\cdot\lim_{d\to\infty}\!\Cov^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}}}\!\left(\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{d},x^{k-2}\rangle,\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{d},x^{l-2}\rangle\right)=\\
=\lim_{d\to\infty}{\!d\cdot\Cov^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}}}\!\left(S_{k}(\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}),S_{l}(\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d})\right)}=\lim_{d\to\infty}d\cdot\left(\frac{u}{\sqrt{\alpha\cdot d}}\right)^{\!\!k+l}\times\\
\times\left\{\mathbb{E}^{\alpha;d}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(S_{k}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})S_{l}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\right)-\mathbb{E}^{\alpha;d}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(S_{k}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\right)\cdot\mathbb{E}^{\alpha;d}_{u;\mathbf{v}}\left(S_{l}(\Lambda_{(\alpha;u)})\right)\!\right\}\\
=\sum_{\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})\in\mathbf{L}^{\conn}(k,l)\atop|\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|=1}\frac{v_{\mu(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})}}{|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma_{1})|\cdot|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma_{2})|}\,\prod_{i\geq 1}(i\cdot g)^{|\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\,i^{\,|\mathbf{P}_{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\\
-\sum_{\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})\in\mathbf{L}(k,l)\atop|\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|=0}\frac{|\mathbf{S}_{-1}(\Gamma_{1})|\cdot|\mathbf{S}_{-1}(\Gamma_{2})|\cdot v_{\mu(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})}}{|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma_{1})|\cdot|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma_{2})|}\,\prod_{i\geq 1}(i\cdot g)^{|\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}.

The calculation of
\mathbb{E}[\langle\Delta_{\infty},\,x^{k-2}\rangle]=\lim_{d\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}}}[\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{d},x^{k-2}\rangle]}
follows the same ideas as in
Theorem

3.8
, and in this case one obtains exactly the RHS of
Eq.

65
, but with
v_{1}=1
.
∎

5.2.
The algebra of
\alpha
-polynomial functions and universality of formulas

5.2.1.
The algebra
\mathscr{P}
of
\alpha
-polynomial functions

We will use the following notations:

\displaystyle\gamma:=-\sqrt{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha}},

\displaystyle X^{(\alpha)}_{\ell}(\lambda):=X_{\ell}(T_{\sqrt{\alpha},\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha}}}\lambda),\quad\forall\,X=M,B,R,S,\ \ \forall\,\lambda\in\mathbb{Y},\ \ \forall\,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}.

We will treat each
X^{(\alpha)}_{\ell}
as a real-valued function on
\mathbb{Y}
.
Note that
X^{(\alpha)}_{1}\equiv 0
is identically zero, for all
X=M,B,R,S
.
The following polynomial algebra is called the
algebra of
\alpha
-polynomial functions
:

\mathscr{P}:=\mathbb{Q}[\gamma,R^{(\alpha)}_{2},R^{(\alpha)}_{3},\dots].

Any element
x\in\mathscr{P}
will be called an
\alpha
-polynomial function
(or just a
polynomial function, for short), and it will be regarded as a function on
\mathbb{Y}
.
The product in
\mathscr{P}
is given by (pointwise) multiplication of functions;
if
x,y\in\mathscr{P}
, their product is denoted
x\times y
, or simply
xy
.

Using (
45
)–(
48
), it is not
difficult to see that

\mathscr{P}=\mathbb{Q}[\gamma,M^{(\alpha)}_{2},M^{(\alpha)}_{3},\dots]=\mathbb{Q}[\gamma,B^{(\alpha)}_{2},B^{(\alpha)}_{3},\dots]=\mathbb{Q}[\gamma,S^{(\alpha)}_{2},S^{(\alpha)}_{3},\dots].

In particular, if we let
X^{(\alpha)}_{\mu}:=\prod_{i\geq 1}{X^{(\alpha)}_{\mu_{i}}}
, for all
X=M,R,B,S
and
\mu\in\mathbb{Y}
, then we can write
\mathscr{P}=\bigoplus_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}{V_{\ell}}=\bigoplus_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}{\tilde{V}_{\ell}}
, where
V_{\ell},\,\tilde{V}_{\ell}
are the following subspaces:

(112)

V_{\ell}:=\Span_{\mathbb{Q}}\{\gamma^{k}\cdot X^{(\alpha)}_{\mu}\colon|\mu|=\ell,k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\},\quad\tilde{V}_{\ell}:=\Span_{\mathbb{Q}}\{\gamma^{k}\cdot X^{(\alpha)}_{\mu}\colon k+|\mu|=\ell\}.

Note that because of
Proposition

3.2
, the definitions (
112
) of
V_{\ell},\,\tilde{V}_{\ell}
are the same regardless of whether we use
M,R,B
or
S
for
X
. We say that a nonzero
x\in V_{\ell}
is homogenous of degree
\deg_{1}(x)=\ell
, and a nonzero
x\in\tilde{V}_{\ell}
is homogenous of degree
\deg_{2}(x)=\ell
.

It will be important for us that
\mathscr{P}
is linearly generated (over
\mathbb{Q}[\gamma]
)
by the following so-called
normalized Jack characters
.

Definition 5.5
.

Let
\alpha>0
and
\mu\in\mathbb{Y}
be given.
For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{Y}
, the value of the
normalized Jack character

\Ch_{\mu}^{(\alpha)}(\lambda)
is given by:

(113)

\Ch_{\mu}^{(\alpha)}(\lambda):=\begin{cases}|\lambda|_{\overline{|\mu|}}\cdot\chi^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}(\mu),&\text{if }|\lambda|\geq|\mu|,\\
0,&\text{if }|\lambda|<|\mu|,\end{cases}

where
\chi^{(\alpha)}_{\lambda}(\mu)
is the irreducible Jack character
(
14
) understood with
the convention (
19
).
In particular, for the empty partition
\mu=\emptyset
, the corresponding
normalized Jack character is the constant function
\Ch_{\emptyset}^{(\alpha)}\equiv 1
, due to (
15
).

Theorem 5.6

(
[
DF16
]
)
.

We have:

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}=\Span_{\mathbb{Q}}

\displaystyle\{\gamma^{k}\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{\mu}\mid k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\,\mu\in\mathbb{Y}\},

\displaystyle\deg_{1}\left(\Ch_{\mu}^{(\alpha)}-\prod_{i=1}^{\ell(\mu)}R^{(\alpha)}_{\mu_{i}+1}\right)

\displaystyle<|\mu|+\ell(\mu),

\displaystyle\Ch_{\mu}^{(\alpha)}

\displaystyle\in\bigoplus^{\lfloor\frac{\mu+\ell(\mu)}{2}\rfloor}_{i=0}\tilde{V}_{|\mu|+\ell(\mu)-2i}.

Note that
Theorem

5.6
implies that
\mathscr{P}=\mathbb{Q}[\gamma,\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{(1)},\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{(2)},\dots]
.
We define also a different algebra structure on the set
\mathscr{P}
, defined on its basis by

\gamma^{k}\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{\mu}\bullet\gamma^{l}\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{\nu}:=\gamma^{k+l}\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{\mu\cdot\nu},

and extended by linearity. Here,
\mu\cdot\nu
is the product of
two partitions
\mu,\nu\in\mathbb{Y}
, as defined in
Section

2.2
. We
denote this algebra by
\mathscr{P}^{\bullet}
.

Let
\chi_{d}:\mathbb{Y}_{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be a Jack character with associated probability measure

\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}
given by
Definition

2.1
; we denote the expectation w.r.t. this
measure by
\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}
.
Each polynomial function
x\in\mathscr{P}
can be restricted to the subset
\mathbb{Y}_{d}\subset\mathbb{Y}

of Young diagrams of size
d
, therefore
x
can be treated as
a random variable on
\mathbb{Y}_{d}
, so it makes sense to compute the
expectation
\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(x)
. In particular, from the definition of
the normalized Jack character, one has

(114)

\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{\mu})=d_{\overline{|\mu|}}\cdot\chi_{d}(\mu),\ \text{ for all }\mu\in\mathbb{Y}.

More generally, for any polynomial functions
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathscr{P}
, we can define the cumulants

\kappa^{\chi,\times}_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
and

\kappa^{\chi,\bullet}_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
by the same equation
(
11
) used previously, but replacing
\chi
by
\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi}
,
and the product of partitions by the products
\times
and
\bullet
, respectively:

\displaystyle\kappa^{\chi,\times}_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

\displaystyle:=\left.\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial t_{1}\cdots\partial t_{n}}\log\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi}\left(\exp_{\times}(t_{1}x_{1}+\cdots+t_{n}x_{n})\right)\right|_{t_{1}=\cdots=t_{n}=0},

\displaystyle\kappa^{\chi,\bullet}_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})

\displaystyle:=\left.\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial t_{1}\cdots\partial t_{n}}\log\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi}\left(\exp_{\bullet}(t_{1}x_{1}+\cdots+t_{n}x_{n})\right)\right|_{t_{1}=\cdots=t_{n}=0},

where

\exp_{\times}(x):=\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{\overbrace{x\times x\cdots\times x}^{n\text{ times}}}{n!},\qquad\exp_{\bullet}(x):=\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{\overbrace{x\bullet x\bullet\cdots\bullet x}^{n\text{ times}}}{n!}.

The following equivalent characterization of the AFP will be important.

Theorem 5.7

(Theorem 2.3 from
[
DS19
]
)
.

Let
(\chi_{d}:\mathbb{Y}_{d}\to\mathbb{R})_{d\geq 1}
be any sequence of Jack characters.
The following conditions are equivalent:

(
A
)

for each integer
n\geq 1
, and all integers
k_{1},\dots,k_{n}\geq 2
, the limit

\lim_{d\to\infty}\kappa^{\chi_{d}}_{n}(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})\ d^{\frac{k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n}+n-2}{2}}

exists and is finite;

(
B
)

for each integer
n\geq 1
, and all
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathscr{P}
, the limit

\lim_{d\to\infty}\kappa^{\chi_{d},\times}_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\ d^{-\frac{\deg_{2}(x_{1})+\cdots+\deg_{2}(x_{n})-2(n-1)}{2}}

exists and is finite;

(
C
)

for each integer
n\geq 1
, and all
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathscr{P}^{\bullet}
, the limit

\lim_{d\to\infty}\kappa^{\chi_{d},\bullet}_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\ d^{-\frac{\deg_{2}(x_{1})+\cdots+\deg_{2}(x_{n})-2(n-1)}{2}}

exists and is finite.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
\alpha=\alpha(d)
be a sequence of positive reals such that
(
10
) is satisfied, for some

g\in\mathbb{R}
. Also, let
\chi_{d}\colon\mathbb{Y}_{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be Jack characters such
that the sequence
(\chi_{d})_{d\geq 1}
fulfills the AFP (enhanced AFP, respectively) and
let
v_{2},v_{3},\dots,
(and
v_{2}^{\prime},v_{3}^{\prime},\dots
,respectively) be the associated parameters given by
Definition

2.2
.
Suppose that
x\in\tilde{V}_{\ell}\oplus\bigoplus_{i\leq\ell-2}\tilde{V}_{i}
.
Then the following conditions hold true.

•

There exists a homogenous polynomial

P_{x}\in\mathbb{Q}[x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell}]
of degree
\ell
, where

\deg(x_{i}):=i
, such that

\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(x)=P_{x}(g,v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell-1})\bigg|_{v_{1}=1}\cdot d^{\frac{\ell}{2}}+o(d^{\frac{\ell}{2}}),\text{ if }(\chi_{d})_{d\geq 1}\,\text{ fulfills the AFP, }

and

\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(x)=P_{x}(g,v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell-1})\bigg|_{v_{1}=1}\!\cdot d^{\frac{\ell}{2}}\\
+\left(g^{\prime}\frac{\partial}{\partial g}+\sum_{i\geq 2}{v_{i}^{\prime}\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{i}}}\right)\!P_{x}(g,v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell-1})\bigg|_{v_{1}=1}\!\cdot d^{\frac{\ell-1}{2}}\\
+o(d^{\frac{\ell-1}{2}}),\text{ if }(\chi_{d})_{d\geq 1}\,\text{ fulfills the enhanced AFP. }

•

Expand
P_{x}
in monomials:

(115)

P_{x}=\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{\ell}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\atop m_{1}+2m_{2}+\cdots+\ell m_{\ell}=\ell}a_{m_{1},\dots,m_{\ell}}\,x_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdots x_{\ell}^{m_{\ell}}.

Then

x-\!\!\sum_{m_{1},\dots,m_{\ell}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\atop m_{1}+2m_{2}+\cdots+\ell m_{\ell}=\ell}a_{m_{1},\dots,m_{\ell}}\cdot(-\gamma)^{m_{1}}\cdot\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{(1^{m_{2}},\dots,(\ell-1)^{m_{\ell}})}\in\bigoplus_{i\leq\ell-2}\tilde{V}_{i}.

Proof.

Step 1.
For any
\mu\in\mathbb{Y}
, we claim that

(116)

\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{\mu})-\prod_{i=1}^{\ell(\mu)}{\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{(\mu_{i})})}=O\left(d^{\frac{|\mu|+\ell(\mu)-2}{2}}\right).

Let
r:=\ell(\mu)
. The moment-cumulant formula (
70
)
shows the “
\bullet
-moment”
\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{\mu})=\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{(\mu_{1})}\bullet\cdots\bullet\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{(\mu_{r})})

is given by the formula

\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{\mu})=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}([1\,..\,r])}{\prod_{B\in\pi}\kappa^{\chi_{d},\bullet}_{|B|}\left(\left\{\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{(\mu_{i})}:i\in B\right\}\right)},

where the sum is over set-partitions of
[1\,..\,r]
.
The term associated to
\pi=\{\{1\},\cdots,\{r\}\}
is

\prod_{i=1}^{r}{\kappa_{1}^{\chi_{d},\bullet}(\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{(\mu_{i})})}=\prod_{i=1}^{r}{\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{(\mu_{i})})}
.
Then our claim (
116
) will be proved if we verify that

(117)

\prod_{B\in\pi}{\kappa^{\chi_{d},\bullet}_{|B|}\left(\left\{\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{(\mu_{i})}:i\in B\right\}\right)}=O\left(d^{\frac{|\mu|+\ell(\mu)-2}{2}}\right),

for all
\pi\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}([r])
,
\pi\neq\{\{1\},\cdots,\{r\}\}
.

Theorem

5.6
shows that
\deg_{2}(\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{(\mu_{i})})=\mu_{i}+1
,
therefore
Theorem

5.7
implies that

\kappa^{\chi_{d},\bullet}_{|B|}\left(\{\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{(\mu_{i})}:i\in B\}\right)\cdot d^{-\frac{\sum_{i\in B}{(\mu_{i}+1)}-2(|B|-1)}{2}}=O(1)
, as
d\to\infty
.
The reader can check that this estimate proves the desired (
117
).

Step 2.
Set
v_{1}:=1
. For any
\mu=(1^{p_{1}},2^{p_{2}},\cdots)\in\mathbb{Y}
, we claim

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{\mu})=

\displaystyle\,(v_{1}^{p_{1}}\cdot v_{2}^{p_{2}}\cdots)\cdot d^{\frac{|\mu|+\ell(\mu)}{2}}+o(d^{\frac{|\mu|+\ell(\mu)}{2}}),\text{ if }(\chi_{d})_{d\geq 1}\,\text{ fulfills the AFP, }

(118)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{\mu})=

\displaystyle\,(v_{1}^{p_{1}}\cdot v_{2}^{p_{2}}\cdots)\cdot d^{\frac{|\mu|+\ell(\mu)}{2}}+\left(\sum_{i\geq 2}{v_{i}^{\prime}\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{i}}}\right)(v_{1}^{p_{1}}\cdot v_{2}^{p_{2}}\cdots)\cdot d^{\frac{|\mu|+\ell(\mu)-1}{2}}

\displaystyle+o(d^{\frac{|\mu|+\ell(\mu)-1}{2}}),\text{ if }(\chi_{d})_{d\geq 1}\,\text{ fulfills the enhanced AFP.}

We prove (
118
) only when
\chi_{d}
fulfills
the enhanced AFP, since the case of AFP is a simplified version of
this proof.

From (
114
) and the enhanced AFP, we have that for any integer
k\geq 2
:

\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{(k)})=d_{\overline{k}}\cdot\chi_{d}(k)=\chi_{d}(k)\cdot d^{\frac{k-1}{2}}\cdot(d^{\frac{k+1}{2}}+O(d^{\frac{k-1}{2}}))=v_{k}\cdot d^{\frac{k+1}{2}}+(v^{\prime}_{k}+o(1))\cdot d^{\frac{k}{2}}.

For
k=1
, we have
\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{(1)})\cdot d^{-1}=\chi_{d}(1)=1=v_{1}
.
Consequently,

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{\ell(\mu)}\!\left(\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{(\mu_{i})})\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{\ell(\mu)}\!\left(v_{\mu_{i}}\cdot d^{\frac{\mu_{i}+1}{2}}+(v^{\prime}_{\mu_{i}}+o(1))\cdot d^{\frac{\mu_{i}}{2}}\right)

(119)

\displaystyle=(v_{1}^{p_{1}}\cdot v_{2}^{p_{2}}\cdots)\cdot d^{\frac{|\mu|+\ell(\mu)}{2}}+\left(\sum_{i\geq 2}{v_{i}^{\prime}\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{i}}}\right)\!(v_{1}^{p_{1}}\cdot v_{2}^{p_{2}}\cdots)\cdot d^{\frac{|\mu|+\ell(\mu)-1}{2}}+o(d^{\frac{|\mu|+\ell(\mu)-1}{2}}).

The desired (
118
) then follows from
(
119
) and the conclusion (
116
) from Step 1.

Step 3.
Set
v_{1}:=1
. For any
p\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and

\mu=(1^{p_{1}},2^{p_{2}},\cdots)\in\mathbb{Y}
, we claim that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}((-\gamma)^{p}\cdot\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{\mu})

\displaystyle=g^{p}(v_{1}^{p_{1}}\cdot v_{2}^{p_{2}}\cdots)\!\cdot\!d^{\frac{p+|\mu|+\ell(\mu)}{2}}\!+o(d^{\frac{p+|\mu|+\ell(\mu)}{2}}),\text{ if }(\chi_{d})_{d\geq 1}\text{ fulfills AFP },

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}((-\gamma)^{p}\cdot\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{\mu})

\displaystyle=g^{p}(v_{1}^{p_{1}}\cdot v_{2}^{p_{2}}\cdots)\!\cdot\!d^{\frac{p+|\mu|+\ell(\mu)}{2}}\!+\left(g^{\prime}\frac{\partial}{\partial g}+\sum_{i\geq 2}{v_{i}^{\prime}\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{i}}}\right)g^{p}(v_{1}^{p_{1}}\cdot v_{2}^{p_{2}}\cdots)

(120)

\displaystyle\cdot d^{\frac{p+|\mu|+\ell(\mu)-1}{2}}

\displaystyle+o(d^{\frac{p+|\mu|+\ell(\mu)-1}{2}}),\text{ if }(\chi_{d})_{d\geq 1}\text{ fulfills enhanced AFP. }

As before, we focus on the case of enhanced AFP. Then this follows from (
118
) in Step 2, and the fact that

\frac{-\gamma}{\sqrt{d}}=\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha}}}{\sqrt{d}}=g+g^{\prime}\cdot d^{-\frac{1}{2}}+o(d^{-\frac{1}{2}})
, as
d\to\infty
.

Step 4.
Recall that
\{\gamma^{p}\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{\mu}\mid p\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\,\mu\in\mathbb{Y}\}

is a
\mathbb{Q}
-basis of
\mathscr{P}
. Since
x\in\tilde{V}_{\ell}\oplus\bigoplus_{i\leq\ell-2}\tilde{V}_{i}
, then
Theorem

5.6
implies
that there exist
a_{m_{1},\dots,m_{\ell}}\in\mathbb{Q}
such that

x-\sum_{m_{1}+2m_{2}+\cdots+\ell m_{\ell}=\ell}a_{m_{1},\dots,m_{\ell}}\cdot(-\gamma)^{m_{1}}\cdot\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{(1^{m_{2}},\dots,(\ell-1)^{m_{\ell}})}\in\bigoplus_{i\leq\ell-2}\tilde{V}_{i}.

Note that
\deg_{2}((-\gamma)^{p}\cdot\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{\mu})=p+|\mu|+\ell(\mu)
,
therefore (
120
) implies that for any
y\in\bigoplus_{i\leq\ell-2}\tilde{V}_{i}
one
has
\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(y)=o(d^{\frac{\ell-1}{2}})
, and moreover:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(x)=P_{x}(g,v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell-1})\cdot d^{\frac{\ell}{2}}

\displaystyle+o(d^{\frac{\ell}{2}}),\text{ if }(\chi_{d})_{d\geq 1}\,\text{ fulfills the AFP,}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(x)=P_{x}(g,v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell-1})\cdot d^{\frac{\ell}{2}}

\displaystyle+\left(g^{\prime}\frac{\partial}{\partial g}+\sum_{i\geq 2}{v_{i}^{\prime}\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{i}}}\right)P_{x}(g,v_{1},\dots,v_{\ell-1})\cdot d^{\frac{\ell-1}{2}}

\displaystyle+o(d^{\frac{\ell-1}{2}}),\text{ if }(\chi_{d})_{d\geq 1}\,\text{ fulfills the enhanced AFP,}

where
P_{x}
is defined by (
115
). This finishes the proof.
∎

Corollary 5.9
.

Let
\alpha=\alpha(d)
be a sequence of positive reals such that (
10
) is satisfied, for some
g\in\mathbb{R}
. Also, let
\chi_{d}\colon\mathbb{Y}_{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be Jack characters such that the sequence
(\chi_{d})_{d\geq 1}
fulfills the AFP and let
v_{2},v_{3},\dots
be the associated parameters given by
Definition

2.2
.
Let
\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}}
be the corresponding limit shape given by
Theorem

2.4
, and let
\mu_{\Lambda_{\infty}}
be its transition measure.

Then the moment
\int_{\mathbb{R}}{x^{\ell}\mu_{\Lambda_{\infty}}(dx)}
is a
polynomial in
g,v_{2},\dots,v_{\ell-1}
.

Proof.

Let
\mu_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}
be the transition measure of
\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}=T_{\sqrt{\frac{\alpha}{d}},\,\sqrt{\frac{1}{\alpha d}}}\lambda
. If
\lambda
is
\mathbb{P}_{\chi_{d}}
-distributed, then
\mu_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}
is a random probability measure, but the expectation
\mathbb{E}_{\chi_{d}}[\mu_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}]
is a deterministic probability measure with moments
\mathbb{E}_{\chi_{d}}[M_{\ell}(\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d})]
.
We shall need two facts:

\bullet
The convergence
\mathbb{E}_{\chi_{d}}[\mu_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}]\to\mu_{\Lambda_{\infty}}
holds in the sense of moments, as
d\to\infty
.

In the proof of
Theorem

5.4
, we argued that
\mathbb{E}_{\chi_{d}}[\mu_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}]\to\mu_{\Lambda_{\infty}}
weakly. In general, weak convergence does not imply convergence of moments. However, this is true if the sequences of moments
\{\mathbb{E}_{\chi_{d}}[M_{\ell}(\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d})]\}_{d}
are uniformly bounded (since this would imply uniform integrability).
In
[
DS19
, (5.5)]
, it is shown that the all limits of the form
\lim_{d\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}_{\chi_{d}}[S_{k_{1}}(\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d})\cdots S_{k_{n}}(\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d})]}
exist and are finite.
Then by
Proposition

3.2
, all limits
\lim_{d\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}_{\chi_{d}}[M_{\ell}(\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d})]}
also exist and are finite; this implies the desired boundedness.

\bullet

M_{\ell}(\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d})=M_{\ell}(T_{\sqrt{\frac{\alpha}{d}},\,\sqrt{\frac{1}{\alpha d}}}\lambda)=d^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\cdot M_{\ell}(T_{\sqrt{\alpha},\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha}}}\lambda)=d^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\cdot M^{(\alpha)}_{\ell}(\lambda)
.

This is a result of the scaling property (
52
).

Putting these facts together gives:

(121)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}{x^{\ell}\mu_{\Lambda_{\infty}}(dx)}=\lim_{d\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}\left[M_{\ell}(\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d})\right]}=\lim_{d\to\infty}{d^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\cdot\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}\left[M^{(\alpha)}_{\ell}\right]},

for any
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.
The proof is then finished by
Lemma

5.8
applied to
x=M^{(\alpha)}_{\ell}
.
∎

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, and
P,Q\in\mathbb{R}[x_{1},\dots,x_{\ell}]
be polynomials such that

(122)

P(u_{1},\dots,u_{\ell})=Q(u_{1},\dots,u_{\ell}),

for all
(u_{1},\dots,u_{\ell})
belonging to an open subset
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
. Then
P=Q
.

In particular, if (
122
) holds whenever
u_{1}=g,\,u_{k}=\left(a_{1}^{k}+\dots+a_{\ell-1}^{k}\right),\ k=2,\dots,\ell
,
for all
a_{1}>\dots>a_{\ell-1}>0
,
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell-1}a_{i}<1
, and
g\in W
, for some open set
W\subset\mathbb{R}
, then
P=Q
.

Proof.

The first statement claims that if the
\ell
-variate polynomial
P-Q
vanishes on an
open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
, then it must be the zero polynomial: this is a well-known fact.
For the second statement, note that the map
\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\to\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
given by

(123)

(g,a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{\ell-1})\mapsto\left(g,\sum_{i=1}^{\ell-1}{a_{i}^{2}},\ \sum_{i=1}^{\ell-1}{a_{i}^{3}},\cdots,\ \sum_{i=1}^{\ell-1}{a_{i}^{\ell}}\right),

has nonzero Jacobian whenever all
a_{i}
’s are distinct and nonzero, in particular,
for all points in the open set
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
of points defined by the inequalities
\ell^{-1}>a_{1}>\dots>a_{\ell-1}>0
.
Note that this set
V
is contained in the set described in the second statement of the lemma (the condition
\ell^{-1}>a_{1}
ensures that
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell-1}a_{i}<1
),
and it is mapped into an open subset
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{\ell}

by the map (
123
). As a result, the first statement can be applied and implies that
P=Q
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

5.1
.

For
\ell=1
, both sides of (
97
) are equal to
0
.
Assume in the remaining that
\ell\geq 2
. Notice that the RHS
of (
97
) is a polynomial
Q(g,v_{2},\dots,v_{\ell-1})
, while

Corollary

5.9
says that the LHS
of (
97
) is also a polynomial
P(g,v_{2},\dots,v_{\ell-1})
. The goal is to prove that
P=Q
.

Theorem

5.4
implies that for any admissible pair
(g,\mathbf{v})
with
v_{1}=1
, there exists a
sequence of characters
(\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}})_{d\geq 1}
such that the corresponding
limit shape
\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}}
(guaranteed by
Theorem

2.4
)
is equal to the limit shape
\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}
arising from
limits of Jack–Thoma measures. Then for such pairs
(g,\mathbf{v})
,
the transition measures
\mu_{\Lambda_{\infty}},\,\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}

are also the same, hence their moments are equal, which proves that

P(g,v_{2},\cdots,v_{\ell-1})=Q(g,v_{2},\cdots,v_{\ell-1})
in this case
(the fact that the moments of
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
are given by the RHS
of (
97
) is shown in
Theorem

3.7
).
Further,
Definition

4.5
and
Theorem

4.4
show that if

g>0,\,v_{1}=1,\,v_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell-1}{a_{i}^{k}},\ \forall\,k\geq 2
, for some
real numbers
a_{1}>\dots>a_{\ell-1}>0
,
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell-1}a_{i}<1
,
then
(g,\mathbf{v})
is an admissible pair with
v_{1}=1
. But then
Lemma

5.10

implies the desired equality of polynomials
P=Q
, finishing the proof.
∎

The following corollary will be important for proving
Theorem

5.2
.

Corollary 5.11
.

The following formulas hold true:

\displaystyle M^{(\alpha)}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathbf{L}_{0}(\ell)}\prod_{i\geq 1}(-i\gamma)^{|\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\rightarrow}^{i}(\Gamma)|}\cdot\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{\mu(\Gamma)}\,+\,\text{ terms of $\deg_{2}$ at most $(\ell-2)$},

\displaystyle S^{(\alpha)}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathbf{L}_{0}(\ell)}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma)|}\prod_{i\geq 1}(-i\gamma)^{|\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\rightarrow}^{i}(\Gamma)|}\cdot\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{\mu(\Gamma)}\,+\,\text{ terms of $\deg_{2}$ at most $(\ell-2)$}.

Proof.

By applying
Lemma

5.8
to
x=M^{(\alpha)}_{\ell}
, we can conclude
that the first identity would follow from the following equality

\lim_{d\to\infty}{d^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\cdot\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}\left[M^{(\alpha)}_{\ell}\right]}=\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathbf{L}_{0}(\ell)}\prod_{i\geq 1}{(i\cdot g)^{|\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\rightarrow}^{i}(\Gamma)|}\,v_{i}^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\Gamma)|}},

which in turn follows from (
121
)
and
Theorem

5.1
. The second identity can be proven in a similar way as the first one, but additionally we need to use the relations between
moments, Boolean cumulants, and the fundamental functionals of shape
given by
Proposition

3.2
. Indeed, that proposition has the following combinatorial
interpretation: if moments are expressed as sums of weighted Łukasiewicz paths, then
Boolean cumulants are instead sums of weighted Łukasiewicz paths not touching
0
except at their starting and
ending points; similarly, the fundamental functionals of shape are sums of weighted Łukasiewicz paths
with the additional multiplicative weight of
1/n
, where
n+1
is the number of points touching
0
.
In particular, we have

\lim_{d\to\infty}{d^{-\frac{\ell}{2}}\cdot\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}\left[S^{(\alpha)}_{\ell}\right]}=\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathbf{L}_{0}(\ell)}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma)|}\prod_{i\geq 1}{(i\cdot g)^{|\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\rightarrow}^{i}(\Gamma)|}\,v_{i}^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\Gamma)|}},

which finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 15
.

Shortly after finishing this paper, the second author with Ben Dali have
found an exact (not just the top-degree term) formula expressing the
basis
M^{(\alpha)}_{\mu}
as a linear
combination of the normalized Jack characters
\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{\nu}
in terms of ribbon Łukasiewicz paths –
see
[
BDD23
, Theorem 3.6]
.

5.2.2.
Central Limit Theorem

Before proving
Theorem

5.2
, we need the following technical lemma.
For any partition
\mu
, and
1\leq i\leq\ell(\mu)
, we denote the partition
\mu\setminus\{\mu_{i}\}
by
\mu^{-}_{i}
.

Lemma 5.12
.

For any integers
k,l\geq 0
, and partitions
\mu,\nu
, we have

(124)

\lim_{d\to\infty}d^{-\frac{|\mu|+|\nu|+\ell(\mu)+\ell(\nu)+k+l-2}{2}}\kappa^{\chi_{d},\bullet}_{2}(\gamma^{k}\Ch^{{(\alpha)}}_{\mu},\gamma^{l}\Ch^{(\alpha)}_{\nu})=(-g)^{k+l}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell(\mu)}\sum_{j=1}^{\ell(\nu)}{v_{(\mu_{i}|\nu_{j})}v_{\mu^{-}_{i}}v_{\nu^{-}_{j}}}.

Proof.

From (
114
), one has

d^{-\frac{|\mu|+|\nu|+\ell(\mu)+\ell(\nu)+k+l-2}{2}}\kappa^{\chi_{d},\bullet}_{2}(\gamma^{k}\Ch_{\mu},\gamma^{l}\Ch_{\nu})\\
=(-g)^{k+l}d^{\frac{|\mu|+|\nu|-\ell(\mu)-\ell(\nu)+2}{2}}\kappa^{\chi_{d}}_{2}(\mu,\nu)\cdot\left(1+O(d^{-1})\right),

so that (
124
) is equivalent to the following limit

\lim_{d\to\infty}d^{\frac{|\mu|+|\nu|-\ell(\mu)-\ell(\nu)+2}{2}}{\kappa^{\chi_{d}}_{2}(\mu,\nu)}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell(\mu)}\sum_{j=1}^{\ell(\nu)}v_{(\mu_{i}|\nu_{j})}v_{\mu^{-}_{i}}v_{\nu^{-}_{j}}.

The moment-cumulant formula (
70
) implies that

(125)

\chi_{d}(\mu\cdot\nu)=\!\!\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}(\mu\cdot\nu)}\prod_{B\in\pi}\kappa_{|B|}^{\chi_{d}}(B)=\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}(\mu\cdot\nu)\\
|\pi|\geq\ell(\mu)+\ell(\nu)-1\end{subarray}}\!\prod_{B\in\pi}{\kappa_{|B|}^{\chi_{d}}(B)}+o\left(d^{-\frac{|\mu|+|\nu|-\ell(\mu)-\ell(\nu)+2}{2}}\right)

by the assumption (
20
).
We slightly abused the notation: here,
\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}(\mu\cdot\nu)
is the set of set-partitions of

\{\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{\ell(\mu)},\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{\ell(\nu)}\}
,
therefore the quantities
\kappa_{|B|}^{\chi_{d}}(B)
are cumulants of the form

\kappa_{n}^{\chi_{d}}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{n})
,
where each
k_{i}
is equal to some
\mu_{j}
or some
\nu_{j}
. Similarly,

(126)

\chi_{d}(\mu)\cdot\chi_{d}(\nu)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}(\mu)\\
\pi^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}(\nu)\end{subarray}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}B^{\prime}\in\pi\\
\,B^{\prime\prime}\in\pi^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\kappa_{|B^{\prime}|}^{\chi_{d}}(B^{\prime})\kappa_{|B^{\prime\prime}|}^{\chi_{d}}(B^{\prime\prime})\\
=\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}(\mu),\ \pi^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}(\nu)\\
|\pi^{\prime}|+|\pi^{\prime\prime}|\geq\ell(\mu)+\ell(\nu)-1\ \ \end{subarray}}\!\!\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}B^{\prime}\in\pi^{\prime}\\
\,B^{\prime\prime}\in\pi^{\prime\prime}\end{subarray}}\!\!\kappa_{|B^{\prime}|}^{\chi_{d}}(B^{\prime})\kappa_{|B^{\prime\prime}|}^{\chi_{d}}(B^{\prime\prime})+o\left(d^{-\frac{|\mu|+|\nu|-\ell(\mu)-\ell(\nu)+2}{2}}\right).

Note that there is a unique set-partition
\pi\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}(\mu\cdot\nu)

with
|\pi|=\ell(\mu)+\ell(\nu)
, namely
\pi=\{\{\mu_{1}\},\cdots,\{\nu_{\ell(\nu)}\}\}
,
and there are unique set-partitions
\pi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}(\mu)
and
\pi^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}(\nu)

with
|\pi^{\prime}|+|\pi^{\prime\prime}|=\ell(\mu)+\ell(\nu)
, namely
\pi^{\prime}=\{\{\mu_{1}\},\cdots,\{\mu_{\ell(\mu)}\}\}

and
\pi^{\prime\prime}=\{\{\nu_{1}\},\cdots,\{\nu_{\ell(\nu)}\}\}
.
The corresponding terms in (
125
) and (
126
) are equal.
Pairs of set-partitions
(\pi^{\prime},\pi^{\prime\prime})\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}(\mu)\times\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}(\nu)
with

|\pi^{\prime}|+|\pi^{\prime\prime}|=\ell(\mu)+\ell(\nu)-1
are bijectively mapped (by

(\pi^{\prime},\pi^{\prime\prime})\mapsto\pi^{\prime}\cup\pi^{\prime\prime}
) to set-partitions
\pi\in\mathcal{S}\mathcal{P}(\mu\cdot\nu)

with
|\pi|=\ell(\mu)+\ell(\nu)-1
such that the unique block of
\pi
of size
2

contains only parts of
\mu
or only parts of
\nu
.
Consequently, taking the difference of the RHSs of the above equations gives

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}d^{\frac{|\mu|+|\nu|-\ell(\mu)-\ell(\nu)+2}{2}}\kappa^{\chi_{d}}_{2}(\mu,\nu)=

\displaystyle\lim_{d\to\infty}d^{\frac{|\mu|+|\nu|-\ell(\mu)-\ell(\nu)+2}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{\ell(\mu)}\sum_{j=1}^{\ell(\nu)}\kappa_{2}^{\chi_{d}}(\mu_{i},\nu_{j})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}m=1\\
m\neq i\end{subarray}}^{\ell(\mu)}{\!\chi_{d}(\mu_{m})}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}n=1\\
n\neq j\end{subarray}}^{\ell(\nu)}{\!\chi_{d}(\nu_{n})}=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell(\mu)}\sum_{j=1}^{\ell(\nu)}v_{(\mu_{i}|\nu_{j})}v_{\mu^{-}_{i}}v_{\nu^{-}_{j}}

by the assumptions (
22
)
and (
24
). This finishes the proof.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

5.2
.

Let
x,y\in\mathscr{P}
and denote
\kappa^{\times}_{\bullet}(x,y):=x\times y-x\bullet y
.
First observe that

(\ell-1)\cdot\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{d},x^{\ell-2}\rangle\!=\!d^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\left(S_{\ell}(\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d})-S_{\ell}(\Lambda_{\infty})\right)\\
=d^{-\frac{\ell-1}{2}}\left(S_{\ell}^{(\alpha)}(\lambda)\!-\!\lim_{d^{\prime}\to\infty}\left(\frac{d}{d^{\prime}}\right)^{\frac{\ell}{2}}\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d^{\prime}}}\!\left[S_{\ell}^{(\alpha)}\right]\right).

As a result,

(127)

(\ell-1)\cdot\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}[\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{d},\,x^{\ell-2}\rangle]=d^{-\frac{\ell-1}{2}}\left(\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}\!\left[S_{\ell}^{(\alpha)}\right]-\lim_{d^{\prime}\to\infty}\left(\frac{d}{d^{\prime}}\right)^{\frac{\ell}{2}}\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d^{\prime}}}\!\left[S_{\ell}^{(\alpha)}\right]\right),\\
(k-1)(l-1)\cdot\Cov^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}\left(\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{d},x^{k-2}\rangle,\langle\Delta^{(\alpha)}_{d},x^{l-2}\rangle\right)=d^{-\frac{k+l-2}{2}}\kappa^{\chi_{d},\times}_{2}(S_{k}^{(\alpha)},S_{l}^{(\alpha)})\\
=d^{-\frac{k+l-2}{2}}\bigg(\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}\kappa^{\times}_{\bullet}(S_{k}^{(\alpha)},S_{l}^{(\alpha)})+\kappa^{\chi_{d},\bullet}_{2}(S_{k}^{(\alpha)},S_{l}^{(\alpha)})\bigg),

and we want to take the limit of these expressions as
d\to\infty
.

Corollary

5.11
together with
Lemma

5.8
imply that

\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}\!\left[S_{\ell}^{(\alpha)}\right]-\lim_{d^{\prime}\to\infty}\left(\frac{d}{d^{\prime}}\right)^{\frac{\ell}{2}}\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d^{\prime}}}\!\left[S_{\ell}^{(\alpha)}\right]\\
=d^{\frac{\ell-1}{2}}\cdot\!\left(g^{\prime}\frac{\partial}{\partial g}+\sum_{i\geq 2}{v_{i}^{\prime}\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{i}}}\right)\!\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathbf{L}_{0}(\ell)}{\frac{1}{|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma)|}\prod_{i\geq 1}(i\cdot g)^{|\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\rightarrow}^{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\,v_{i}^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}}+o(d^{\frac{\ell-1}{2}}),

and by taking the limit
d\to\infty
in (
127
) we get the desired formula for
\mathbb{E}[\langle\Delta_{\infty},\,x^{\ell-2}\rangle]
.

In order to compute

\lim_{d\to\infty}d^{-\frac{k+l-2}{2}}\kappa_{2}^{\chi_{d},\bullet}{(S_{k}^{(\alpha)},S_{l}^{(\alpha)})}

we express
S_{k}^{(\alpha)},\,S_{l}^{(\alpha)}
as linear combinations of the normalized Jack characters,
by
Corollary

5.11
, and then apply (
124
). The result is:

(128)

\lim_{d\to\infty}d^{-\frac{k+l-2}{2}}\kappa^{\chi_{d},\bullet}_{2}(S_{k}^{(\alpha)},S_{l}^{(\alpha)})=\\
=\sum_{\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})\in\mathbf{L}(k,l)\atop|\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|=0}\sum_{s_{1}\in\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\nearrow}(\Gamma_{1})\atop s_{2}\in\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\nearrow}(\Gamma_{2})}v_{(\deg(s_{1})|\deg(s_{2}))}\cdot\frac{v_{\mu(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})\setminus\{\deg(s_{1}),\,\deg(s_{2})\}}}{|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma_{1})|\cdot|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma_{2})|}\prod_{i\geq 1}(i\cdot g)^{|\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}.

Since item (A) from
Theorem

5.7
holds by assumption then (B) and (C) also hold. In particular, both
\lim_{d\to\infty}{d^{-\frac{k+l-2}{2}}\kappa_{2}^{\chi_{d},\times}(S_{k}^{(\alpha)},S_{l}^{(\alpha)})},\,\lim_{d\to\infty}{d^{-\frac{k+l-2}{2}}\kappa_{2}^{\chi_{d},\bullet}(S_{k}^{(\alpha)},S_{l}^{(\alpha)})}
are finite, and so is their difference
\lim_{d\to\infty}{d^{-\frac{k+l-2}{2}}\cdot\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}\kappa_{\bullet}^{\times}(S_{k}^{(\alpha)},S_{l}^{(\alpha)})}
.
Then
Lemma

5.8
implies that
\deg_{2}\kappa^{\times}_{\bullet}(S_{k}^{(\alpha)},S_{l}^{(\alpha)})=k+l-2
,
and there exists a polynomial
P(x_{1},\dots,x_{k+l-2})
such that

(129)

\lim_{d\to\infty}d^{-\frac{k+l-2}{2}}\cdot\mathbb{E}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}\kappa^{\times}_{\bullet}(S_{k}^{(\alpha)},S_{l}^{(\alpha)})=P(g,v_{2},\dots,v_{k+l-2}).

The equations (
127
), (
128
), (
129
) imply
that the LHS of (
102
) is equal to the sum of the RHS’s of (
128
) and (
129
), in particular it is a polynomial in the variables

g,\,(v_{i})_{i},\,(v_{(i|j)})_{i,j}
that will be completely determined if we find

P(g,v_{2},\dots,v_{k+l-2})
. In order to find this polynomial,
we employ
Theorem

5.4
, which treats the case of the sequence of Jack characters
(\chi_{d;g,\mathbf{v}})_{d\geq 1}
in (
106
), where
(g,\mathbf{v})
is an admissible pair with
v_{1}=1
. For these characters, the cumulants of order
n\geq 2
vanish, so
v_{(i|j)}=0
, for all
i,j
, and therefore the RHS of (
128
) vanishes in this case.
As a result, whenever
(g,\mathbf{v})
is an admissible pair with
v_{1}=1
, it follows that

(130)

P(g,v_{2},\dots,v_{k+l-2})=\sum_{\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})\in\mathbf{L}^{\conn}(k,l)\atop|\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|=1}\frac{v_{\mu(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})}}{|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma_{1})|\cdot|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma_{2})|}\prod_{i\geq 1}(i\cdot g)^{|\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\,i^{\,|\mathbf{P}_{i}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}\\
-\sum_{\vec{\bm{\Gamma}}=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})\in\mathbf{L}(k,l)\atop|\mathbf{P}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|=0}\frac{|\mathbf{S}_{-1}(\Gamma_{1})|\cdot|\mathbf{S}_{-1}(\Gamma_{2})|\cdot v_{\mu(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})}}{|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma_{1})|\cdot|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma_{2})|}\prod_{i\geq 1}(i\cdot g)^{|\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\vec{\bm{\Gamma}})|}.

Let
g>0
and
a_{1}>\dots>a_{k+l-3}>0
be any real numbers with
\sum_{i=1}^{k+l-3}a_{i}<1
. By
Theorem

4.4
, the pair
(g,\mathbf{v})
with
v_{1}:=1
and
v_{m}:=\sum_{i=1}^{k+l-3}a_{i}^{m}
,
\,m\geq 2
, is an admissible pair.
Then for such parameters
g,\,(v_{i})_{i}
, the equality (
130
) holds.
Finally,
Lemma

5.10
implies that this equality actually holds always.
This means that the LHS of (
102
) is equal to the sum of the RHS’s of (
128
) and (
130
), and this sum can be expressed as the RHS of (
102
), see
Remark

13
. The proof is now finished.
∎

6.
Properties of the limit shape in high and low temperature regimes

Theorem

5.1
implies that in the fixed temperature regime (i.e. when
g=0
)
the limit shape
\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}
does not depend on the choice of the value of
\alpha>0
, in
particular, it is the same as in the classical setting when
\alpha=1
. In this section, we study the limit shape
\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}
(equivalently, the associated transition measure
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
)
which arises in the high and low temperature regimes, i.e. when
g>0
and
g<0
, respectively.

6.1.
Uniqueness and duality between measures
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
with

g>0
and
g<0

Let
g,g^{\prime},v_{2},v_{3},\cdots\in\mathbb{R}
. Recall from
Proposition

2.3

that
\mathcal{F}^{\AFP}_{g,g^{\prime},\mathbf{v}}
is
the set of the sequences of Jack characters with the AFP with the parameters
g,g^{\prime},\mathbf{v}
described
in (
10
) and

Definition

2.2
.

Proposition 6.1

(The measure
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
is determined by its moments)
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{F}^{\AFP}_{g,g^{\prime},\mathbf{v}}\neq\emptyset
.
Then, if we set
v_{1}:=1
, there exists a unique probability measure
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
with moments

(131)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}{x^{\ell}\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}(dx)}=\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathbf{L}_{0}(\ell)}\prod_{i\geq 1}(i\cdot g)^{|\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\rightarrow}^{i}(\Gamma)|}\,v_{i}^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\Gamma)|},\quad\forall\,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}.

The probability measure in this proposition is the transition measure of the limit shape
\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}
of
certain ensembles of large random partitions, see
Theorem

5.1
. Note that,
because of
Theorem

5.4
, if
(g,\mathbf{v})
is an admissible pair (in the sense of

Definition

4.5
) with
v_{1}=1
, then the conditions of the proposition are satisfied.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\Gamma\in\mathbf{L}_{0}(\ell)
. Then the following properties
hold true.

(1)

If
\mathbf{S}^{i}(\Gamma)\neq\emptyset
, then
i\leq\ell
,

(2)

\sum_{j\geq 1}{j|\mathbf{S}_{j}(\Gamma)|}=|\mathbf{S}_{\searrow}(\Gamma)|\leq\ell,

(3)

|\mathbf{L}_{0}(\ell)|\leq 4^{\ell}
, for all large enough

\ell
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbf{L}_{0}(\ell)\subset\mathbf{L}(\ell)
,
property (1) is a special case of
3.13
.

Note that for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, the quantity
j|\mathbf{S}_{j}(\Gamma)|
can be interpreted as

\sum_{w_{i}:=(i,y_{i})\in\mathbf{S}_{j}(\Gamma)}(y_{i}-y_{i-1})
. Since any Łukasiewicz path
has only down steps of degree
1
and
y_{\ell}=y_{0}=0
, one has

\sum_{j\geq 1}{j|\mathbf{S}_{j}(\Gamma)|}-|\mathbf{S}_{\searrow}(\Gamma)|=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}(y_{i}-y_{i-1})=y_{\ell}-y_{0}=0,

which proves property (2).

Finally, Łukasiewicz paths of length
\ell
(with the classical
convention that we allow horizontal steps at height
0
) are in
bijection with non-crossing partitions of
[1\,..\,\ell]
, whose number is famously given
by the Catalan number
C_{\ell}=\frac{1}{\ell+1}{2\ell\choose\ell}\sim\frac{4^{\ell}}{\ell^{3/2}\sqrt{\pi}}
, as
\ell\to\infty
. This proves (3).
∎

Proof of
Proposition

6.1
.

The existence follows from
Theorem

2.4
and
Theorem

5.1
.
For the uniqueness, we will check Carleman’s condition.
In other words, let
M_{\ell}
be the
\ell
-th moment
of
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
given by the RHS of (
131
);
we verify that the sum
\sum_{k\geq 1}{(M_{2k})^{-1/(2k)}}
diverges.

Let us find an upper bound for
M_{2k}
.
Take any
\Gamma\in\mathbf{L}_{0}(2k)
and upper bound the absolute values of the
summand in the RHS of (
131
) that corresponds to
\Gamma
. From

Lemma

6.2
(1),

\prod_{i\geq 1}{|i\cdot g|^{|\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\rightarrow}^{i}(\Gamma)|}}=\prod_{i=1}^{2k}{|i\cdot g|^{|\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\rightarrow}^{i}(\Gamma)|}}\leq\prod_{i=1}^{2k}{(2k|g|)^{|\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\rightarrow}^{i}(\Gamma)|}}=(2k|g|)^{|\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\Gamma)|}.

On the other hand, our assumption (
21
) implies that
there exists a constant
C>0
such that
|v_{i}|\leq(i\cdot C)^{i}
, for all
i\geq 1
.
Then from
Lemma

6.2
(2),

\prod_{i\geq 1}{|v_{i}|^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\Gamma)|}}\leq\prod_{i\geq 1}{(i\cdot C)^{i|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\Gamma)|}}\leq(2kC)^{\,\sum_{i\geq 1}{i|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\Gamma)|}}=(2kC)^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{\searrow}(\Gamma)|}.

Since
|\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\Gamma)|+|\mathbf{S}_{\searrow}(\Gamma)|\leq|\mathbf{S}(\Gamma)|=2k
,
these inequalities imply that

0\leq\left|\prod_{i\geq 1}{(i\cdot g)^{|\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\rightarrow}^{i}(\Gamma)|}\,v_{i}^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\Gamma)|}}\right|\leq(Dk)^{2k},

for some constant
D>0
that is independent of
k
and of the Łukasiewicz path
\Gamma\in\mathbf{L}_{0}(2k)
. Then by
Lemma

6.2
(3), the
(2k)
-th moment of
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
is bounded as follows (for large
k
):

0\leq M_{2k}\leq(Dk)^{2k}\cdot|\mathbf{L}_{0}(2k)|\leq(Dk)^{2k}\cdot 4^{2k}.

The previous inequality shows that for large
k
,
we have
(M_{2k})^{-1/(2k)}\geq\frac{1}{4Dk}
.
Since
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\frac{1}{k}}
diverges, so does the sum

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{(M_{2k})^{-1/(2k)}}
.
This proves Carleman’s condition and completes the proof.
∎

Let us complete here one of the steps in the proof of
Theorem

3.7
that was left out in
Section

3.8.3
. There we proved the existence of a probability measure
P_{\infty}
with moments

(132)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}{x^{\ell}P_{\infty}(dx)}=\frac{v_{1}^{-\frac{\ell+2}{2}}}{\ell+1}\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathbf{L}_{0}(\ell+2)}{\frac{1}{|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma)|}\prod_{i\geq 1}(i\cdot g)^{|\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\Gamma)|}\,v_{i}^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\Gamma)|}},\quad\forall\,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

As a reminder,
P_{\infty}
was relevant to us because we proved that it has density
\frac{\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}(x)-|x|}{2}
, where
\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}
is the limit shape of random Young diagrams obtained in
Theorem

3.7
.

Corollary 6.3
.

Let
g,v_{1},v_{2},\cdots\in\mathbb{R}
be parameters for which there exist sequences of positive reals

\alpha(d),\,u(d)>0
such that the weak version of
3.6
is satisfied.
We showed in
Section

3.8
that there exists a probability measure
P_{\infty}
with moments given by (
132
).
Then the probability measure
P_{\infty}
is uniquely determined by its moments.

Proof.

Theorem

4.2
implies that for each
d
, there
exist sequences
a_{1}(d)\geq a_{2}(d)\geq\cdots\geq 0
,
b_{1}(d)\geq b_{2}(d)\geq\cdots\geq 0
such that
u(d)\cdot v_{1}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}(a_{i}(d)+b_{i}(d))
and
u(d)\cdot v_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}a_{i}(d)^{k}+(-\alpha(d))^{1-k}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}b_{i}(d)^{k}
, for all
k\geq 2
.
One can easily check that this implies that the sequence
(v_{k})_{k\geq 1}
satisfies the assumption (
21
) from
Definition

2.2
. This allows us to use the same estimates from the proof of
Proposition

6.1
to prove Carleman’s condition.

For all
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, let
|m_{\ell}|:=\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathbf{L}_{0}(\ell)}\left|\prod_{i\geq 1}(i\cdot g)^{|\mathbf{S}^{i}_{\tiny\rightarrow}(\Gamma)|}\,v_{i}^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\Gamma)|}\right|
, and let
M_{\ell}^{\infty}
be the
\ell
-th moment of
P_{\infty}
. Since
|\mathbf{S}^{0}(\Gamma)|\geq 1
, then
|M_{\ell}^{\infty}|\leq|v_{1}|^{-\frac{\ell+2}{2}}\cdot|m_{\ell+2}|
, and in particular,

(M_{2k}^{\infty})^{-1/(2k)}\geq C_{1}\cdot\left(|m_{2k+2}|^{-1/(2k+2)}\right)^{(k+1)/k},

for some constant
C_{1}>0
and all
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.
In the proof of
Proposition

6.1
, it was shown that
(m_{2k+2})^{-1/(2k+2)}\geq\frac{C_{2}}{k}
, for some constant
C_{2}>0
and all
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
.
Putting these two inequalities together gives the existence of some constant
C>0
such that
(M_{2k}^{\infty})^{-1/(2k)}\geq\frac{C}{k}
, for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. Since
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\frac{1}{k}}
diverges, so does
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{(M_{2k}^{\infty})^{-1/(2k)}}
. This proves Carleman’s condition and finishes the proof.
∎

For the next proposition, recall that we use the notation
\pm\mathbf{v}:=(v_{1},-v_{2},v_{3},-v_{4},\dots)
.

Proposition 6.4

(Duality between
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
and
\mu_{-g;\,\pm\mathbf{v}}
)
.

Let
\left(\chi_{d}^{(\alpha)}\right)_{d\geq 1}\in\mathcal{F}^{\AFP}_{g,g^{\prime},\mathbf{v}}
, and
let
\left(\tilde{\chi}_{d}^{(\tilde{\alpha})}\right)_{d\geq 1}\in\mathcal{F}^{\AFP}_{-g,-g^{\prime},\pm\mathbf{v}}
be the associated sequences from

Proposition

2.3
. Then

\omega_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}(-x)=\omega_{\Lambda^{(\tilde{\alpha})}_{d}}(x)

as random functions. In particular

\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}(x)=\omega_{\Lambda_{-g;\pm\mathbf{v}}}(-x),\text{ and }\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}=\mu_{-g;\,\pm\mathbf{v}}.

Proof.

For any partition
\lambda
of
d
, we have

(133)

\omega_{T_{\sqrt{\frac{\alpha}{d}},\sqrt{\frac{1}{\alpha d}}}\lambda}(-x)=\omega_{T_{\sqrt{\frac{1}{\alpha d}},\sqrt{\frac{\alpha}{d}}}\lambda^{\prime}}(x)=\omega_{T_{\sqrt{\frac{\tilde{\alpha}}{d}},\sqrt{\frac{1}{\tilde{\alpha}d}}}\lambda^{\prime}}(x).

Proposition

2.3
states that
\mathbb{P}^{(\tilde{\alpha})}_{\tilde{\chi}_{d}}(\lambda)=\mathbb{P}^{{(\alpha)}}_{\chi_{d}}(\lambda^{\prime})
, which together with (
133
) implies
that
\omega_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}(-x)
and
\omega_{\Lambda^{(\tilde{\alpha})}_{d}}(x)
have the same distribution.
Passing to the limit
d\to\infty
,

Theorem

5.1
implies that
\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}(x)=\omega_{\Lambda_{-g;\pm\mathbf{v}}}(-x)
.
Finally,
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}=\mu_{-g;\,\pm\mathbf{v}}
follows from the Markov–Krein correspondence.
∎

6.2.
The limit shape
\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}
is an infinite staircase

For the remainder of this section, assume that
(g,\mathbf{v})
is an
admissible pair with
v_{1}=1
, and
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
is the probability
measure determined by
Proposition

6.1
.

Note that if
(g,\mathbf{v})
is an admissible pair, then so is

(-g,\pm\mathbf{v})
. In particular, this means that both probability measures

\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
and
\mu_{-g;\,\pm\mathbf{v}}
exist and are uniquely determined
by their moments.

Proposition

2.3
and
Proposition

6.4
imply that the cases
g>0
and
g<0
are dual to
each other. Thus, in the proofs below we will stick to only one of these two cases, either
g>0
or
g<0
, if the other one follows by duality.
We would like to completely describe the limit shape

\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}
for
g\neq 0
.
Qualitatively, this shape is described by the following result.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
(\chi_{d})_{d\geq 1}
be the Jack characters described in
Theorem

5.4
, let
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}
be the corresponding probability measures, and let
\lambda^{(d)}\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}\,
be
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}
-distributed random partitions.

If
g>0
, then the following limits exist in probability

(134)

\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime}_{i}:=g^{-1}\cdot\lim\frac{(\lambda^{(d)})^{\prime}_{i}}{d},

for all
i=1,2,\dots
, and

(135)

\omega_{\Lambda_{g,\mathbf{v}}}(x)=\begin{cases}-x,&\text{ if }x<-\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime}_{1},\\
2\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime}_{i}+x,&\text{ if }x\in\big[-\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime}_{i}+(i-1)g,\ -\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime}_{i}+ig\big],\text{ for some }i\geq 1,\\
-x+2ig,&\text{ if }x\in\big[-\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime}_{i}+ig,\ -\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime}_{i+1}+ig\big],\text{ for some }i\geq 1.\end{cases}

Likewise, if
g<0
, then the following limits exist in probability

(136)

\tilde{\lambda}_{i}:=-g^{-1}\cdot\lim\frac{\lambda^{(d)}_{i}}{d},

for all
i=1,2,\dots
, and

(137)

\omega_{\Lambda_{g,\mathbf{v}}}(x)=\begin{cases}x,&\text{ if }x>\tilde{\lambda}_{1},\\
2\tilde{\lambda}_{i}-x,&\text{ if }x\in\big[\tilde{\lambda}_{i}+ig,\ \tilde{\lambda}_{i}+(i-1)g\big],\textrm{ for some }i\geq 1,\\
x-2ig,&\text{ if }x\in\big[\tilde{\lambda}_{i+1}+ig,\ \tilde{\lambda}_{i}+ig\big],\textrm{ for some }i\geq 1.\end{cases}

Proof.

Assume that
g>0
. Recall that, in the notation (
27
), we have the rescaled partition
\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}=T_{\sqrt{\frac{\alpha}{d}},\,\sqrt{\frac{1}{\alpha d}}}\,\lambda^{(d)}
.
Since the parameters
\alpha,\,d,\,g
are related by (
107
), we have
\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}=T_{g,\,\frac{1}{gd}}\left(\lambda^{(d)}\right)
.
This means that
\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}
is a random diagram, which is obtained (in the French notation) by stacking rectangles of sizes
g\times\frac{(\lambda^{(d)})^{\prime}_{1}}{gd},g\times\frac{(\lambda^{(d)})^{\prime}_{2}}{gd},\cdots
next to each other from
left to right.
Equivalently, the profile of
\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}
is equal to

\omega_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}(x)=\begin{cases}-x,&\text{ if }x<-\frac{(\lambda^{(d)})^{\prime}_{1}}{gd},\\
2\frac{(\lambda^{(d)})^{\prime}_{i}}{gd}+x,&\text{ if }x\in\big[-\frac{(\lambda^{(d)})^{\prime}_{i}}{gd}+(i-1)g,\ -\frac{(\lambda^{(d)})^{\prime}_{i}}{gd}+ig\big],\text{ for some }i\geq 1,\\
-x+2ig,&\text{ if }x\in\big[-\frac{(\lambda^{(d)})^{\prime}_{i}}{gd}+ig,\ -\frac{(\lambda^{(d)})^{\prime}_{i+1}}{gd}+ig\big],\text{ for some }i\geq 1.\end{cases}

Due to the existence of a limit shape
\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}}
of the
profiles
\omega_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}
in the supremum norm in probability
(
Theorem

2.4
) and the fact that
g>0
is deterministic, it
necessarily follows that the limits in probability

\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime}_{i}:=g^{-1}\cdot\lim\frac{(\lambda^{(d)})^{\prime}_{i}}{d}

exist, for all
i=1,2,\cdots
.
Moreover, the limit
\Lambda_{\infty}
of the rescaled Young diagrams is obtained (in the French notation) by putting rectangles of sizes
g\times\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime}_{1},g\times\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime}_{2},\dots
next to each other from left to right.
Equivalently, the limit profile
\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}}
is given by the RHS of (
135
)
11
11

11

See
Fig.

2
for an illustration of the rescaled Young diagram
\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}
and the limit shape
\Lambda_{\infty}
when
g<0
.
.
By
Theorem

5.4
, the limit profile
\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}}
is equal to
\omega_{\Lambda_{g,\mathbf{v}}}
, therefore
Eq.

135
follows.
∎

As a result of
Proposition

6.5
, the limit profile
\omega_{\Lambda_{g,\mathbf{v}}}
is a piecewise linear curve that only has
slopes
\pm 1
(it is a
staircase shape
). Moreover, assuming
that
\tilde{\lambda}_{1}^{\prime}>\tilde{\lambda}_{2}^{\prime}>\dotsm
when
g>0
(resp.
\tilde{\lambda}_{1}>\tilde{\lambda}_{2}>\dotsm
when
g<0
) are all distinct, then
\omega_{\Lambda_{g,\mathbf{v}}}

is entirely determined by the locations of its local minima.
The interplay between the local minima of
\omega_{\Lambda_{g,\mathbf{v}}}
and the support of the transition measure
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
in the next result will play a critical role.

Proposition 6.6
.

The support of
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
is discrete, and it coincides with the set of local minima of
\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}(x)
.
Moreover, if
g>0
, then
\supp(\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}})\subseteq[-g^{-1},\,+\infty)
, whereas if
g<0
, then
\supp(\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}})\subseteq(-\infty,\,-g^{-1}]
.
Finally, the distance between two consecutive points of
\supp\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
is at least
|g|
.

Proof.

Assume that
g>0
.
By
Proposition

6.5
, the limit shape
\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}
only has slopes
\pm 1
, thus it is uniquely determined by its local minima
x_{i}
and local maxima
y_{i}
that form an interlacing sequence of the form
x_{1}<y_{1}<x_{2}<y_{2}<\cdots
(which could be finite or countably infinite).
As a result,
Eq.

44
implies that
\supp(\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}})=\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots\}
is discrete.

Moreover, by (
135
), the smallest local minimum is
x_{1}=-\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime}_{1}
.
Since
(\lambda^{(d)})^{\prime}_{1}\leq\big|\lambda_{1}^{(d)}\big|=d
, it follows that

x_{1}=-\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime}_{1}=-g^{-1}\cdot\lim\frac{(\lambda^{(d)})^{\prime}_{i}}{d}\geq-g^{-1}\cdot 1=-g^{-1}.

Finally, by (
135
), two consecutive local minima must be of the form
-\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime}_{i}+(i-1)g
and
-\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime}_{j}+(j-1)g
, for some
j>i
.
Since
(\lambda^{(d)})^{\prime}_{i}\geq(\lambda^{(d)})^{\prime}_{j}
, whenever
j>i
, the distance between these consecutive local minima is

(-\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime}_{j}+(j-1)g)-(-\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime}_{i}+(i-1)g)=(j-i)g+g^{-1}\cdot\lim\frac{(\lambda^{(d)})^{\prime}_{i}-(\lambda^{(d)})^{\prime}_{j}}{d}\geq g,

which ends the proof.
∎

It is not clear from
Proposition

6.5
whether the staircase shape
\omega_{\Lambda_{g,\mathbf{v}}}
has infinitely many local minima or only finitely many.
In fact,
\omega_{\Lambda_{g,\mathbf{v}}}
will have only finitely many local minima if some
\tilde{\lambda}_{i}^{\prime}
(resp.
\tilde{\lambda}_{i}
), as defined by (
134
) when
g>0
(resp. by (
136
) when
g<0
), is equal to zero;
the next theorem shows that this never happens.

Theorem 6.7
.

The support of
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
is countably infinite. Moreover, if
\pm g>0
, then the unique accumulation point of the support of
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
is
\pm\infty
.
Consequently, the limit shape
\omega_{\Lambda_{g,\mathbf{v}}}
is a semi-infinite staircase shape with only slopes
\pm 1
that extends to
+\infty
, if
g>0
, and extends to
-\infty
, if
g<0
.

Proof.

The statement about
\omega_{\Lambda_{g,\mathbf{v}}}
follows from ones about
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
, together with
Propositions

6.5
and
6.6
.
Thus it remains to prove the statements about
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
.
Assume that
g>0
. By
Proposition

6.6
, the measure
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
is discrete and has
at most one accumulation point, namely
+\infty
.
If we proved that
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
does not have compact support, the theorem would follow.
Thus, for the sake of contradiction, assume that the support of
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
is compact,
and therefore finite. In particular, there exist
C,\epsilon>0
such
that the moments
M_{\ell}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}{x^{\ell}\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}(dx)}
of

\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
are bounded as follows:

(138)

|M_{\ell}|\leq C\epsilon^{-\ell},\ \text{ for all }\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}.

On the other hand, recall that the moments
M_{\ell}
are given by
(
131
). For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, consider the following Łukasiewicz path
\Gamma_{0}\in\mathbf{L}_{0}(3k)
:

•

w_{0}=(0,0),\,w_{1}=(1,1),\cdots,w_{k}=(k,k)
(
k
up steps of degree
1
).

•

w_{k+1}=(k+1,k),\,w_{k+2}=(k+2,k),\cdots,w_{2k}=(2k,k)
(
k
horizontal steps).

•

w_{2k+1}=(2k+1,k-1),\,w_{2k+2}=(2k+2,k-2),\cdots,w_{3k}=(3k,0)
(
k
down steps).

The term corresponding to
\Gamma_{0}
in the sum (
131
) is equal to

(kg)^{k}\cdot v_{1}^{k}=(kg)^{k}
.
Next, since
\mathbf{v}
determines a totally Jack-positive specialization,
Theorem

4.4
implies that
v_{i}\geq 0
, for all
i
, therefore all terms in the sum (
131
) are nonnegative and so

M_{3k}\geq(kg)^{k}
. But according to (
138
),
M_{3k}\leq C\epsilon^{-3k}
.
As a result,
C\geq(kg\cdot\epsilon^{3})^{k}
, for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
,
which is impossible. Hence,
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
does not have compact support.
∎

We can now derive precise information about the
edge asymptotics
(longest rows or columns) of random partitions, starting from the knowledge we have about
\supp(\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}})
.
Denote

\max\supp(\mu):=\sup\{x\mid x\in\supp(\mu)\},\quad\min\supp(\mu):=\inf\{x\mid x\in\supp(\mu)\},

for any measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}
.

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
\left(\chi_{d}\right)_{d\geq 1}\in\mathcal{F}^{\AFP}_{g,g^{\prime},\mathbf{v}}
, let
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}
be the corresponding probability measures, and let
\lambda^{(d)}\in\mathbb{Y}_{d}\,
be
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}
-distributed random partitions.
Further, set
v_{1}:=1
, and let
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
be
the transition measure of the corresponding limit shape

\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}}
(whose existence is given in
Theorem

2.4
).
Equivalently, since
\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}}=\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}
(due to
Theorem

5.1
),
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
is
the probability measure on
\mathbb{R}
determined by
Proposition

6.1
.

If
g>0
, then we have

\frac{(\lambda^{(d)})^{\prime}_{1}}{-g\cdot d}\to\min\supp(\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}),

in probability.
If
g<0
, then we have

\frac{\lambda^{(d)}_{1}}{-g\cdot d}\to\max\supp(\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}),

in probability.

Proof.

Assume that
g<0
and set
M:=\max\supp(\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}})
.
According to
Proposition

6.6
,
M
is the largest local minimum of
\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}
; in the notation of
Proposition

6.5
and
Eq.

137
, this means
M=\tilde{\lambda}_{1}
, and therefore
\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}(x)=x
, for
x>M
, and
\omega_{\Lambda_{g;\mathbf{v}}}(x)=2M-x
, for
x\in[M+g,M]
.
A simple geometric argument thus implies that, if
\lambda^{(d)}_{1}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}}{\sqrt{d}}\geq M
, then

(139)

\left|\omega_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}(M)-\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}}(M)\right|\geq 2\cdot\min\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha d}},\ \left|\lambda^{(d)}_{1}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}}{\sqrt{d}}-M\right|\right\}.

Similarly, if
\lambda^{(d)}_{1}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}}{\sqrt{d}}\leq M
, then

(140)

\left|\omega_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}\left(\lambda^{(d)}_{1}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}}{\sqrt{d}}\right)-\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}}\left(\lambda^{(d)}_{1}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}}{\sqrt{d}}\right)\right|\geq 2\cdot\min\left\{-g,\ \left|\lambda^{(d)}_{1}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}}{\sqrt{d}}-M\right|\right\}.

Recall that in our regime of interest we have
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha d}}\sim-g
.
Take any small
-g>\epsilon>0
, and assume that
\epsilon\geq\|\omega_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}-\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}}\|_{\infty}
and
\epsilon/2>|\sqrt{\alpha d}+g^{-1}|
.
Then the previous inequalities (
139
)-(
140
) imply

\frac{\epsilon}{2}\geq\left|\lambda^{(d)}_{1}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}}{\sqrt{d}}-M\right|.

By our choice of
\epsilon>0
, it follows that

\epsilon\geq\left|\frac{\lambda^{(d)}_{1}}{-g\cdot d}-M\right|.

Hence,

\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(\|\omega_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}-\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}}\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon)\leq\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}\left(\left|\frac{\lambda^{(d)}_{1}}{-g\cdot d}-M\right|\leq\epsilon\right),

for all large
d
.

Theorem

2.4
shows
\lim_{d\to\infty}\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}(\|\omega_{\Lambda^{(\alpha)}_{d}}-\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}}\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon)=1
, therefore

\lim_{d\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}\left(\left|\frac{\lambda^{(d)}_{1}}{-g\cdot d}-M\right|\leq\epsilon\right)}=1,

which is the desired limit in probability.
∎

Remark 16
.

For concreteness, let us consider only the case
g>0
in this remark.
We cannot tie the quantity
\frac{(\lambda^{(d)})^{\prime}_{i}}{-g\cdot d}
to the
i
-th smallest point in the support of
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
(equivalently, the
i
-th local minima of
\omega_{\Lambda_{g,\mathbf{v}}}
) , unless
i=1
, because it is not clear from
Eq.

135
what is the
i
-th smallest local minimum of
\omega_{\Lambda_{g,\mathbf{v}}}
.
The problem occurs when, in the notation of
Proposition

6.5
,
\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime}_{j}=\tilde{\lambda}^{\prime}_{j+1}
, for some
j
’s.
This is why the general
Proposition

6.8
only deals with the longest columns
(\lambda^{(d)})^{\prime}_{1}
of the random partitions.
In the next section, we will study one case where this analysis can be extended to several of the longest columns
(\lambda^{(d)})^{\prime}_{1},(\lambda^{(d)})^{\prime}_{2},\cdots
(or several of the longest rows, if
g<0
).

In the next section, we consider the special case when
\mathbb{P}^{(\alpha)}_{\chi_{d}}
are the Jack–Plancherel measures and we find not only the limits in probability of the longest row/column of the random partition in terms of
\supp(\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}})
, but we will compute exactly the limits of any finite number of longest rows/columns, and therefore we will entirely determine the limit shape
\omega_{\Lambda_{\infty}}
in this case.

6.3.
Edge asymptotics of Jack–Plancherel-distributed random partitions

Consider the
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
matrix
J_{g;\mathbf{v}}
defined by
(J_{g;\mathbf{v}})_{i,j}:=ig\cdot\delta_{i,j}+v_{j-i}
, for all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, where
v_{-1}:=1
and
v_{0}=v_{-l}:=0
, for all
l\geq 2
. Also consider the special case when
v_{1}=1,\,v_{i}=0
, for all
i\geq 2
, to be denoted by
J^{\text{Planc}}_{g}:=J_{g;(1,0,0,\cdots)}
, i.e.

J_{g;\mathbf{v}}:=\begin{bmatrix}0&v_{1}&v_{2}&v_{3}&\cdots\\
1&g&v_{1}&v_{2}&\ddots\\
0&1&2g&v_{1}&\ddots\\
0&0&1&3g&\ddots\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots\end{bmatrix},\qquad J^{\text{Planc}}_{g}:=\begin{bmatrix}0&1&0&0&\cdots\\
1&g&1&0&\ddots\\
0&1&2g&1&\ddots\\
0&0&1&3g&\ddots\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots\end{bmatrix}.

Proposition 6.9
.

The
(0,0)
-entries of the powers of
J_{g;\mathbf{v}}
are equal to the moments of the measure
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
:

(141)

(J_{g;\mathbf{v}})^{\ell}_{0,0}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}{x^{\ell}d\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}(x)},\ \text{ for all }\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

Proof.

Denote
J:=J_{g;\mathbf{v}}
and take any
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Then

(J^{\ell})_{0,0}=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{\ell-1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}J_{0,i_{1}}J_{i_{1},i_{2}}\cdots J_{i_{\ell-1},0}.

The sum is over sequences
(i_{1},\dots,i_{\ell-1})\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{\ell-1}
with
i_{k+1}-i_{k}\geq-1
, for all
k
, since otherwise the term vanishes.
To any such sequence
(i_{1},\dots,i_{\ell-1})
, associate the excursion
\Gamma=(w_{0},w_{1},\cdots,w_{\ell})
, defined by
w_{k}=(k,y_{k}),\,y_{0}=y_{\ell}=0
, and
y_{k}=i_{k}
, for
1\leq k\leq\ell-1
. The condition
i_{k+1}-i_{k}\geq-1
implies that
\Gamma
is a Łukasiewicz path of length
\ell
. Moreover, the term
J_{0,i_{1}}J_{i_{1},i_{2}}\cdots J_{i_{\ell-1},0}
contains a factor
v_{i}
for every up step of degree
i
, and a factor
i\cdot g
for every horizontal step at height
i
, so the term equals
\prod_{i\geq 1}{(i\cdot g)^{|\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\rightarrow}^{i}(\Gamma)|}\,v_{i}^{\,|\mathbf{S}_{i}(\Gamma)|}}
. Hence,
(J^{\ell})_{0,0}
is the sum in the RHS of (
131
), which also equals the
\ell
-th moment of
\mu_{g;\mathbf{v}}
.
∎

In the special case
\mathbf{v}=(1,0,0,\cdots)
, the resulting
J^{\text{Planc}}_{g}
is a
Jacobi matrix
, i.e. a real symmetric, tridiagonal matrix whose off-diagonal entries are positive. The (unique) probability measure
\mu_{g}^{\text{Planch}}:=\mu_{g;(1,0,0,\cdots)}
satisfying (
141
) is said to be the
spectral measure of
J^{\text{Planc}}_{g}
.
Note also that the moment formula (
131
) simplifies because the terms coming from Łukasiewicz paths with up steps of degree
\geq 2
vanish. Let
\mathbf{M}_{0}(\ell)\subseteq\mathbf{L}_{0}(\ell)
be the subset of Łukasiewicz paths whose up steps (and down steps) all have degree
1
: this is the set of
Motzkin paths of length
\ell
, see
Fig.

7
.
12
12

12

Our Motzkin paths do not have horizontal steps at height
0
, though this is usually allowed in the literature.

Figure 7
.
All three Motzkin paths in
\mathbf{M}_{0}(4)
.

If
g=0
, then
J^{\text{Planc}}_{g=0}
is a Toeplitz matrix with spectral measure
\mu_{g=0}^{\text{Planch}}
being the semicircle distribution. When
g\neq 0
, our Propositions
6.1
,
6.9
and Theorem
6.7
imply the following.

Corollary 6.10
.

If
\pm g>0
, the spectral measure
\mu_{g}^{\text{Planch}}
of
J^{\text{Planc}}_{g}
has discrete countable support
\supp(\mu^{\text{Planc}}_{g})
with unique accumulation point
\pm\infty
. Moreover,
\mu_{g}^{\text{Planch}}
is the unique probability measure with moments:

\int_{\mathbb{R}}{x^{\ell}\mu^{\text{Planc}}_{g}(dx)}=\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathbf{M}_{0}(\ell)}\prod_{i\geq 1}(i\cdot g)^{|\mathbf{S}_{\tiny\rightarrow}^{i}(\Gamma)|},\ \text{ for all }\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}.

Let

J_{\nu}(x)=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{m}}{m!\Gamma(m+\nu+1)}\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2m+\nu}

be the Bessel function of the first kind, where
\Gamma(z)
is the
Gamma function. It turned out that the operator
J^{\text{Planc}}_{g}
was studied in the past, and the following relation between the zeroes of
J_{\nu}(x)
, as a function of the label parameter
\nu
, and the spectrum of
\mu_{g}^{\text{Planch}}
is well-known (see
[
GZ73
,
IKF91
,
ŠŠ15
]
).

Theorem 6.11
.

Let
g>0
, and let
